<meta>This page serves as the standard introductory disclaimer and usage guide for a digitized volume from the Google Books project. It establishes the legal and functional context for the digital manuscript that follows.</meta>

<image-desc>The logo for "Google" is displayed at the top left in its traditional multi-colored serif typeface: a blue 'G', red 'o', yellow 'o', blue 'g', green 'l', and red 'e', followed by a small trademark symbol.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain <note>The "public domain" refers to creative works to which no exclusive intellectual property rights apply, allowing the public to use them freely.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to marks or comments written in the margins of a book.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text.</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

## About this Book

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved on library shelves for generations before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project intended to make the world's books available online.

The book has outlived its copyright and can now be made available to the public. A public domain book is a book that was never subject to copyright or for which the copyright protection period has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateway to the past and represent a historical, cultural, and scientific wealth that is often difficult to discover.

Traces of use, annotations, and other marginalia contained in the original volume are also found in this file—a reminder of the long journey the book has taken from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage Guidelines

Google is proud to work in partnership with libraries to digitize public domain material and make it accessible to a broad audience. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is costly. To continue providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties. This includes technical restrictions on automated queries.

We ask that you adhere to the following guidelines:

+ *Use of the files for non-commercial purposes* We designed Google Book Search for end users and want you to use these files only for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *No automated queries* Do not send automated queries of any kind to the Google system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR</note>, or other areas where access to text in large quantities is useful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain material for these purposes and may be able to help you.

+ *Retention of Google branding elements* The Google "watermark" that you find in every file is important for providing information about this project and helps users find further material via Google Book Search. Please do not remove the watermark.

+ *Stay within the law* Regardless of your intended use, you must be aware of your responsibility to ensure that your use is legal. Do not assume that a book that we believe is in the public domain for users in the USA is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still subject to copyright varies from country to country. We cannot provide advice on whether a specific use of a specific book is legally permitted. Do not assume that the appearance of a book in Google Book Search means it can be used in any form and anywhere in the world. A copyright violation can have serious consequences.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s goal is to organize the world’s information and make it universally useful and accessible. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books and supports authors and publishers in reaching new audiences. You can search the entire text of the book on the Internet at <insert>http://books.google.com</insert>.

--- Page 11 ---

# Preface.

---

The following pages form a continuation of my earlier works regarding the philosophical school of the Arabs, who, in the second half of the 10th century AD, strove to encompass the entire field of knowledge—as it was then mastered by the Arabs—in an encyclopedia arranged by subject matter and containing 51 treatises. In their struggle for science against an orthodoxy that suppressed all research and morality, they had joined together into a well-structured order. This group, known by the name of the *Ikhwan al-Safa* <note>original Arabic: "ihwān aṣ-ṣafā"</note> or the "Brethren of Purity or the Sincere Brethren," was based in Basra. From there, it spread through all parts of the vast Arabian empire and found a fertile field for its scientific and moral endeavors, especially in Spain, the true center of culture in the Middle Ages. I have written three works about this order: first, by translating the fable "Man and Beast," I described the general spiritual direction of this school and listed the individual treatises in an attached excursus; then, I treated their natural science and view of nature in a specific book; and in 1865, their propaedeutics <term>propaedeutics: preliminary instructions or introductory studies that prepare a student for more advanced learning</term>, that is—

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces his ongoing research into the Ikhwan al-Safa (Brethren of Purity), a 10th-century secret philosophical society in Basra that produced a 51-volume encyclopedia of knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Ikhwan al-Safa, Brethren of Purity, Basra, Medieval Islamic Philosophy, Encyclopedia, History of Science, Propaedeutics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page V, detailing the publication of the introductory mathematical works of the Ikhwan al-Safa and transitioning into their logical and psychological treatises.</meta>

published the geometric and mathematical introductory school <term>introductory school: translating the German "Vorschule," often referring to a preparatory course or propaedeutic</term> in order to show how this school, in a Neo-Pythagorean manner, viewed number and measure as the framework already present in the spirit, upon which they attempted to build the structure of science.\*) To the kind reader who has followed me in these studies, I now present in this book a new series of treatises by these philosophers, which concern logic and psychology. These include, in the order of the 51 treatises, the 7th through the 13th, namely: 7. Theoretical Science. 8. Practical Science. 9. Doctrine of Character (Psychology). 10. Introduction to Logic. 11. Categories. 12. Hermeneutics and Prior Analytics. 13. Posterior Analytics. —

If we place Treatise VII first as the general discourse concerning the division of the sciences, and then set aside VIII and IX as not directly belonging to the sequence, we have in 10–13 the complete course of Aristotelian logic: the *Organon* <note>The "Organon" (Instrument) is the traditional name for Aristotle’s collected works on logic.</note> along with the introduction composed by Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century scholar whose "Isagoge" became the standard introduction to Aristotle.</note>, which is usually printed before these writings of Aristotle. As is well known, these books have served as the support and guide for all studies of logic.

Since the far greater part of the material (Treatises 1–21) is now available for general assessment across the four works I have written on the philosophy of this school, I would like to permit myself to summarize the most essential results in brief strokes.

1. At the time of the Sincere Brethren <note>original: "Lautere Brüder," translating the Arabic "Ikhwān al-Ṣafā"</note> in the second half of the 10th century CE, the treasures of Greek philosophy had long since become the common property of scholars, who used them for their proofs concerning the nature of God—

---

\*) Cf. *Conflict between Man and Beast*. Berlin 1858. *Natural Science and the View of Nature of the Arabs*. Berlin 1861. *Propaedeutics of the Arabs*. Berlin 1865.

<summary>The author outlines the contents of the current volume, focusing on the logical and psychological treatises of the Ikhwan al-Safa, which are heavily influenced by the Aristotelian Organon and Porphyry. He begins a summary of the historical context of Greek philosophy's influence on 10th-century Islamic thought.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian logic, Organon, Porphyry, Neo-Pythagoreanism, Ikhwan al-Safa, Islamic Philosophy, 10th Century, Psychology, Classification of Sciences</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page VI, discussing how the school of the Sincere Brethren interpreted and utilized God's revelation.</meta>

and His revelation for and against <note>original: "pro et contra"</note> in an orthodox or liberal manner, interpreting and utilizing it.*)

2. Therefore, this school attempted to disseminate the scientific results directed toward these goals to even wider circles. Their aim was to establish a purer view of God and His essence, no longer regarding God as a cruel tyrant and man as a mere will-less slave, but instead viewing God as an omnipotent-benevolent being and man as a free being.

3. The philosophy that seemed most suitable for this purpose was the so-called eclectic school, which sought to grasp the true essence of all knowledge by uniting the greatest masters, Aristotle and Plato. However, they were unaware of the vast difference between those systems: the Aristotelian system ascends from the multiplicity of perceived things—the world—to their principle and beginning, which is God; while the other, the Platonic or rather Neoplatonic system, derives the multiplicity of perceived things—the world—from an abstract Being, which is God. Thus, it has come to pass that in the <term>propaedeutic: introductory or preparatory studies</term> 1–6, we find the Neoplatonic spiritual direction prevailing; in logic, the Aristotelian; in natural science, again the Aristotelian; and in the doctrine of the world-soul 30–40, once again Neoplatonic elements. The final part of the treatises, the theology 41–51, certainly offers much from the <term>Theologumena: theological principles or writings</term> of Aristotle, though these components are so overgrown with mystical Muslim ideas that they are barely recognizable <note>The "Theology of Aristotle" mentioned here was actually a collection of writings by the Neoplatonist Plotinus that was misattributed to Aristotle in the medieval Islamic world.</note>.

4. The two children, whom this philosophy still sheltered side-by-side in its womb, developed

---

*) Cf. <note>original: "Vgl." - compare</note> Steiner, *The Mutazilites* <note>The Mu'tazilites were an early Islamic school of theology that emphasized human free will and divine justice through reason.</note>, 1865. — al-Kindi <note>An early Arab philosopher known as the "Philosopher of the Arabs."</note> by Flügel. — Chwolsohn, *The Sabians* <note>The Sabians of Harran were a group of pagans who preserved Hellenistic philosophy and science.</note>. cf. Thābit ibn Qurra <note>A famous 9th-century mathematician and astronomer from the Sabian community.</note> 553 ff.

<summary>This page explains how the Sincere Brethren synthesized Aristotelian and Neoplatonic philosophy to promote a view of God as benevolent and humans as free agents. It notes the specific distribution of these philosophical influences across the different sections of their fifty-one treatises.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Neoplatonism, Mu'tazilites, al-Kindi, Thabit ibn Qurra, propaedeutic, theology, world-soul, Sabians, free will</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the bottom of page VII, where the author was likely introducing two branches of thought ("the two children") that emerged from the mix of Greek and Islamic ideas.</meta>

rose to greatness among the Arabs; specifically, the Neoplatonic school of thought within the <term>Sufis: practitioners of Islamic mysticism</term> and their leader <term>al-Ghazali: a highly influential 11th-century theologian and philosopher</term>, and the Aristotelian school among those philosophers and interpreters of Aristotle who, starting from Spain, reintroduced Aristotelianism to the West—most notably <term>Averroes: the Latinized name for the scholar Ibn Rushd, known for his influential commentaries on Aristotle</term>.\*)

5. Thus, Eastern education followed the same path as Western education, where the Neoplatonic school of thought also predominated initially, until after a long struggle between <term>Realism and Idealism: competing medieval philosophies regarding whether general concepts (like "humanity") are real things or just names</term>, the school of Aristotle emerged victorious. However, because Eastern education never entirely lost sight of Aristotle, it remained a significant step ahead of the West, serving as a teacher and guide toward Aristotelian philosophy. —

6. To bring the teachings of philosophy into harmony with the teachings of the Quran, they made use of a mystical interpretation of its verses. Similarly, in Christianity, <term>Origen: an early Christian scholar</term> attempted an "inner interpretation" of Holy Scripture; likewise, in <term>Scholasticism: the medieval method of teaching that dominated European universities</term>, philosophy served <term>dogmatics: the study of established religious doctrines</term> by providing logical proof for its dogmas. —

7. The treatises of this school presented here are not intended for the specialized study of any single discipline—as they themselves reference many other works—but they do provide a portrait of the general education of the Arabs at a time when they were, without doubt, the most educated people in the world. It will become increasingly clear how the Arabs, by absorbing all elements of the Greek learning that reached them and weaving them into a unified whole, acted as the vital link in the chain connecting the knowledge of the ancient world to the burgeoning sciences of the modern era. —

Regarding my own editing of these treatises, let it also be—

---

\*) Compare Renan, *Averroes and Averroism*. <note>original: "Averroès et l'Averroïsme"</note>

<summary>The author explains how Arabic scholarship preserved and evolved Greek philosophy, particularly the Neoplatonic and Aristotelian schools, acting as a bridge that eventually returned this knowledge to the West. He compares the Islamic reconciliation of faith and reason to similar movements in Christian Scholasticism.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Sufism, al-Ghazali, Aristotle, Averroes, Ibn Rushd, Realism, Idealism, Quran, Origen, Scholasticism, Dogmatics, History of Science, Greek Influence</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface started on the previous pages. It continues the author's explanation of his editorial choices regarding the translation and organization of the scientific and philosophical treatises.</meta>

...emphasized that I have made it my primary rule to faithfully and accurately reproduce all the scientific material. In order to make this material useful for <term>linguistics: the scientific study of language</term>, I have included in an appendix not only the scientific <term>terminology: the specialized technical words of a subject</term> themselves, but also the synonyms, opposites, definitions, and classifications, as well as the fundamental rules connected to them, listed in Arabic <note>original: "arabisch"</note>. — In the case of the frequently very long mystical and ethical <term>digressions: passages that stray from the main subject</term>, which might weary the reader—especially in the section on <term>psychology: in this historical context, the study of the soul and its faculties</term>—I have contented myself with a general summary of the content. —

As is self-evident, the index organizes the additional material under the main concepts.

->Berlin, September 1867.<-

->Fr. Dieterici.<-

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, Friedrich Dieterici explains his commitment to scientific accuracy, his inclusion of an Arabic linguistic appendix, and his decision to summarize lengthy mystical passages to keep the text engaging.</summary>
<keywords>linguistics, scientific terminology, Arabic, psychology, Friedrich Dieterici, methodology, appendix</keywords>

<meta>This is the first page of the treatise on theoretical science. It establishes the distinction between practical and theoretical knowledge, setting the stage for a systematic categorization of the sciences.</meta>

# Theoretical Science

---

In this treatise, the theoretical works (*Theoretica*) <note>original: "Theoretica"; here referring to the contemplative sciences</note> of man shall be discussed, whereas a previous work addressed his practical works (*Practica*) <note>original: "Practica"; referring to the manual arts and crafts</note>. In that earlier work, their essence was described, along with the variety of their categories and the specific types within those categories; it also demonstrated how the capacity for action manifests within them <note>The text states that this work on practical arts is already completed, even though it follows this current treatise as the eighth, while this is the seventh. This frequent citation of later treatises suggests that the compilers had the entire collection before them during the editing process.</note>.

The difference between the theoretical and the practical is as follows: In the practical arts, the "subject" (the raw material) is always a natural body, and the "product" remains a physical substance. The purpose of the latter is the cultivation of the earth and the perfection of worldly life. The theoretical sciences, by contrast, take spiritual substances as their "subject"—namely, the souls of the learners—and their effects upon these learners are purely spiritual—compare the treatise on Logic <note>No subsequent treatise carries this specific name, but this is a fundamental theme for later tracts. This should be viewed as a general reference to the study of reasoning.</note>.

Consequently, the purpose of this treatise on the theoretical sciences emerges: It must highlight the essence of the sciences, the variety of their categories, and the specific types within those categories, and demonstrate how to bring the sciences, which exist as a potentiality within the soul, into actualization. The highest—

---

<summary>This page introduces the distinction between practical arts (which deal with physical matter and worldly life) and theoretical sciences (which act upon the soul as a spiritual substance).</summary>
<keywords>theoretical science, practical arts, soul, spiritual substance, logic, actualization, potentiality, education</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on theoretical science, shifting from the general definition of the sciences to the nature of the "subject" of these sciences: the human soul and its relationship to the body.</meta>

The goal of teaching remains to properly restore the substances of souls that would otherwise perish, to rightly guide their negative character traits, and to thus complete and perfect them so that they may endure in the afterlife.

Man is a composite being <term>Gesammtwesen; a unified entity formed from different parts</term> joined together from a physical body and a spiritual soul. These two components are two substances <note>In this philosophical context, a "substance" is a fundamental reality that exists in itself.</note> separated from one another by their properties and standing in opposition to one another in their states; they otherwise share a connection only in accidental actions and secondary qualities. In regard to his physical body, man strives to remain in this world forever; in regard to his spiritual soul, he longs for the other world and strives to reach it.

The same holds true for most human matters and the shifting of his states. They are twofold and opposed to one another: life and death, sleep and waking, knowledge and ignorance, memory and forgetfulness, rationality and folly, sickness and health, chastity and unchastity, greed and generosity, timidity and bravery, pain and pleasure. Man drifts back and forth between friendship and enmity, between poverty and wealth, youth and old age, fear and hope, truth and lies, between right and wrong, correctness and error, between good and bad, ugly and beautiful, and similar character traits, deeds, and speeches which stand in opposition and are distinct from one another.

Such things manifest in man because he is a composite being joined together from a physical body and a spiritual soul—for the qualities just listed relate neither to the body nor to the soul alone; rather, they relate to man as a composite being who, as such, is living, rational, and mortal. His life and his rationality stem from his soul, but his mortality stems from his body. Sleep comes to him from the side of his body, waking from the soul, and the same principle applies to...

<summary>This page defines man as a dualistic composite of body and soul, explaining that human experiences and traits arise from the tension between these two opposing substances.</summary>
<keywords>soul, body, composite being, dualism, life, death, sleep, waking, rationality, mortality</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the human being as a composite entity, specifically detailing which characteristics arise from the soul versus the physical body, and describing the Aristotelian and Galenic foundations of the body’s composition.</meta>

the rest of the separated and opposing things and states, which stem partly from the side of the soul and partly from the side of the body. Reason and knowledge, deliberation, reflection, gentleness, and all praiseworthy qualities stem from the soul and the purity of its substance <note>In this philosophical context, "substance" refers to the fundamental essence of a thing.</note>, but their opposites stem from the mixtures of the body and the mingling of these mixtures.

The properties specifically belonging to the body alone arise from the fact that the body is a physical, natural substance with taste, color, and smell; with movement and rest, roughness and smoothness, softness and hardness. It arises from the four mixtures <term>Mischungen; also known as the "four humors" (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) which were believed to determine a person's health and temperament</term>: blood, phlegm, and the two biles (black and yellow bile). These are produced from the food taken from the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—as well as from the "natures" or primary qualities: heat, cold, dryness, and moisture. The body is subject to corruption—meaning it changes and transforms—and after death (that is, when the soul leaves the body and no longer makes use of it) it returns to the four elements.

The properties specifically belonging to the soul alone stem from the fact that the soul is a spiritual, heavenly light-substance. By its very essence, it is knowing. In its capacity, it accepts instruction, and in its action, it works upon bodies. It makes use of these bodies and brings to completion the animate and plant bodies\* until a certain appointed time; thereafter, however, it leaves them, separates from them, and returns to its fundamental element, its foundation, and its beginning, just as it was in the beginning. The soul does this either with gain and bliss, or in sin, sorrow, and loss.

> cf. Quran 7:29–30. <note>The original text cites 7:28, following a different verse numbering system common in older translations.</note> "As He began you, so shall you return; a group He has guided, and a group deserved error."

24, 117. Do you believe then that we created you only for a joke <note>original: "Scherz"; in this theological context, it implies creating without purpose or in sport</note> and that you would not return to us?\*)

Just as most things concerning man are twofold and opposed to one another—because he is a collective being composed of opposing substances—so too is his fundamental nature twofold in kind. Man has a physical goal, namely money and the usufruct <term>Niessnutz; the right to use and enjoy the fruits or profits of something belonging to another, here referring to the temporary enjoyment of the material world</term> of this world, and a spiritual goal, namely knowledge and religion. Knowledge is the primary goal of the soul; possession is that of the body. Just as man is able, through possession, to partake in the pleasures of food and drink in life, so through knowledge he grasps the path to the other world, and through faith he arrives there. Enlightened by knowledge, he elevates the soul so that it becomes healthy, just as the body increases, grows, and strengthens through food and drink.

Accordingly, there are also two realms: one for food and drink, for joy, play, and bodily lust, which clings to the flesh of animals and the plants of the earth in order to sustain this body—which is subject to change, corruption, and passing away—and another for knowledge, wisdom, and the spiritual perception of the soul’s delight, whose substances do not perish and whose joys in the other world are never interrupted.

The other finds its goal in knowledge, in order to establish the welfare of the soul, the dark...

<meta>This page concludes the introductory thought from the previous page regarding the soul’s journey and begins a formal epistemological discussion. It transitions from the theological purpose of knowledge to the philosophical method of inquiry, specifically influenced by the "Brethren of Purity" (Ikhwan al-Safa), a medieval secret society of Muslim philosophers.</meta>

...to escape folly and find the path to the afterlife, so that the soul may flee this world of coming into being and passing away <note>original: "Entstehens und Vergehens"; a reference to the Aristotelian concept of generation and corruption in the material world</note> and ascend to the expanse of heaven, to Paradise.

## **The Question.**

He who strives for knowledge and wishes to investigate the true nature of things must be clear about this: what knowledge is and what the "known" is, and furthermore, in how many ways a question can be asked and how each of these must be answered; for he who does not understand the asking does not understand the answering.

Knowledge <note>or "Science"; German: "Wissen (Wissenschaft)"</note> is the form of the known thing within the soul of the knower. Ignorance is the opposite: the absence of this form in the soul. The souls of the learned are "knowing" in actuality <note>original: "der That nach"; referring to the philosophical state of *in actu*</note>, while those of the students are "knowing" in potentiality <note>original: "dem Vermögen nach"; referring to the state of *in potentia*</note>. Teaching and being taught is nothing other than the bringing forth of that which lies in potential—that is, in possibility—into actuality, which is real being. When referred to the teacher, this is called "teaching"; when referred to the student, it is called "learning."

Questions are divided into nine types, just as there are nine units <note>a reference to the single-digit numbers 1-9, which the authors often used to mirror the categories of logic</note>.
1) Whether something is; 2) What something is; 3) How much something is; 4) How something is; 5) What kind of thing something is; 6) Where something is; 7) When something is; 8) Why something is; 9) Who something is.

I. **Whether something is?** This is the question regarding the presence or absence of a thing. The answer is "yes" or "no." The concepts of presence and absence are dealt with in the treatise on Reason and its Object <note>original: "Abhandlung über die Vernunft und ihr Object"</note> \*.

II. **What something is?** This is the question regarding the actual constitution or essence of what exists. The nature of a thing is recognized either (a) through its boundary <term>Definition; the exact statement of what a thing is, setting its limits</term> or (b) through its outline <term>Merkmal; a distinguishing characteristic or feature</term>.

---

\*) The 31st treatise of these philosophers; I translated it in the Journal of the German Oriental Society <note>Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenländischen Gesellschaft</note>, Volume XV, page 586.

<summary>This page defines knowledge as a spiritual form existing within the soul and outlines a systematic method of inquiry through nine fundamental questions. It distinguishes between the potential knowledge of a student and the actualized knowledge of a teacher.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, potentiality and actuality, nine questions, nature of the soul, Ikhwan al-Safa, definition, essence of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methodology of inquiry. The previous page ended mid-sentence while introducing the "nine types of questions" used to investigate reality. This page addresses how to define and understand the nature of things, specifically distinguishing between "composite" and "simple" entities, a core concept in medieval Aristotelian philosophy used by the Brethren of Purity.</meta>

All things fall into two categories: a) **composite** and b) **simple**. A **body**, for example, is **composite**, whereas **matter** and **form** are **simple**, for these are the two things from which the body is composed; see the **Treatise on Matter** <note>German: "Abhandlung über die Materie"</note>.*) We understand the nature of a composite thing when we recognize the elements from which it is made.

If we ask, for instance, about the actual nature of **clay**, the answer is: "Clay is dust and water mixed together." If we ask about **oxymel** <note>a medicinal drink made of honey and vinegar; from the Greek *oxymeli*</note>, we say it is honey and vinegar blended. This applies to every composite thing. When inquiring about it, one must specify the things from which it is composed and through which it is described. Scholars call this description a **definition**, and they therefore say the definition of a **body** is: "It is a thing that has length, width, and depth." In this context, the word "thing" refers to the matter, and "length, width, and depth" refer to the form; for the nature of a body is found only in those attributes mentioned in its definition.

The definition of **Man** is: "A living, rational, mortal being." With "living and rational," one signifies the **soul**, and with "mortal," the **body**. For **Man** is an **integrated being** <note>German: "Gesammtwesen"</note> composed of these two: the physical body and the spiritual soul.

In this manner, we recognize the nature of composite things. However, regarding things which are not composed of two others, but are instead brought forth and initiated anew by the Will of God, their nature is recognized through the properties uniquely inherent to them.

If we ask, for example, about the nature of **matter**, the answer is: Matter is a simple **substance**, which takes on forms and possesses no quality of its own. If we ask, "What is **form**?" the answer is: Form is that whereby

---

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical distinction between simple and composite entities. The previous page ended while explaining that simple things (those not made of parts) cannot be defined by their components, but must be described by their specific attributes.</meta>

...the thing that it is, what it is. Scholars call such descriptions an **attribute** <note>German: *Merkmal*; in logic, this refers to a distinguishing characteristic that identifies a thing without necessarily defining its internal essence</note>.

Thus, the difference between a **definition** and an **attribute** is that the definition is derived from the parts that compose the thing being defined, whereas the attribute is taken from the specific descriptions that belong uniquely to that particular entity. Another difference is that the definition expresses the **substance** <note>German: *Substanz*; the fundamental "what-ness" or essence of a thing</note> of the defined thing and distinguishes it from everything else, whereas an attribute only distinguishes the specific thing it identifies from that which is outside of it.

Therefore, one should not rush to answer the question regarding the nature of a thing, but should first consider whether it is composite or simple.

**III. How much is something?** is the question regarding the **measure of a thing**. Measurable things fall into two kinds: a) **continuous** and b) **discrete** <note>German: *getrennte*; refers to things that can be counted as individual units</note>. Of the continuous, there are five: **line, surface, body, time, and place**. Of the discrete, there are two: **number and motion**.

For all of these, one asks "how much?"

The essence of number is treated in **Arithmetic** <note>original footnote: cf. Dieterici, *The Propaedeutics of the Arabs*, 1865. Treatise I</note>. The essence of motion, time, place, and body is discussed in the **Treatise on Matter**.

The essence of lines and surfaces is dealt with in **Mathematics**. (II.)

**IV. How is something?** is the question regarding the descriptions of things. These fall into many kinds. This is discussed in the **Treatise on the Ten Categories**, each of which is a **genus of genera** <note>German: *Gattung von Gattungen*; a fundamental class of being that is not a sub-class of anything else</note>. (XI.)

**V. What kind of thing is something?** is the question regarding one out of a multitude, or a part of a whole. If one says, for example, "The star rose," one asks "Which star?" for there are many stars. But if one says "The sun rose," one does not ask "Which sun?" since there is no plurality in its **genus** <note>German: *Gattung*</note>, and the same applies to the moon.

**VI. Where is something?** is the question regarding the place, the position

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the categories of existence. The previous page concluded by defining "how much" (quantity) through continuous measures (lines, surfaces, bodies, time, place) and discrete measures (number and motion). It now transitions into the categories of "where" or "position," distinguishing between physical place, the station of attributes, and spiritual rank.</meta>

...or the level of a thing. Between these three there is the following difference:

a) **Place** <note>German: *Ort*</note> is a description for some, but not all, bodies. If one asks, for example: "Where do we want to go?" one answers: "into the house, to the market, to the mosque," or to some other location.

b) **Station** <note>German: *Stelle*; referring to the "place" or "position" of a property within a subject</note> is a description of the <term>Accident</term> <note>Latin: *Accidens*; in classical philosophy, a property or quality that is not essential to the nature of a thing, such as its color or a specific mood</note>. This itself is divided into two types: physical and spiritual. The physical <term>Accident</term> is a condition of the body. If one asks, for example: "Where is the blackness?" one answers that it is a condition of the black body. This applies to all colors, tastes, and smells; they are conditions found in bodies endowed with taste, color, and scent. The same applies to every physical <term>Accident</term>.

The spiritual <term>Accidents</term> are conditions within spiritual substances. If one asks: "Where is science?" one answers that it is a condition of the **soul** of the scholar; the same applies to generosity, courage, justice, and similar qualities. They are conditions of the **soul**, as are their opposites.

Many scholars, who possess neither clear insight regarding the **soul** nor knowledge of its substances, believe these <term>Accidents</term> are conditions within the body, and specifically that each of them is attached to a specific organ.

Thus they say that knowledge lies in the heart, desire in the liver, reason in the brain, courage in the gallbladder, and timidity in the spleen <note>This reflects the medieval theory of humorism, where different organs were thought to produce the "humors" or fluids that governed human temperament and personality</note>.

We, on the other hand, have demonstrated in the treatise on the composition of the body that these members are merely tools and instruments for the **soul**, through which and by which such actions and character traits manifest in the body\*).

c) **Degree** <note>German: *Stufe*; referring to rank or level of existence</note> is a description of spiritual substances. If one asks, for example: "Where is the **soul**?" one answers that it stands under—

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the philosophical categories used to describe existence. While the previous page dealt with physical place and the 'station' of accidents (properties), this page moves into the metaphysical rank of spiritual substances, followed by the categories of Time (When), Causality (Why), and Identity (Who).</meta>

...of reason and above nature. If one asks where the number five stands among numbers, one says: after four and before six. It is the same with **spiritual substances**. These are described neither by physical place nor by their station, but by their **rank** <note>German: *Stufe*; referring here to a hierarchical level of being rather than a physical location</note>; compare the *Treatise on the Principles of Reason* <note>original: "Abhandlung von den Principien der Vernunft"</note>.*

VII. **When** is the question regarding the time of a thing’s existence. There are three divisions of time: the **past**, such as yesterday; the **future**, such as tomorrow; and the **present**, such as today. The same applies to years, months, days, and hours. The question of what time is and the various opinions of scholars on the matter are discussed in the *Treatise on Matter*.

VIII. **Why?** is the question regarding the **cause** <note>German: *Ursache*</note> of a thing that has been brought about. Every effect produced has four causes:
a) the **material**
b) the **formal**
c) the **efficient** <note>German: *bewirkende*; the agent or force that brings the thing into being</note>
d) the **final** <note>German: *vollendende*; the ultimate purpose or end goal</note>.

In the case of a chair, a gate, or a bed:
a) the **material cause** is the wood;
c) the **efficient cause** is the carpenter;
b) the **formal cause** is the square shape;
d) the **final cause**—the purpose—is to sit upon it (for a chair), to sleep in it (for a bed), or to secure the house (for a gate).
In this way, every effect has four causes. Therefore, if one asks for the cause of a thing, one must first know which cause is being asked for and then answer accordingly.

IX. **Who?** is the question regarding a specific identification. Grammarians say this question is only applicable to beings endowed with **reason**; others claim it applies to any being endowed with knowledge and the power of discernment**). This question is answered by one of these three:

a) In relation to the city of birth, such as a **Basran** <note>German: *Basrenser*; an inhabitant of Basra, a major intellectual center in Iraq</note>.

b) In relation to lineage, that is, family, such as a **Hashemite** <note>German: *Haschimit*; referring to the Banu Hashim, the clan of the Prophet Muhammad</note>.

c) In relation to a trade, such as a **carpenter**.

---

*) The sequence given there is: 1) **God**, 2) **Universal Intellect** <note>German: *Urvernunft*</note>, 3) **Universal Soul** <note>German: *Allseele*</note>, 4) **Nature**; see XXXII. *Animal and Man* 232.

**) This category thus also includes animals of a higher level, as poets often use the word "who" <note>German: *man* or *wer*</note> in reference to animals.

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical discussion of the "Eight Questions" (specifically the "Why") and transitions into a comprehensive classification of human knowledge. It bridges the gap between general metaphysical inquiry and the specific study of Logic and the various sciences.</meta>

So ends the concise presentation of the questions and answers regarding scientific research and the consideration of the nature of things. These must be established as an introduction before the treatment of Logic—indeed, even before the introduction (<term>Isagoge</term> <note>original: *Isagoge*; a reference to Porphyry’s "Introduction" to Aristotle’s Categories, a standard textbook in the Middle Ages</note>) to Logic itself.

## The Classification of the Sciences.

We move now to highlighting the genres of the sciences and the species of these genres; for the soul has a longing for the various sciences and types of education just as the body longs for different tastes, colors, and smells.

The sciences pursued by men fall into three genres: a) Preliminary Studies, b) Religious-Legal, c) Properly Philosophical.

I. **Preliminary Studies** are educational sciences, most of which are established for the pursuit of a livelihood and to properly manage the affairs of life. They fall into nine types: a) Reading and Writing; b) Lexicography and Grammar; c) Calculation and Accounting; d) Poetry and Metrics; e) The study of Premonitions and Omens; f) The study of Sorcery and Amulets, Alchemy, Deceptions <note>German: *Listen*; likely referring to mechanical tricks or illusions</note>, and the like; g) The study of Manual Trades and Crafts; h) The study of Buying and Selling, Commerce, Agriculture, and Animal Husbandry; i) Biographies and Historical Accounts.

II. The **Religious-Legal Sciences** serve the soul’s striving for the next world. There are five of these: a) The Science of Revelation; b) The Science of Interpretation <note>German: *Erklärung*; likely referring to *Tafsir* or exegesis</note>; c) The Science of Citation and Reporting <note>German: *Anführung und Berichtung*; likely referring to the study of *Hadith* or prophetic traditions</note>; d) Jurisprudence, Statutes, and Decisions; e) The study of the Remembrance of God, Exhortation, Asceticism, and <term>Sufism</term> <note>original: *Sufithum*; Islamic mysticism</note>—the study of dreams also belongs here.

Scholars of Revelation are the Reciters <note>German: *Vorleser*; referring to Quranic reciters</note> and those who preserve the text in memory. Scholars of Interpretation are the Imams and the representatives of the Prophets. Scholars of Citations...

<summary>This page concludes the introductory philosophical categories and begins a systematic classification of knowledge, dividing sciences into practical preparatory studies for daily life and religious-legal sciences for the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Classification of Sciences, Logic, Isagoge, Education, Sufism, Religious Law, Alchemy, Jurisprudence</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the Religious-Legal sciences from the previous page before moving into the third major genre: the Philosophical Sciences. It specifically focuses on the structure of introductory studies and the foundational role of logic and Aristotelian demonstration.</meta>

...are the masters of Tradition <note>referring to the *Hadith* or oral traditions in an Islamic context</note>. Scholars of statutes and decrees are the Jurists. Scholars of the Remembrance of God are the servants of God, the ascetics, the Sufis, monks, and the like.

**III.** The philosophical sciences are divided into four branches: **a)** Propaedeutics <note>preparatory or introductory instruction</note>, **b)** Logic, **c)** Natural Science, and **d)** Theology.

**a)** Propaedeutics is divided into Arithmetic, Geometry, Astronomy, and Music*).

**b)** The logical sciences. These are divided into two types: **a)** the **Analytics**, that is, the knowledge of the art of understanding; **b)** the **Rhetorics**, that is, the knowledge of the art of those who seek to lead others astray during disputes and verbal combat (the Sophists). Scholars of earlier and later times have treated these arts and sciences and have composed books about them which are widely circulated among the people. **Aristotle** wrote three books on this subject, which he positioned as an introduction to the *Book of Demonstration*: **a)** **Categories**, **b)** **On Interpretation** <note>original: *Hermeneutica*</note>, **c)** **Prior Analytics** <note>original: *Analytika priora*</note>.

**Aristotle** then devoted his greatest care to the *Book of Demonstration* <note>referring to the *Posterior Analytics*, which deals with obtaining certain knowledge</note>. For demonstration is the scale of the scholars, through which they distinguish truth from falsehood, the correct from the erroneous, as well as right from wrong, and good from evil—just as the common people recognize the actual value of weighed, measured, and surveyed things through weights, measures, and yardsticks. For just as one proceeds in those matters through various estimations and conjectures, scholars recognize the true nature of things through the art of demonstration when they differ in opinion and view. Skilled poets also prove the symmetry or the flaws of verses through metrics—which is indeed the scale of the art of poetry—wherever a difference of opinion asserts itself.

I have omitted the further details of the content of this article here; geography, as a part of general astronomy, and geometric relation, as a part of arithmetic and geometry, are not listed here.

<meta>This page continues the classification of the philosophical sciences, transitioning from the general overview of logic to the specific curriculum of the Aristotelian "Organon." It focuses on the introductory works required before a student can master the "Book of Demonstration."</meta>

Porphyry of Tyre wrote a book which he called *Isagoge* <note>original: *Isagoge*; Greek for "Introduction"</note> and which serves as an introduction to the art of philosophical logic.

These sciences have been treated extensively and in great detail, and those books have been translated from one language into another. However, this was done by people who did not understand their true meaning, so that the actual sense remained hidden from students. For this reason, each of these sciences shall be treated here in its own separate treatise, in which the necessary points are highlighted and all wordiness has been avoided. It is appropriate here to state their objectives in advance.

**I.** The purpose of the *Isagoge* is to provide knowledge of the meaning of the six words <note>The standard Aristotelian tradition usually refers to "five voices" (quinque voces), but this text includes "the individual" as a sixth.</note> which philosophers apply in their pronouncements. These are the terms: **Individual**, **Species**, **Genus**, **Essential Difference**, **Permanent Property**, and **Accidental Property** <term>Accident <note>In logic, an "accident" is a characteristic that is not part of the essential nature of a thing—for example, being "tall" is an accident of a human being, whereas being "rational" is an essential difference.</note></term>.

**II.** The treatise on the *Categories* <note>Aristotle's *Kategoriai*</note> aims to present the meaning of the ten words, each of which represents a "genus of genera" <note>A primary classification of all things that exist.</note>. One of these is **Substance** and nine are **Accidents**. This treatise teaches us what each of these is and how many species each has; it then shows us the distinguishing mark that separates one from the others; finally, it indicates how these categories lead toward all meanings that reside within the thoughts of the soul.

**III.** The treatise *Hermeneutica* <note>original: *Hermeneutica*; Greek title *Peri Hermeneias*, or "On Interpretation"</note> has the purpose of showing the combination of the ten words (the categories) and indicating the meanings that result from this combination, so that pronouncements and judgments arise. These are partly false and partly correct.

**IV.** The treatise *Analytika priora* <note>original: *Analytika priora*; "Prior Analytics"</note> indicates how one—

->The natural sciences are divided into seven types.<-

I. The science of the origins of the body. This is the knowledge of five things: matter, form, time, place, and motion.

II. The science of heaven and earth. This is the knowledge of the essence of the celestial substances and the stars, their number, the manner of their arrangement, and the reason for their revolution; furthermore, it provides the answer to the question: whether these are subject to generation and corruption <note>Coming into being and passing away.</note> in the same way as the four elements beneath the lunar sphere or not. Furthermore, it specifies the cause of the motion of the stars and their variations in speed and slowness. It teaches us the reason why the earth rests in the middle of the celestial sphere as the center point and, finally, answers the questions: whether there is another body outside the world or not; whether there is a vacuum in the world in which there is nothing, and other such matters.

III. The treatise on generation and corruption <note>Referring to Aristotle's *De Generatione et Corruptione*.</note> teaches the nature of the substance of the four elements: fire, air, water, and earth. It shows how one of these transforms into another through the influence of the celestial bodies on high, so that from this, that which newly arises and exists—mineral, plant, and animal—develops until they return again to the elements upon their decay.

IV. The treatise on atmospheric phenomena <note>Referring to the *Meteorologica*.</note> shows how the air changes through the influence of the stars, their movements, and the casting of their rays upon these elements, and what the air undergoes from them. It is a special property of air to take on many colors and changes, such as light and darkness, cold and heat. Furthermore, this [treatise] shows...

V. The doctrine of mineral substances. They thicken from vapors held within the interior of the earth and from juices that are produced through transformation in the air, in caves, in the soil, and in the depths of the sea. These include substances and aromatic scents, sulfur and mercury, <term>alums <note>A group of hydrated double salts, historically used in dyeing and medicine.</note></term> and salts, ammonia, gold, silver, ore, iron, lead, <term>black lead <note>Original: *Schwarzblei*; historically used to refer to graphite or molybdenum.</note></term>, <term>stibium <note>Antimony, often used in antiquity as a cosmetic or medicinal substance.</note></term>, beryl, <term>hyacinth <note>In historical texts, this usually refers to a yellow, orange, or reddish-brown zircon or garnet, rather than the flower.</note></term>, <term>bezoar stone <note>A mass of undigested material found in the gastrointestinal tract of animals, once prized for its supposed power as an antidote to poison.</note></term>, and the like. Their unique characteristics, their uses, and their harms are demonstrated.

VI. The treatise on plants deals with everything that is planted or sown, or that sprouts of its own accord upon the earth, on the peaks of mountains, in the depths of the sea, or along the banks of rivers—such as trees, crops, vegetables, herbs, grass, and climbing vines. It teaches the multitude of their species and subspecies, and speaks of the places where they sprout across various lands; how they stretch their roots into the earth and raise their branches into the air; and how they spread across the ground and extend their boughs in every direction. It then describes the form of their branches—which may be long, short, thin, thick, straight, or crooked—and the shape of their leaves—which may be broad, narrow, delicate, or rough. Finally, their flowers and blossoms, the formation of their fruits, pits, and grains, their resins, tastes, and smells, as well as their unique characteristics, uses, and harms are discussed one by one.

VII. The doctrine of animals deals with other bodies, such as the worms in the bellies of animals, in the hearts of plants, and in fruit and grain. The multitude of their genera, their species, and the subspecies of those species are described. It shows how they arise in the womb, in the egg, or through <term>decay <note>Original: *Fäulniss* (putrefaction). This refers to the historical theory of "spontaneous generation," the belief that certain organisms like maggots or parasites could arise directly from rotting matter.</note></term>; how their limbs are assembled and their bodies joined, and how their forms vary and their types are composed. The theological sciences are divided into 5 types.

**I. The Doctrine of the Creator;** a description of His unity, how He is the founder of everything in existence and the creator of all that is created; how He encompasses existence within Himself and bestows it. God is the embodiment of all excellence and goodness; He maintains order, grants permanence, and guides the universe; He knows that which is hidden. God is the First of every thing as its beginning and the Last of it as its end; He is visible over every thing in power and hidden within every thing in knowledge <note>This description of God as "The First and the Last, the Evident and the Immanent" is a direct reference to Surah Al-Hadid (57:3) in the Quran.</note>. He is hearing, knowing, kind, well-informed, and gentle toward His servants.

**II. The Doctrine of Spiritual Beings.**

This is the knowledge of simple, rational, knowing, and creative <term>substances <note>Original: *Substanzen (Wesen)*; in this philosophical context, "substances" refers to independent, immaterial essences that do not require a physical body to exist.</note></term>, which are the angels of God and His pure servants. These are forms free from matter, which make use of bodies (assuming them) and guide them, and then their effects proceed from these. Furthermore, it is discussed here how some of these beings connect to others and how some are ranked above others. These are the spiritual spheres, which encompass the physical bodies.

**III. The Science of the Soul,** that is, the knowledge of souls and spirits, which [govern] the natural and spherical bodies from the surrounding sphere to the center of the earth

### IV. The Doctrine of Guidance

This is divided into five types: a) prophetic guidance, b) royal guidance, c) guidance of communities, d) guidance of those close to one, e) guidance of one's own self.

**a) Prophetic guidance** teaches how to establish the <term>Nomos</term> <note>original: "Nomos (Urgesetz)"; the Greek word for "law," here referring to the primordial or divine law</note>, that is, the pleasing statutes and customs, and how to compile them into eloquent words. It also heals sick souls of false beliefs, incorrect views, bad habits, and injustice, removing such perspectives from within. This is achieved by leading them away from evil and toward the good, and by making them eager for the glorious reward on the Day of Return <note>likely referring to the afterlife or resurrection</note>; however, it turns bad souls who stray from the right goal back toward salvation through threats and reprimands, and awakens negligent souls from the sleep of carelessness.

**b) Royal guidance** provides the knowledge of how to preserve the law for the people and thereby enliven religion by commanding what is good and forbidding what is evil. This is accomplished by upholding the provisions and decisions of legal experts, preventing injustice, suppressing enemies, and protecting the good. Such guidance is specifically characteristic of the chosen prophets and the rightly-guided <term>Imams</term> <note>spiritual and political leaders; in this context, they are the successors who maintain the divine order</note>, who decide upon what is right.

**c) Community guidance** is the leadership of congregations; such as that of the <term>Emirs</term> <note>commanders or governors</note> who are placed over districts and cities, that of the village headmen over the villages, and that of the military commanders over the soldiers. For this, one must know the ranks and conditions, the relationships (lineages) and skills, and the methods of teaching and characters of those being ruled. One must establish their orders, watch over their affairs, seek out what is necessary for them, regulate their community, mediate between them, and—

**d) Specific guidance** teaches how every person may find their rightful place, properly order their life with their servants, laborers, children, and slaves, and interact correctly with their relatives, neighbors, companions, allies, and brothers. It shows how to seek what is right and how to well-regulate the affairs of others for both this world and the world to come.

**e) Self-guidance.** Every person must know themselves and investigate their own character, properly ordering action and word in desire, anger, and pleasure, and correctly grasping all their affairs.

### V. The Doctrine of the Return and Escape

The doctrine of the Return and the escape to the other world <note>This refers to the "Return" (Ma'ad) to God or the afterlife, a central theme in Islamic philosophy.</note> shows how spirits are called forth from the dark body and how souls awaken from a careless slumber; it explains how they are gathered on the Day of Return to walk the straight path and are united for the reckoning on the Day of Judgment, so that they may recognize the reward of the good and the punishment of the evil.

The purpose of all scientific treatises is this: they must mark the path which the prophets, the chosen ones, the scholars, and the sages once trod. The beginning of all science <note>In this context, the German "Wissenschaft" refers to organized knowledge or philosophical study.</note> rests in the knowledge of one's own self; for he who knows himself, knows his Lord, and he who knows himself best, knows his Lord best <note>original: "wer sich selbst kennt, kennt auch seinen Herrn"; a famous Sufi tradition (Hadith) often cited in medieval philosophy.</note>.

The knowledge of one's self rests on four points for a human being.

**a)** Man must know that he is a collective being composed of two substances that are distinct from one another. He consists of a body—provided with length, breadth, and depth and physical substances, perceptible by the five senses—and a soul, which is a simple, spiritual, rational, heavenly <term>light-substance</term> <note>original: "Lichtsubstanz"; a Neoplatonic concept describing the soul as a literal or metaphorical spark of divine light.</note>; this soul is knowing, perceiving, active, and provided with spiritual <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "Accidensen"; a philosophical term referring to qualities or properties that belong to a substance but are not part of its essential nature.</note>; among these one counts knowledge, determination...

<meta>This page marks the transition from the general study of the human soul to the specific science of Logic. It follows the previous page's curriculum on the soul's journey by introducing "rational speech" (the Greek *logos*) as the unique human faculty that allows for the study of all other sciences.</meta>

# The Introduction to Logic <note>This tenth treatise is titled using the Greek term *Isagoge* (εἰσαγώγη), meaning "Introduction." In the medieval world, this usually referred to the famous introduction to Aristotle’s logic written by Porphyry.</note>

Man is the most excellent creature under the **sublunary sphere** <note>original: "Mondkreis"; in medieval cosmology, this refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—the realm below the moon where all things are subject to change, growth, and decay.</note>. Among his many virtues are the sciences and the arts. Yet, the most perfect of his arts is **speech** <note>The German "Rede" here translates the Arabic *mantiq*, which encompasses both "speech" and "logic/reasoning."</note> (specifically, rational speech). Since man is distinguished from all other creatures by speech, we shall now present the *what*, the *how many*, and the *how* of this faculty. Man is defined as a living, speaking, and mortal being, whereas other creatures are living and mortal, but not speaking.

Among all the works of human art, speech is the one that is inherently spiritual. For all other arts, physical bodies are used as the raw material, and their products are entirely physical substances. In the case of speech, however, the substances of the **partial souls** <note>The "partial soul" (al-nafs al-juz'iyya) is the individual human soul, which the Brethren of Purity believed was a fragment or emanation of the "Universal Soul."</note> serve as the material. The effects of speech upon these souls are spiritual in nature, such as promises, threats, encouragement, deterrence, praise, and blame.

The effect of speech on souls is similar to the effect of one physical body upon another. The effects of bodies on one another are sometimes healing and sometimes destructive. Food and drink are healing for the bodies of living creatures, as are medicinal potions and remedies for a sick body; conversely, fire is destructive to the bodies of animals and plants, as is the cut of a sword or a knife. In the same way, speech and discourse bring sometimes healing and sometimes ruin to souls.

<note>original: "*) Die X. Abhandlung ist mit dem griechischen Wort Isaguga εἰσαγώγη überschrieben."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the power of speech from page 19, shifting from its psychological effects to its formal definition and the distinction between internal thought and external expression. It introduces the fundamental Aristotelian and Neoplatonic division of "Logos" into two forms, a concept central to the Brethren of Purity’s logic.</meta>

Eulogy and praise inspire the soul toward noble deeds; admonition and threats deter it from evil. Conversely, slander and intimidation bring ruin, as do words that incite the soul to hatred. Thus it is said: "Many a word sparks division and strife, yet many another extinguishes the fires of battle." A poet sings: "A word that inspires awe in souls suffices them like a supreme commander." Swords never achieve through slaughter what pens accomplish through their threats and promises.

Among the virtues of speech is its correspondence to that which exists, just as a number <gloss>as a function</gloss> corresponds to the things counted; hence the multitude of languages, the diversity of pronouncements, and the various turns of phrase. No one recognizes them entirely, except for God. A few of these aspects shall be mentioned here.

## Spoken Discourse

**Mantiq** <note>original: "Manṭiḳ"; this Arabic term means both "logic" and "speech," mirroring the Greek word *logos*.</note> (rational speech) is derived from **nataqa** <note>original: "naṭaḳa"; the Arabic root meaning "to speak" or "to articulate."</note>. It is one of the acts of the human soul, and this activity is divided into two types: internal thought and external expression (thought-speech and word-speech).

Spoken discourse is something physical and perceptible to the senses; thought-speech, however, is something spiritual, graspable only by the intellect. Spoken discourse consists of audible sounds for which there is an alphabet; it originates from the tongue—an organ of the human body—and travels to the place of hearing in the ears, which are likewise organs of the human body.

The study of this speech and the investigation into it—the discourse on its "how" and its variations, and finally the meaning it conveys—is called the **science of spoken discourse** <note>In this context, the author is likely referring to grammar and phonetics as the physical precursors to logic.</note>.

Internal speech, by contrast, is something spiritual and conceptual. it consists of the meanings of things being formed within the essence of the soul, so that the soul perceives the characteristics of sensible things within its own substance and distinguishes them through its thinking. With this speech...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 19 and 20 regarding the nature of speech (Logos/Mantiq). It moves from the psychological impact of words to a formal definition of "Man" and a semiotic breakdown of how thoughts are transformed into spoken and written language, following the system of the Brethren of Purity.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<sig>1</sig>

Man is defined when it is said: he is a living, speaking, mortal <term>being</term> <note>This is the classical Aristotelian definition: *animal rationale mortale*.</note>. Thus, speech and the life of man proceed from the soul, while his death proceeds from the body. For the name "man" applies only to the soul and body joined together.

The theory of this discourse, the investigation into it, and the knowledge of how the soul grasps the meanings of all that exists in its essence by means of the senses, and how it receives those meanings into its faculty of thought through reason, is called **Revelation** and **Inspiration**. The expression of these meanings in the words of any language is called the doctrine of **philosophical logic**.

Since spoken discourse is something physical—a clearly emerging, perceptible sound—it was established among men so that one might make known to others the meanings that were within his soul when they questioned him or conversed with him.

Spoken discourse consists of words which are composed of articulated letters. These letters fall into three types: a) Thought-letters, b) Word-letters, c) Written letters.

**Thought-letters** are spiritual forms in the thoughts of souls, which are shaped within the substance of the soul even before it sends forth its meanings into words.

**Word-letters** are sounds carried through the air and captured by the power of hearing via the ears; compare the treatise on sensory perception, XXIII <note>original: "XXIII"; referring to the 23rd Epistle of the Brethren of Purity regarding the senses.</note>.

**Written letters** are outlines written with reeds <note>original: "Schreibröhren"; referring to the *qalam* or reed pen used in manuscript traditions.</note> upon the surface of tablets or within books, and are captured by the power of sight via the eyes. — Written letters were established to indicate word-letters; word-letters stand as signs for thought-letters. — Thought-letters are therefore the original, and we shall treat them specifically. Word-letters are nothing but sounds produced in the throat, on the palate, and between the tongue and lips during the expulsion of breath from the lungs, after it has fanned the natural heat of the heart.

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of language and logic from page 21. While the previous page defined the nature of "man" and the different forms letters take (mental, spoken, and written), this page moves into a hierarchical breakdown of language—from the smallest unit (the letter) to complex logical and religious arguments. The author draws a significant parallel between the physical tools of trade (weights and measures) and the intellectual tools of logic.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

In the Arabic language, there are 28 letters, while in others there are sometimes more and sometimes fewer; compare the causes of the diversity of languages (XXX) <note>original: "XXX"; This likely refers to the 30th Epistle of the Brethren of Purity, which discusses the diversity of languages.</note>.

Letters, through their pronunciation, become words; words, when they contain meanings, become names; and names, when they follow one another correctly, become parts of speech. Parts of speech, however, when they are expanded, become a **statement** (sentence).

Statements fall into two types:
a) **Measured speech**, such as verses, improvisations, and rhymes.
b) **Prose** <note>original: "zerstreutes"; a translation of the Arabic *nathr*, meaning "scattered," which is the standard term for prose.</note>.

Prose is further divided into two types: into eloquence and rhetoric; and into conversation and address. These latter two also fall into two types: those used by the general public to satisfy their daily needs without bringing forth a compelling reason or legal proof; or those used in assertions and disputes where a compelling reason and proof are applied.

Assertion and dispute are again divided into two types: they concern either worldly affairs or matters of religion, doctrine, and science.

Just as the knowledge of the correctness of a claim in worldly affairs occurs only through witnesses and legal documents, the proofs of the correctness of statements in religious, doctrinal, and scientific matters rest only upon testimonies in the divine books, upon reports from the scholars of religious law, and the consensus of opposing parties. This is achieved partly through the testimony of reason and partly through correct <term>analogy</term> <note>original: *Qiyas*; the method of deductive analogy used in Islamic jurisprudence and logic to find a solution for a new problem based on a known precedent.</note>, which is indeed the **Scale of Truth**.

Furthermore, just as the estimation and opinion regarding the value of things that can be weighed or measured necessitated the establishment of measures and weights in order to resolve disputes, the differences among scholars in the determination and opinion regarding things hidden from the senses forced the establishment of **norms** <note>In this context, "norms" refers to the rules of logic (Mantiq).</note> in order to balance the differences in theoretical considerations.

Just as one finally, in order to correctly [determine] weight and measure—

It argues that just as physical trade requires standardized weights, intellectual and religious disputes require the "Scale of Truth"—logic and analogy—to reach a correct conclusion.</summary>

This is further elaborated in the books on logic <note>The author is referring to the *Organon*, the traditional collection of Aristotle's logical treatises which heavily influenced medieval thought.</note>.

### **The words which signify the meanings residing within the thoughts of the soul.**

We shall first state: what is the name, who is the namer, what is the naming, and what is the named; furthermore: who is the describer, what is the described, and what is the description; finally: who is the one stating an attribute, what is the thing possessed of an attribute, and what the attribute itself is.

**Name** is every word that leads to one of the meanings.

**Namer** is the one who speaks the name.

**Naming** is the utterance of the speaker.

**Named** is the indicated meaning.

**Describer** is the speaker.

**Description** is a meaning attached to the thing being described.

**Describing** is the utterance of the speaker.

**Described** is the essence of that which is being pointed out.

**Attributor** <note>original: "Beeigenschaftend"; refers to the person assigning a quality or property to something.</note> is the speaker.

**Attribute** is the word used by the speaker.

**The Attributed** (that which is provided with an attribute) is the essence of that which is designated.

In this last class, there is no specific term for the meaning attached to the thing provided with an attribute, as is the case with "description," since the description inherently clings to the described.

The words which the philosophers employ in their dictates to express the meanings resting within the thoughts of the soul fall into six types. Three of these signify **essences** <note>original: "Wesen"; in this philosophical context, it refers to the 'being' or 'substance' of a thing.</note> which are described, and three of them result in meanings which are **descriptions**. The...

<summary>This page establishes logic as the "weight and measure" of truth and defines the semantic relationship between speakers, words, and the objects they describe. It introduces a technical framework for naming, describing, and attributing qualities to things.</summary>
