--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of logical terms from page 23. It transitions from defining the acts of naming and describing to categorizing the specific types of words used in these processes—specifically the "Five Universals" (predicables) that were foundational to medieval logic and science.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

...the three words that signify the things described are: **Individual**, **Species**, and **Genus**; the three words that signify the descriptions are: **Difference**, **Property**, and **Accident** <note>These six terms (expanded from the traditional five) are the building blocks of Aristotelian logic, used to categorize everything in the natural world.</note>.

**a) An Individual** <note>original: "Individuum"</note> refers to a word that signifies a single entity, existing apart from other things and perceptible through the senses—such as *this* man, *this* tree, *this* wall, or *this* stone. All these words signify separate things that exist on their own.

**b) A Species** <note>original: "Art"</note> refers to a word that signifies a plurality encompassed by a single form, such as "human" or "horse." In short, it is any word that encompasses a number of individuals of a matching form.

**c) A Genus** <note>original: "Gattung"</note> refers to a word that indicates a multitude of different forms which are all, in turn, enclosed by another broader form—such as "creature," "plant," "fruit," "grain," and similar words. Each of these encompasses a multitude of different forms. "Creature" encompasses humans, livestock, wild animals, birds, fish, and aquatic animals; these are all different forms, all of which possess life. This would therefore be the form of "Living Being" <note>original: "Creatur" / "Lebendes"</note> with a complete body.

The descriptions are divided into three types\*:

**a)** Those which, if they cease to exist, also cause the thing they describe to become void. These are called **differences** (distinctive), **essential**, or **substantial**. Such descriptions include heat for fire, moisture for water, dryness for stone, and the like. If the heat of the fire ceases, the existence of the fire is also over; with the cessation of moisture, the existence of water also ends.

**b)** Those which, when they vanish, do not cancel the existence of the described thing, yet they depart from it only slowly. For example: the blackness of pitch, the whiteness of snow, the sweetness of honey, the scent of musk.

---

\*) <note>The *Isagoge* (Introduction) by Porphyrius <note>A 3rd-century scholar whose introduction to Aristotle became the standard logic textbook for over a millennium.</note> recognizes only five words: **genus** <note>original Greek: γένος</note>, **species** <note>original Greek: εἶδος</note>, **difference** <note>original Greek: διαϕόρα</note>, **property** <note>original Greek: ἴδιον</note>, and **accident** <note>original Greek: συμβεβηκός</note>; in Latin: *genus, species, differentia, proprium, accidens*.</note>

<summary>This page defines the hierarchical relationship between individuals, species, and genera, while also distinguishing between essential qualities (differences) and non-essential but stable qualities (accidents).</summary>
<keywords>logic, Porphyrius, genus, species, individual, essential difference, accident, Aristotelian philosophy, taxonomy</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of logical descriptions, specifically distinguishing between "accidental" properties that change quickly and "specific" properties (propria) that help define a species.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

and of camphor. It is not necessary that, if the blackness of pitch and the whiteness of snow are no longer present, the essences of these things also cease to exist.

Such descriptions are called specific <note>original: "specielle"; here referring to properties that are characteristic of a specific type of thing</note>.

**c)** The rapidly vanishing qualities are called <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "Accidens"; in logic, an "accident" is a characteristic that can change or disappear without changing what the thing actually is—like a person sitting down or standing up</note>, such as the redness of shame, the yellowness of fear, sitting, standing, sleeping, waking, and the like; they are called <term>accidental</term> (incidental), because they happen to the thing and then vanish without the thing itself ceasing to exist.

The slowly vanishing qualities are called specific, for these are those that belong to one species to the exclusion of other species; the essential substantial properties are called **differences** <note>The technical term is "differentia"—the specific trait that distinguishes one species from another within the same broader group</note>, because they divide the genus and establish it as various species.

There are four types of so-called specific descriptions:

**a)** specific to one species, yet in such a way that another species also shares in it; for example, the specific description of a human. He is a creature with two feet. Birds also share in this trait.

**b)** specific to one species, so that no other species shares in it, yet this specific description is not found in all individuals of that species; for example, writing, trade, or craft. One finds these specifically within the species "human," but not in every human being.

**c)** the specific description which is found in all individuals of the species, yet not at all times; for example, gray hair. Among all creatures, this belongs only to humans, yet only toward the end of life.

**d)** the specific description which belongs to one species alone and is found in all individuals at all times; this is called "doubly specific." Thus, laughing and crying are found among all creatures only in humans; furthermore, all individuals possess these traits at all times, from birth until death. The same applies to the neighing of horses, the braying of donkeys, the barking of dogs, and so forth.

In short, every species of creature has a description that belongs to it specifically above all other species; the same applies

<summary>This page explains the logical distinction between accidental properties, which change frequently, and specific properties (propria), which define a species. It outlines four categories of specific descriptions, ranging from shared traits like bipedalism to unique, universal traits like human laughter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical classification of descriptions begun on page 25. It moves from defining species via "differences" to distinguishing individual members of a species through "accidents," eventually transitioning into a philosophical discussion on the nature of internal thought versus spoken language.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

of all existing things. Every kind has a peculiarity that separates it from other kinds. This is called a characteristic, whether it is known or not.

Through differences (distinctive descriptions), the **genera** <note>The plural of "genus," the broader category in logic</note> are divided and their **species** arise. Also, through both of these (genus and differences), the species are defined, as they are composed of both.

Through the characteristics—that is, the "doubly special" descriptions—the species are distinguished from one another and stand here in opposition to each other.

Through the specific descriptions—that is, the <term>accidents</term> <note>In logic, "accidents" are properties that are not essential to a thing's nature, such as hair color or height</note> of slow disappearance—the individuals of a species are distinguished from one another. For having blue or dark-brown eyes, being thick or thin, tall or short, and the like are descriptions through which individual people are marked out and by which one is distinguished from another.

Through the accidental descriptions—that is, the <term>accidents</term> of rapid disappearance—the states of individual persons are distinguished. Standing, sitting, anger, pleasure, and similar descriptions are not permanent, and their opposite can follow immediately.

### Internal Speech.

Internal speech <note>original: "Denkrede"; refers to the Greek concept of *logos endiathetos* or internal discourse</note> is the original (the root), and spoken speech is the derivative (the branches). Words are merely signs that lead to the meanings held within the thoughts of the soul. These words were established among people so that every person might express the meanings found within their soul during address and inquiry.

All things, we claim, are collectively forms of essence which the Creator allowed to emanate upon the creative intellect <note>original: "schaffende Vernunft"; likely referring to the Universal Intellect in Neo-Platonic philosophy</note>. This intellect is a simple substance that grasps the true nature of things; see "The Beginnings of Reason," XXXI.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of internal thought and its relationship to the cosmos, specifically focusing on the Neoplatonic theory of emanation. It transitions from the logic of language to the hierarchy of being—from the Creator to the World Soul and down to human individual souls.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

From the **Universal Intellect** <note>original: "Vernunft"; in this philosophical context, it refers to the first created entity in the Neoplatonic hierarchy of being</note> they emanated upon the heavenly **Universal Soul**, which is the very soul of the world; compare the concept of the "World as a Great Man" (the **Macrocosm**), Treatise XXXIII.

From the Universal Soul they emanated upon **Prime Matter** <note>original: "Urmaterie"; the fundamental, formless substance that underlies all physical reality</note>, the essence of which is presented in the treatise *Matter and Form*, XIV.

From Prime Matter they emanated upon the human **individual souls** <note>original: "Theilseelen"; literally "partial souls," referring to the idea that human souls are fragments or parts of the Universal Soul</note>, the origin of which we spoke of in the treatise *Man as a Microcosm* <note>original: "der Mensch eine kleine Welt"; the idea that the human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe</note>, XXV.

These forms are the "Known" <note>original: "das Gewusste"; the objects of knowledge or ideas</note> that man represents in his thoughts after he has perceived them in matter by means of the senses. Now, whoever wishes to recognize how the forms of things were in the Universal Soul before their outpouring <note>This "outpouring" refers to the process of emanation, where reality flows from a higher source to a lower one like water from a fountain</note> onto matter, he may take as an example the state of the established forms (archetypes) of man, as they existed in his soul before he brought them to light in his works using the materials provided to him; compare the treatise *On the Arts*, VIII.

Furthermore, whoever wishes to know how the forms of things were in the creative Intellect before they emanated upon the Universal Soul, and how the Soul received these characteristics and forms, let him take as an example the state of the characteristics of the "known" that were found in the soul of the teacher—how the emanation of these forms then took place upon the student, and how the student's acceptance of them followed; compare the treatise *On Instruction*.

Whoever then wishes to know what the state of what is known (by us) was like in the knowledge of the Sublime Creator before He allowed it to emanate upon the Intellect, let him take as an example the state of **Number**: how it existed within the **One**, which comes before the **Two**, and how Number proceeded from the One; compare *Arithmetic*, I.

**Science** is nothing other than the form of the "known" in the soul of the knower, and **Art** is nothing other than the production of this form from the soul of the knowing artist, combined with its placement into matter.

The souls of scholars are knowing in actuality <note>original: "in der That"; likely a translation of the Aristotelian "in actu" or Arabic "bi'l-fi'l," meaning a state of realized potential</note>, while the

The souls of learners are knowing in **potentiality** <note>original: "im Vermögen"; refers to the Aristotelian concept of *dynamis*, the latent capacity to possess knowledge before it is consciously realized</note>. Teaching is nothing other than the bringing forth of that which is in potentiality into **actuality** <note>original: "zur That"; refers to *energeia*, the state of being active or fully realized</note>; being taught is nothing other than the transition from potentiality to actuality. Every thing that exists in potentiality proceeds toward actuality because of something already existing in actuality that leads it forth.

The heavenly **Universal Soul** is knowing in actuality; the **individual soul** <note>original: "Theilseele"; literally "partial soul"</note> is closer to the Universal Soul the more it knows and the more wisely it creates, for it then stands in a closer relationship to it and greatly resembles it. Thus it is said in the definition of **philosophy**: it is a **becoming like God**, as far as this is possible <note>This is a famous Neoplatonic definition of philosophy—*homoiosis theō*—originating in Plato's *Theaetetus*</note>.

Therefore, one should strive to acquire much knowledge; then our creating will be wise and all our deeds will be pure. One acquires spiritual things just as the "children of this world" strive to acquire money—that is, physical things. For just as man acquires desired sensual pleasure and physical life through possession, so do souls grasp the pleasure of the "other world" through knowledge and draw near to God through knowledge. Also, through this same knowledge, one individual distinguishes himself above another.

Compare **Qur'an 39:9** <note>The text cites 39:12, but this refers to verse 9 in modern numbering</note>: "Are those who know and those who do not know equal? Only those of understanding take heed (of God)."

Through knowledge, souls are resurrected from the death of **folly** and awakened from the sleep of **carelessness**.

Compare **Qur'an 6:122**: "Is he who was dead and whom we then gave life, and for whom we set a light that he might walk with it among men, like him who bides in darkness and does not emerge from it?"

Knowledge leads us upon the path of the **Kingdom of Heaven** and supports those who ascend there.

Compare **Qur'an 35:10** <note>The text cites 35:11</note>: "To Him ascends the good word, and the righteous deed exalts it."

In **Qur'an 7:40** <note>The text cites 7:38</note> it is said of the "people of the camel" <note>The Thamudites; an ancient Arabian civilization cited in the Qur'an as a warning</note>: "Never shall the gates of the Kingdom of Heaven be opened to them, nor shall they enter Paradise, until the camel passes through the eye of the needle."

## Summary.

Meanings are, in their entirety, forms and characteristics within the thoughts of the **individual souls**; these souls grasp those forms from matter by means of the senses.

An **emanation** <note>In Neoplatonic thought, an emanation is a "flowing out" of divine reality from the source (the Creator) down through levels of existence.</note> occurs from the heavenly **Universal Soul** onto the forms in matter; an emanation from the **creative Intellect** occurs onto the forms of this Universal Soul; and onto the forms in this creative Intellect, there is an emanation from the **Creator**. —

Concerning words. Letters are individual sounds; if these follow one another, words arise; if the words contain meanings, they are names; if these follow one another, they become parts of speech; and well-ordered parts of speech are utterances.

Meanings resemble souls, while words resemble bodies. A word without sense is like a body without a soul, and a meaning in the thought of the soul without a word is exactly like a soul without a body.

Parts of speech, when joined together well, become utterances. These differ: a) on the part of the word, b) on the part of the meaning, c) from both together. — They are divided into five types:

a) Shared in word, different in meaning <note>These are homonyms. The German text uses the example of "Auge" (eye), mirroring the Arabic word *'ayn*, which can mean a physical eye, a spring of water, the sun, or gold.</note>; for example, "eye" encompasses various things: the eye of a human, the "eye" (spring) of water, of the sun, of gold, and the like.

b) Opposed to this are the **synonyms**, that is, different in word, shared in meaning; such as grain and wheat.

c) Distinct from one another, that is, different in word and meaning; such as wood and stone.

d) Opposed to this is the **coincident**, that is, coinciding in word and meaning; compare: "This is a person named Said, and this other person is also named Said."

The scholars claim: All things fall into two types—**Substance** and **Accident**. <term>Accident: In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that does not exist on its own but only within a substance, such as color, size, or position.</term> The latter are divided into nine categories, which are the states of substances and descriptions for them. <note>This refers to Aristotle’s Ten Categories: one substance plus nine types of accidents (quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and affection).</note>

However, the Creator is not described as being either an accident or a substance; rather, He is their Maker, their **efficient cause**. <term>Efficient cause: The agent or force that brings something into being.</term>

We further assert that all things are various forms and essences, ordered one beneath the other, just as numbers are ordered. The existence of one is attached to the other, just as number in general derives its existence from the **One**, which precedes the **Two**; see the *Treatise on Number, I*. <note>The Ikhwan al-Safa viewed mathematics as the bridge to spiritual knowledge; they believed the hierarchy of the universe mirrored the hierarchy of numbers emerging from unity.</note>

The Creator is the primary cause of all; it is He who grants them existence; see the *Treatise on Principles, XXXI*.

Form is divided into two types: a) **constitutive** <note>original: "herstellende" (producing/constitutive)</note> and b) **perfecting** <note>original: "vollendende"</note>. Scholars call the constitutive forms "substances" and the perfecting forms "accidents." See *Matter and Form, XIV* and *Coming-to-be and Passing-away, XVI*.

If man were capable of one person understanding the meanings and knowledge within the thoughts of another’s soul without the mediation of language, he would have no need for speech and pronunciation, which are, after all, merely audible sounds. For in hearing and understanding there lies a great effort for the souls, and likewise in verbal pronunciation, eloquence, and explanation. 

But since the soul of every human being is, as it were, submerged in the **body** <note>original: "Leib"</note> and covered with the darkness of the physical form, so that one soul sees of the other only the visible structure—that is, the body, which has length, breadth, and depth—it is impossible to know what knowledge each soul possesses. This remains hidden unless each person testifies to what is in their soul and makes it known to their peers. This can only be achieved through the preparation and tools of the tongue, the lips, the inhalation and exhalation of breath, and...

We must therefore understand spoken language well; one must allow oneself to be taught and must consider its requirements.

Pure, incorporeal souls do not require speech and utterances for one to take from another the knowledge and meanings that lie within their thoughts. For these heavenly souls are free from bodily desires. They have escaped the **sea of matter** <note>The "sea of matter" is a common Neoplatonic metaphor for the physical world, viewed as a chaotic and drowning environment for the soul.</note> and the bonds of nature; therefore, they do not need to dwell with dark bodies in the lowest regions and in the world of **becoming and passing away**. <term>Becoming and passing away: A philosophical term (Greek: genesis and phthora) referring to the physical world where things are born, change, and die, as opposed to the eternal, unchanging spiritual realm.</term> They ascend to the uppermost region of the high world and penetrate the luminous, transparent substances of the stars and spheres.

Since divine wisdom did not bind them to the bodies that veil them, they also have no need to shroud their secrets, nor to hide what is within them; they are free from everything impure and corrupt and have no need to keep anything bad secret. Rather, they unite with the light-substances and the transparent spheres, in which the part reveals itself in the whole and the whole in the part; just as the surfaces of two mirrors placed directly opposite each other show one another, or as in a crowd facing each other, the face of one is reflected in the eyes of the crowd, and the countenances of the crowd are reflected in the eyes of that one. <note>This mirror analogy illustrates the concept of "emanation" and "reflection," where spiritual beings share knowledge instantaneously without the distortion of words.</note> Thus, they neither need to make their secrets known to others, nor to inquire after theirs, as they stand in the highest light, the source of all that is good and pure. — So strive then, O brother, that your soul may become pure! It says in the **Koran** 59, 19: <note>Though the text cites 59:19, the quote actually corresponds to Surah 57:20.</note>

> 3, 15. <note>The original manuscript cites this as 3, 13, but it corresponds to Surah 3, verse 15 of the Quran.</note> Shall I tell you of something better than this for those who preserve themselves: With their Lord are gardens beneath which rivers flow; they shall dwell therein forever with pure companions and the pleasure of God. Indeed, God watches over His servants.

> 28, 83. We have reserved that other world for those who seek neither arrogance on earth nor corruption. The good end, however, is for those who are righteous.

May God guide you, oh reader, along with our brothers!

# The Categories.

The introduction dealt with the six words and their meaning. This section, by contrast, focuses on the ten words of the categories, and their meanings and properties shall be provided here.
Each of these words is a name for one of the genera of existing things, and it must be demonstrated how all meanings are encompassed by these ten words. <note>The "ten words" refer to Aristotle’s *Categories*, which were fundamental to medieval logic and philosophy.</note>
The sages observed visible things with their eyes; they bore witness to manifest things through their senses and reflected with their reason upon their inner meaning. They searched for what was hidden with their inner intuition and grasped the true essence of existing things through their power of discernment. It then became clear to them that all things are diverse beings, connected in existence like numbers, arranged and ordered one after another. Their existence and endurance proceed from a **Primal Ground**—that is, the glorious **Creator**—just as numbers are connected to one another and ordered one after another starting from One, which indeed comes before Two; cf. *Arithmetic* (I). <note>The author uses a Neoplatonic mathematical analogy: just as all numbers derive from the "One," all existence derives from the Creator.</note> As things appeared clearly to the sages in the manner mentioned, they called things of prior existence "**Matter**" and things of later existence "**Form**."
Furthermore, it became clear to them that forms are divided into two types: a) the constitutive, and b) the perfecting; cf. *On Coming-to-Be and Passing-Away* (XVI). <note>The German "herstellend" (constitutive/productive) and "vollendend" (perfecting) refer to the philosophical distinction between substantial forms that define an object's essence and accidental forms that provide its secondary characteristics.</note> They called the constitutive form **Substance** and the perfecting form **Accident**. Then it became clear that the same applies to all constitutive forms, and they were called

It establishes a hierarchy of existence where all things flow from a Primal Creator, categorized fundamentally into Matter and Form, and subsequently into Substance and Accident.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Aristotelian logic, specifically focusing on the classification of existence. The previous page ended while describing the two types of forms: constitutive and perfecting. This page explains how these forms and substances relate to the ten categories, using mathematical analogies and biological classifications familiar to medieval scholars.</meta>

...therefore, all substances form a single genus. It also became clear that the perfecting forms behave differently; it was said that the **accidents** <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that does not belong to the essence of a thing—for example, the color of a shirt is an accident, while being a garment is part of its essence.</note> belong to different genera—specifically, they form nine genera, corresponding to the nine units. **Substance** is among existing things what the number One is among numbers; accidents, by contrast, are like the nine units that follow the number One.

Consequently, all existing things consist of ten types, corresponding to the ten units. One is always subordinated to the other, just as numbers are ordered, which in their existence are connected to the One that comes before the Two.

The ten words, which encompass the meaning of everything in existence, are: **Substance**, **How much** (Quantity), **How** (Quality), **Relationship** (Relation), **Where** (Place), **When** (Time), **Position**, **Having**, **Acting**, and **Being Acted Upon** <note>original: "Leiden" (Suffering/Passivity). In this context, it refers to the category of 'Passion,' or receiving an action from something else.</note>.

Each of these words is a name for a genus of existing things. Every genus is divided into a number of species, and every species again into other species (sub-species), until the division reaches the level of individuals. The sages contemplated what exists. The first things they recognized were individuals, such as Said, Amr, and Khalid <note>These are traditional names used as placeholders in Arabic logical and grammatical texts, much like "Alice and Bob" or "John Doe" are used today.</note>. Then they considered whether the essence of these individuals might not apply to all humans—those present or those absent—and they realized that all humans are encompassed by the "human form," even if they differ from one another in characteristics such as height or shortness, blackness or whiteness, redness or brownness, or in slowness or agility and the like. Therefore, they called them all **Human** and called "human" a species; for it is a collective of individuals who agree in form but differ in accidents. Then they saw other individuals, like Said's donkey, Amr's she-donkey, and Khalid's stone-donkey, and saw that the "donkey form" encompassed them all; they then called these together a species. Then they noticed Said's mare, Amr's stallion, and Khalid's foal; they saw that the "horse form" encompassed them all and called them another species. Following this analogy, they considered the other animals, livestock,

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of existence within the framework of Aristotelian logic. While the previous page introduced the ten categories (substance and the nine accidents), this page explains the hierarchical relationship between "genus" and "species," moving from specific animals up to the primary category of Substance.</meta>

Beast of prey, bird, water-animal, and land-animal. Each collective group of these was encompassed by a form, and therefore, they were called a **species** <note>original: "Art". In logic, a species is a narrow classification of things that share the same essential characteristics.</note>.

When one then reflected upon all of them together, it was recognized that life encompasses them all, and they were collectively called **living things**. "Living" is then a **genus** <note>original: "Gattung". A genus is a broader category that contains multiple species.</note> that encompasses a multitude of different forms, and these forms are then species for this genus.

After this, other individuals (examples) were observed—the tree, the plant, and their species; it was seen that "growth" enclosed them all, and they were called **growing things**. "Growing" is thus a genus with the two species "Animal" and "Plant."

Then other things were noticed, such as stones and other solid objects <note>original: "concreta" (concrete/solid things).</note>. It was seen that all those mentioned so far are **bodies**, and thus this was called a genus. It was known that the body as such has neither movement nor reason, neither senses nor knowledge. But then it was found that bodies did move, adopted reason, and that shapes, forms, outlines, and colors were created upon them; thus, it was recognized that a spiritual substance was also connected to the body. All of this was summarized in one word, namely **Substance**, and so Substance became a genus, and "spiritual" and "corporeal" became two species of it. "Corporeal" then became a genus for what stood beneath it—that is, for "growing" and "solid"—and these two are species for the former. "Growing" is then again a genus for what stands beneath it, for "creature" and "plant," and these two are its species. "Creature" becomes a genus for what stands beneath it, such as human, beast, poultry, dwellers of the air, swimmers, dwellers of the water, runners, dwellers of the mainland, crawlers, dwellers of the dust, and angels, dwellers of the heavens. All of these are species of the "creature," and "creature" is the genus for them. "Human" is one of the species, and "Substance" is one of the genera. "Body," "growing," and "creature" are either species or genus depending on the relationship. In relation to what stands below them, they are called a genus; and in relation to what stands above them, they are called <note>The text cuts off here; logically, the missing word is "species" (Arten).</note>.

**Species**, **Genus**, **Individuals**, **Growing things**, **Body**, **Substance**, **Creature**, **Human**, **Angel**

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Aristotelian logic, specifically the "Ten Categories." The previous page established "Substance" as the primary genus; this page defines Substance's characteristics and introduces the subsequent categories of "Accidents"—the attributes that describe a substance, such as its size, color, or relationships.</meta>

...species of it. So much regarding the first of the ten words, "Substance," and its division into species and individuals.

Substance has no formal definition, yet its distinguishing characteristic is this: Substance exists by itself, it is independent, and it is capable of receiving opposing accidents <note>original: "Accidensen." In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature—for example, a chair is still a chair whether it is painted red or blue.</note>.

When people observed bodies and described them as being three cubits <note>original: "Ellen" (ells/cubits). An ancient unit of length based on the forearm.</note> long, four pounds in weight, or containing five measures, they grouped all these together and called them the genus of "How Much" (Quantity). However, these are all merely accidents of the body. Then, other things were found that were not the body itself and were not answered by the question "how much?"; such as blackness, whiteness, sweetness, bitterness, scent, and the like. These were grouped together and called the genus of "How" (Quality). These accidents are part of the description of the body; the body is defined by them and they exist within it, serving as perfecting forms for the body (see *On Coming-to-Be and Passing-Away* <note>original: "Entstehen und Vergehen." This refers to Aristotle’s work *De Generatione et Corruptione*.</note>, Book XVI).

Then, various names were found that applied to a thing, yet in a way that the thing’s essence did not change. This happened only because of its relationship to various other things. This was called the genus of the "Related" (Relation). For example, a man might be called father, son, brother, enemy, comrade, and so on. These names only appear when there are two things, where one corresponds to the other in a certain sense. These meanings, however, do not exist within the essence of either person, but only in the mind <note>original: "Seele" (soul/mind).</note> of the observer. Both were called the genus of the Related, and those who study properties call these "states."

Furthermore, other names with different meanings were found, such as "above," "below," "there," "here," and similar terms. These were grouped together and called the genus of "Where" (Place).

Then, still other names with different meanings were found, such as "day," "month," "year," "time," "duration," and the like; these were grouped together and called the genus of "When" (Time).

<meta>This page concludes the enumeration of Aristotle's "Ten Categories," specifically detailing the final categories of Position, Possession, Action, and Passion. It then provides a practical example using the individual "Said" to show how all ten categories can apply to one person, before transitioning into the methodology of logic.</meta>

Then other names were found with still other meanings, such as standing, sitting, bowing, bending, or leaning. These were grouped together and called the genus of "Positioning"—that is, "Posture" <note>original: "Stellen," "Setzen." This refers to the category of *Situs*.</note>.

Then other names were found, such as for him, on him, from him, upon him, with him, and the like. These were grouped together and called the genus of "Possession"—"Having" <note>original: "Besitz," "Haben." This refers to the category of *Habitus*.</note>.

Then still other names were found, such as hitting, laying, doing, and similar words that express an effect of an actor upon a sufferer; these were grouped together and called the genus of "he acts" (Doing) <note>original: "Thun." This refers to the category of *Action*.</note>.

Finally, other names were found, such as being cut, being broken, being afflicted, and the like. These were grouped together and called the genus of "he suffers" (Suffering) <note>original: "Leiden." This refers to the category of *Passion* or *Undergoing*.</note>.

Thereupon, all things were observed, and no meaning was found outside of those mentioned here. Thus, the meanings are <gloss>in</gloss> no more than ten words, just as the steps of the ones <note>The decimal system of digits 0 through 9.</note> are expressed in ten words.

The ten genera <note>Commonly known as the "Categories" in Aristotelian philosophy.</note> encompass everything in existence, both substances and accidents <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are properties that are not essential to a thing's nature, like color or position.</note>, everything that will be, that was, and that is. No one can conceive of anything that lies outside these genera or the species and individuals they contain.

Often, all these meanings can be found in a single individual. Take Said <note>Said is a common placeholder name used in medieval Arabic logic texts, similar to "Socrates" in Greek texts.</note>, for example: he is a substance; in him is the **How Much** (Quantity), for he is tall; in him is the **How** (Quality), for he is black; in him is the **Relationship**, for he is a son; also the **Where**, for he is in a place; furthermore the **When**, for he is at a certain time; also the **Position** (the posture) is in him, for he is standing or sitting. In him is also **Having** (Possession), for he has property; likewise **Doing**, when he strikes, and **Suffering**, when he is struck.

## The Division of Genera into Species.

There are four paths of instruction: a) the path of **definition** (**delimitation**), b) the path of **proof**, c) the path of **analysis** (**resolution**), d) the path of **division** (**partition**).

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Aristotelian logic and metaphysics, moving from the general definition of categories to a detailed hierarchical tree of Substance and Quantity. It organizes the world into a series of nested types, from the celestial realms down to individual living things and the properties of space and time.</meta>

The latter is as follows.

I. **Substance** is divided into two types: **Corporeal** and **Spiritual**.
The **Corporeal** is divided again into two types: **Celestial** and **Natural**.
The **Natural** is divided into two types: **Composite** and **Simple**.
The **Simple** is divided into four types: **Fire**, **Air**, **Water**, and **Earth** <note>These are the four classical elements that were believed to make up all matter.</note>.
The **Composite** is divided into two types: **Inanimate** and **Growing**. The **Inanimate** <note>original: "Concret," here referring to solid, non-living matter.</note> are the **minerals** <note>original: "Minenkörper," literally "bodies of the mine," referring to ores and stones.</note>.
**Growing** is divided into two types: **Plant** and **Living Creature**.
The **Plants** are divided into three types: **Planted** (**Trees**), **Sown** (such as grain), and those that sprout of their own accord (such as herbs and fodder).
The **Living Creature** is divided into two types: **Rational** and speaking (that is, man) and **Irrational** (the other creatures).
These are divided into three types: a) those born from the womb, b) those from an egg, c) those that arise (spontaneously) through putrefaction <note>Historical science often assumed that certain insects or small creatures were "spontaneously generated" from decaying matter.</note>. Among each of these types, there are sub-types until one arrives at the individual.

The spiritual substances are divided into two types: **Matter** (**Primal Matter**) <note>original: "Urmaterie." In this philosophical context, this refers to the underlying "stuff" of the universe before it is given a specific form.</note> and **Form**.
The **Form** is divided into two types: a) separable, such as reason and soul, b) inseparable, such as shape and color.

II. **Quantity** <note>original: "Das Wieviel," literally "The How-Much," translating the Aristotelian category of *Quantitas*.</note> is divided into two types: **continuous** and **discrete** (**separable**).
**Continuous** quantities consist of five types: **Line**, **Surface**, **Body** <note>Referring here to three-dimensional geometrical solids.</note>, **Time**, and **Place**.
The **discrete**, however, are of two types: **Number** and **Movement**.
The **Line** is divided into three types: **straight**, **bent**, and **curved**.
The **Surface** likewise has three types: **flat**, **convex** <note>original: "gewölbt," meaning arched or vaulted.</note>, and **concave** <note>original: "gesenkt," meaning lowered or depressed.</note>.
The division of bodies was dealt with above (see **Geometry**, **II.**).
**Place** is divided into seven types: **Above**, **Below**, **In Front**, **Behind**, **Right**, **Left**, and the **Center**.
**Time** has three types: **Past**, **Present**, and **Future**, and each of these is divided into four parts: **Year**, **Day**, **Month**, and **Hour**.

It also details the category of Quantity, breaking down the concepts of geometry, space, and time into their constituent parts.</summary>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of Aristotelian categories, following the previous discussion on Substance. Having defined the types of corporeal and spiritual substances, the text now transitions into the categories of Quantity (Number), Motion, Quality (the "How"), and Relation.</meta>

Number has two types: Even and Odd, and each of these is divided into ones, tens, hundreds, and thousands; they also branch off into: a) Whole numbers, b) Fractions.

Motion has six types: Coming-to-be and passing-away, increase and decrease, alteration, and change of place (locomotion) <note>These six types of motion are drawn directly from Aristotle's *Categories* and *Physics*.</note>. The sub-type of this genus is uniform and non-uniform.

**III.** The "How" <note>This refers to the category of Quality (Latin: *Qualitas*).</note> is divided into two types: physical and spiritual. The physical "How" is that which is grasped through the senses. The spiritual "How" is that which is grasped through reason, such as knowledge, intention, courage, and faith.

The physical "How" is divided into two types: simple (existing for itself) and composite.

Simple, existing for itself, is divided into two types: a) active, such as heat and cold; b) passive, such as dryness and moisture.

Composite is divided into two types: a) permanent traits, such as tastes, colors, scents, snub-nosedness, and similar qualities; b) temporary states, such as standing, sitting, turning yellow with fear, or red with shame.

The spiritual "How" is divided into four types: knowledge, aptitude, opinion, and mode of action. The sub-types of these are the similar and the dissimilar.

**IV.** Relation is divided into two types: mutually equal and unequal. Equal relations are those in which both descriptions involved in the relation share the same name, such as brother, neighbor, or friend <note>For example, if person A is a brother to person B, then person B is also a brother to person A; the relationship is symmetrical.</note>. Unequal relations are those where the two related terms have different names, such as father and son, master and servant, cause and effect, half and double, or larger and smaller. These all stand in relation, but their natures exist in two ways: namely, either one existed before the other (such as father and son, or cause and effect), or both existed prior to the relation itself (such as servant and master, neighbor, or friend).

The genus of the relative, when placed in relation to its counterpart, enters into the class of substance—specifically in regards to accidents <term>accident: a philosophical term for a property or characteristic that a thing possesses (like being "red" or "sitting") which is not essential to what the thing actually is (its "substance")</term>, but not according to its essence. This is because the substance is described through its accidents; the accidents, therefore, serve as descriptions for the substance.

<summary>This page outlines the categorical classifications of Number, Motion, Quality (the "How"), and Relation, organizing them into hierarchical sub-types. It distinguishes between physical qualities perceived by senses and spiritual qualities perceived by reason, while also defining the nature of symmetrical and asymmetrical relations.</summary>
<keywords>categories, quantity, motion, quality, relation, accident, substance, Aristotelian logic, classification of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the Aristotelian categories. The previous page ended with the definition of equal relations (like "brother"). This page concludes the discussion on the "simple" categories (Substance, Quantity, Quality, and Relation) and introduces the six "composite" categories that describe how a substance interacts with its environment and time.</meta>

Description, however, is a description for something described—specifically, for something that is capable of being described by that description, just as a father is the father of the son and a son is the son of the father.

A sub-species of this genus is when, of two things standing in relation, one is indeed directed toward the other, yet they do not negate each other; instead, both stand in relation to a third thing (compare the relative terms: brother, sister—father). These four genera of the categories <note>Substance, Quantity, Quality, and Relation.</note> are called "simple," while the other six are called "composite," namely:

a) **Where**, that is, the composition of a substance with a place. There are seven types of place; compare the section above regarding "How much" <note>This refers back to the category of Quantity/Magnitude.</note>.

b) **When**, that is, the composition of a substance with time. We spoke of the types of time under "How much."

c) **Position** <term>Position (Situs)</term> is the composition of one substance with another, such as that which is supported and that which supports it, or that which leans and that against which it leans.

d) **Possession** <term>Possession (Habitus)</term> is the composition of one substance with another. Possession is divided into two types: Internal and External.
The **Internal** is either in the soul, such as knowledge, reason, and goodness; or in the body, such as beauty, grace, and radiance.
The **External** is divided again into two types: living things and inanimate things (growing things and solid objects); for example, a servant, a beast of burden, a dirham <note>original: "Dirham"; an Arabic silver coin.</note>, a land estate, or a dwelling.

e) **Action** is divided into two types: 
1. That in which the trace of the actor remains in the work, such as writing, a building, and the like.
2. That in which the trace does not remain, such as singing.

f) **Passion** <note>In this context, "Passion" (from the Greek *paschein*) does not mean emotion, but the state of "being acted upon" or "suffering" an effect.</note> is divided into two types: 
1. In bodies, such as the practical arts (praxis).
2. In the souls, such as the theoretical arts (theory).

### Relation.

When one thing enters into a relationship with another, this can occur only in speech or in essence. That which exists in speech is affirmation or...

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the ten categories into the logical relationships between concepts, specifically focusing on how descriptions are applied or denied, and how opposites interact. It follows the previous page's classification of "simple" and "composite" categories by explaining the formal nature of their relationships.</meta>

Negation. Affirmation is the establishment of a description for something described. Negation is the removal of a description from something described. Within these two relationships, truth and falsehood are possible. The relationship existing in the essence of things is of three kinds: a) they are opposites of one another, b) they are in relation to one another, c) they exist in Being and Non-being. <note>In Aristotelian logic, these correspond to Contraries, Relatives, and Privation/Possession.</note>

a) Two things are **opposites** of one another when one negates its counterpart and does not include (excludes) it. They are divided into those that have an intermediate <note>original: "Mittleres"</note> and those which have no intermediate; for example, Blackness and Whiteness are both opposites, yet intermediate colors lie between them: Red, Yellow, Green, and others. Likewise, Sweet and Bitter are opposites, yet between them lie: Sour, Salty, Sub-sweet, and other tastes.

Those without an intermediate are, for example, Health and Sickness. <note>This implies a binary state where one is either healthy or ill, with no "middle" state defined in this philosophical system.</note>

Among the characteristics of this class is that if one of them attaches to the body—that is, is characteristic of the body—then the other is also found in the body. If, on the other hand, one of them attaches to the soul, then the other is also characteristic of the soul.

Furthermore, if one is perceived through a sense, the other is also perceived through the same sense. Blackness, for example, exists only in the body and is perceived only through sight; it is the same with White. Knowledge, furthermore, is perceived only through the reason <term>Reason (Vernunft)</term> and exists only in the soul. The same applies to ignorance.

b) **Relatives** stand in relation to one another; however, they do not negate each other, but rather one includes the other within itself. <note>For example, you cannot have the concept of a "teacher" without the implied existence of a "student."</note>

c) **Being and Non-being** is similar to the previous two, the opposites and relatives. Non-being <note>Often referred to in philosophy as "privation," the absence of a natural quality.</note> does indeed stand in relation to Being, yet Being does not stand in relation to Non-being. We speak of the "blindness of the sighted," but not of the "sight of the blind." <note>This suggests that blindness is only a meaningful concept in relation to a creature that possesses the capacity for sight.</note>

Furthermore, Being and Non-being never occur at the same time, just as opposites exclude one another.

<vocab>Negation, Affirmation, Opposites, Relatives, Being, Non-being, Intermediate, Reason, Soul, Health, Sickness</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the logical relationships between subjects and their descriptions, categorizing how things can be opposites, relatives, or exist in states of being and privation. It emphasizes that certain qualities, like health or color, belong to specific domains like the body or the soul and are perceived through corresponding faculties.</summary>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of logical relationships and categories. Following the previous discussion on opposites (contraries) and negation, the author now examines the states of Being and Non-being, and the various ways things can be prior to, inside, or alongside one another.</meta>

If <term>Being</term> <note>Sein; the state of existing</note> is corporeal, then <term>Non-being</term> <note>Nichtsein; the state of not existing</note> is also corporeal; if it is spiritual, then Non-being is also spiritual. One speaks of Being in relation to the non-existent only when the time of its <term>existence</term> arrives; just as one only calls a child a child when the time has come for it to emerge as a human being. No action is grasped except at the time when it actually occurs.

### **Priority.**

Of things, one precedes another. This <term>priority</term> <note>Vorsein; the state of being "before" something else</note> occurs in five ways:
a) In time and in place; for example, Moses was before Jesus.
b) In nature; for example, the animal preceded the human—stone, plant, animal, human. <note>This describes a hierarchy of nature where simpler forms are seen as foundational to more complex ones.</note>
c) In dignity; for example, the sun precedes the moon.*)
d) In numerical rank; for example, five stands before six.
e) In essence, such as <term>cause</term> and <term>effect</term>.

### **In-being.**

One says of a thing that it is "at" or "in" another in various ways: a) in place, b) in time, c) in a vessel; furthermore: the <term>accident</term> <note>Accidens; a property that is not essential to a thing's nature, such as its color</note> is in the <term>substance</term> <note>Substanz; the essential nature of a thing</note> and the substance is in the accident, the <term>individual</term> is in the <term>species</term> and in the <term>genus</term> and vice versa. The leader is with the led, and the led with the leader. The thing is in its completion, in its parts, in the whole, and the like.

### **Simultaneity.**

One says a thing is "with" another in three ways:
a) In time; for example, "I come with the first ray of morning."
b) In relation, as with two relatives; for example, as mentioned above.
c) Among <term>species</term> <note>Art</note> that stand together under one <term>genus</term> <note>Gattung</note>.

---

*) I supplement here with the phrase: "in glory" <note>original Arabic: "bil-maǧdi"</note>—meaning the sun is more excellent than the moon.

<summary>This page defines the philosophical concepts of priority, inherence (In-being), and simultaneity, categorizing the different ways objects and concepts relate to one another in time, space, and hierarchy.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract logic of "In-being" and "Priority" to a more metaphorical and pedagogical explanation of the Ten Categories. The author uses the imagery of a garden to illustrate how universal concepts branch out into specific realities.</meta>

The Ten Words <note>original: "die zehn Worte"; a reference to the ten Aristotelian categories (Decem Categoriae) which classify all possible modes of being</note> and the meanings they encompass—that is, the ten <term>categories</term> <note>Gattungen; also known as genera or classes of being</note> which include the meaning of all things—resemble, with their species and their individual parts, a garden with ten trees. Each tree has a number of branches and limbs, and on every limb grows a number of shoots. These each have a mass of leaves, and among them are blossoms and fruits. Every fruit has its own taste, color, and scent, which differ from those of every other.

The discerning soul knows the meanings of these ten categories; she visualizes them in their essence, observes their manifold transformations, and recognizes their far-reaching forms with their various qualities and vibrant colors. She is like the owner of the garden who, when opening its door, gazes upon the colors and flowers and breathes in their fragrance. He tastes the flavors and rejoices in the fruits of his garden. Therefore, strive for <term>knowledge</term> <note>Wissenschaft; in this context, systematic or philosophical learning</note> and <term>cultivation</term> <note>Bildung; the refinement of the mind and character through education</note>, for the sciences are the orchards of the soul, and their various meanings are like the various fruits. Knowledge is the nourishment of the soul just as food is the nourishment of the body; in them lies the life, the pleasure, and the joy of the soul once it has eventually separated from the body.

---

<meta>This page continues the logical progression from the previous discussion of the Ten Categories. # On Interpretation <note>original: "Hermeneutica"; derived from the Greek *Peri hermeneias*, the title of Aristotle’s second logical work which deals with sentences and propositions</note>

---

In the earlier treatises, the following have been discussed:
a) The ten <term>categories</term> <note>the basic classes of all things that exist, such as substance, quality, and quantity</note>, each of which encompasses a genus of meanings—that is, the forms abstracted from matter—which then impressed themselves as signs and images upon the thoughts of the human soul.
b) The six words used by philosophers are discussed in the <term>Isagoge</term> <note>original: "Isagoge"; Greek for "introduction," referring to Porphyry's famous introduction to Aristotle's categories</note>. It was shown there that individual letters, when joined together, form words; but words, when they contain a meaning, become names <note>or nouns</note>, which, when correctly followed by one another, result in meaningful speech—a sentence.
We now continue here with the fact that the sentence is divided into three types or parts <note>This refers to the parts of speech</note>:
a) Names which designate essences; logicians and grammarians call these <term>nouns</term> <note>original: "Nomina"</note>.
b) Names which designate the effects of these essences, of one upon another. These the learned grammarians [call verbs]...

---

<note>original: "*)"</note> The twelfth treatise is titled *Baraminias* or *Baramjas*, which is a misunderstanding of the Greek title *Peri hermeneias* <note>meaning "On Interpretation"</note>. It is called the third logical treatise.
<note>original: "**)"</note> In Arabic grammar, the following classification prevails:
a) If a word provides a meaning in itself without connection to the concept of time, it is a noun <note>Arabic: *ism*</note>.
b) If it provides a meaning in connection with the concept of time, it is a verb <note>Arabic: *fi'l*</note>.
c) If it provides no meaning in itself, but only in combination with another word, it is a particle <note>Arabic: *harf*</note>; compare the grammarian Ibn Akil, edited by Dieterici, page 3.

It categorizes language into nouns and verbs, while providing a linguistic comparison between Western logical traditions and Arabic grammatical structures.</summary>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the parts of speech, distinguishing how grammarians and logicians name them. It moves from individual words to the construction of propositions, specifically distinguishing between sentences that can be proven true or false (judgments) and those that cannot (such as commands or wishes).</meta>

[matti]cians call them action (**verbs** <note>original: "Verba"</note>), but logicians call them parts of speech (**words** <note>original: "kalimāt"</note>) or (**verbs** <note>original: "Zeitwort"</note>).

c) **Names** which indicate meanings that serve only as **tools** for the speaker to connect nouns with verbs. Grammarians call these **particles** (**letters** <note>original: "ḥarf"; in Arabic grammar, this term refers to particles, prepositions, and conjunctions</note>), while logicians call them **linkages** (**auxiliary words** <note>original: "Hülfswort"</note>).

a) **Nouns** <note>original: "Nomina"</note> are all words that indicate a meaning without a determination of time; compare Said, Amr <note>common Arabic names used as examples, like 'John' and 'James'</note>, stone, wood, and the like.

b) The **Verb** <note>original: "Verbum/Zeitwort"</note> is a word that indicates the meaning of something newly entering into time; compare: "he has struck," "he strikes."

c) **Particles** <note>original: "Partikeln"</note> are words such as: from, upon, in, and similar words dealt with in grammar.

In short, he who wishes to pursue philosophical logic should first practice grammar.

Verbs and nouns, when they are well-ordered, become **propositions** <note>original: "Aussprüche"</note>\*). These propositions, however, fall into two **types**:

a) Those in which truth and falsehood can take place.

b) Those in which truth and falsehood cannot take place.

These latter ones fall into four types: command, invocation, question, and wish; compare: "do this!", "O you there!", "where do you come from?", "if only God would forgive us!"

The propositions in which truth and falsehood can take place are called **assertions** (**judgments**). They fall into two **types**:

a) Stating the necessity of a description for a thing described (affirming).

b) Indicating the removal of such a description from it (negating); compare: "fire is a hot thing" (is hot), "fire is not cold." The statement "fire is a hot thing" is an affirmation, and the statement "fire is not cold" is a negation.

---

\*) Here the Arab author evidently follows the Greek model, as in the Arabic language the simple joining of nouns already forms sentences <note>The author is noting that while Greek (and German) logic usually requires a verb or copula to make a sentence, Arabic can create a complete thought just by putting two nouns together</note>.

<summary>This page defines the three parts of speech—nouns, verbs, and particles—from the perspectives of both grammar and logic, and introduces the concept of the "judgment" as a statement capable of being true or false.</summary>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of propositions where truth and falsehood occur. The previous page ended with examples of speech acts that cannot be proven true or false, such as commands or questions. This page transitions into the logical analysis of categorical and conditional statements, specifically focusing on how "quantifiers" (boundaries) define the clarity of a statement's truth value.</meta>

**Affirmation** is either true or false; the same applies to **negation**. "Fire is hot" is a truth; "fire is cold" is a lie. "Fire is not hot" is a lie; "fire is not cold" is a truth. Thus, negation and affirmation can at one time be truth and at another time a lie.

Affirmation and negation can sometimes form a **categorical judgment** <note>original: "geschlossenes Urtheil"</note>, but at other times a **conditional statement** with a subsequent clause. A full affirmation is, for example: "The sun over the earth is day." A condition would be: "If the sun is over the earth, then it is day"; and likewise for the negation: "The sun not over the earth is not day," or: "If the sun is not over the earth, then it is not day."

The categorical judgment is divided into two types: in one, truth and falsehood are obvious and clear within it, but in the other, they are hidden. Namely, if the statement of a speaker allows for an **interpretation** <note>original: "Ausdeutung"; in logic, this refers to a statement that is ambiguous or requires further context to determine its truth</note>, then truth and falsehood are not clear in it; however, if it allows for no interpretation, truth and falsehood are clear in it.

A statement that does not allow for interpretation can be **bounded** <note>original: "umgrenzt"; this refers to "quantified" statements in logic</note>; these are statements which are surrounded by a **limit** <term>boundary/quantifier <gloss>Schranke</gloss>; the logical term for words like "all," "none," or "some" that define the scope of a subject</term>. The boundaries of statements fall into two types: a) universal, b) partial.

a) A **universal boundary** <note>a universal quantifier</note> is, for example: "Every man is an animal"; this is a distinct, clear truth, for it has a universal boundary. A distinct, clear lie would be: "Not even one man is an animal"; that is a distinct, clear lie, for it has a universal boundary.

b) **Partial boundaries** <note>particular quantifiers</note> are such statements as: "Some men are scribes" and "some men are not scribes." The truth and falsehood in each of them is clear and distinct, for they both have a partial boundary.

The **unbounded** statements—that is, those without a boundary—fall into two types: **vague** (imprecise) and **specific**.

Vague statements are those such as: "man is a scribe" (one who can write) and "man is no scribe," for in

It introduces the concept of quantifiers—referred to here as "boundaries"—which categorize statements as universal or partial to determine their truth or falsehood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of propositions. The previous page introduced "boundaries" (quantifiers) as the necessary components to determine if a statement is clearly true or false. This page explores specific (singular) judgments and the conditions required for the Law of Contradiction to hold.</meta>

In both, truth and falsehood are not clear; it is possible for it to be affirmative (correct) for some and negative (correct) for some others.

**Specific judgments** <note>original: "Specielle Urtheile"; in this context, these are singular propositions referring to specific individuals rather than universal classes</note> are those such as "Said is a scribe" and "Said is not a scribe"; in these as well, truth and falsehood are not clear, for it is possibly correct for a Said from Kufa and false for a Said from Basra.

But if there is a **boundary** <term>boundary <gloss>Schranke</gloss>; the logical term for a quantifier, such as "all," "none," or "some," which defines the scope of a subject</term> around every statement, as we indicated, then truth and falsehood are clear, for the negation can only intend to negate exactly what the affirmation asserts.

The listener must necessarily grasp what the speaker's word asserts and intends in order thereby to reach what is within the speaker’s heart; for the interior is known to God alone.

It is now clear through the examples that if speech is not enclosed by boundaries, truth and falsehood do not clearly emerge from it. The boundaries first add the descriptions to the subject being described, and these subjects must be marked with known and recognized signs; for if the subject is not known by its name, then truth and falsehood will never emerge in the statement. To this category belong statements such as: "A non-human is an animal," and "Non-Said is a scribe," and "The non-animal is a dead substance," and similar phrases which are not signs for known beings, but rather belong collectively to every thing except that which is specifically excluded by them.

**Negation** and **affirmation** are two judgments which contradict one another in word and meaning. They cannot both be true or false at the same time, in the same manner, and in the same relationship regarding a single property of a described subject. <note>This is a classic formulation of the Principle of Non-Contradiction.</note> However, if one of these mentioned conditions is missing, both can coincide in truth and falsehood. For example: "Some humans are scribes" and "some are not scribes." Small children are indeed scribes **potentially** <note>original: "dem Vermögen nach"; an Aristotelian concept referring to a latent capacity</note>, but not in **actuality** <note>original: "in der That"; referring to the current exercise of a skill</note>. Of the same man one can say: he is knowledgeable and he is not knowledgeable; that is, he is...

<summary>This page explains that logical truth and falsehood only become clear when a statement is "bounded" by quantifiers or specific names. It also defines the Principle of Non-Contradiction, noting that opposites can only coexist if the context, time, or potentiality of the subject changes.</summary>

<keywords>logic, truth and falsehood, quantifiers, Said, Kufa, Basra, negation, affirmation, potentiality vs actuality, principle of non-contradiction</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of propositions, moving from the necessity of "boundaries" (quantifiers) to the classification of judgments based on their complexity (the number of elements involved) and the nature of contradictions between universal and particular statements.</meta>

...knowledgeable of one thing, but not of another. Furthermore: He is fasting and not fasting—that is, he fasts during the month of Ramadan, but during the day and not during the night. Or: He is large in relation to what is smaller than him, but he is not large in relation to what is larger than him.

If one attributes a description to a subject being described, this judgment is called a **two-part judgment**; such as: "Said is a scribe." If one connects a time to the judgment, it is called a **three-part judgment**; compare: "Said wrote yesterday," "he will write tomorrow," "he writes today." If one adds one of the three elements—namely: **Possible**, **Necessary**, or **Impossible**—to a three-part judgment, it is called a **four-part judgment**; for example: "It is possible that this youth will one day become a strong man," or: "It is impossible that he will one day carry a thousand pounds," or: "It is necessary that he will one day die."

---

Negation and affirmation are divided into two types: universal and particular. <note>original: "gänzliche, theilweise"</note>

The **universally affirmative** is, for example: "Every fire is hot." Its negation would be: "No fire in any way is hot." If these two stand opposite one another, this is called a **Major Contrast**. <term>Major Contrast <gloss>Gross-Gegensatz</gloss>; in traditional logic, this refers to the relationship between universal statements that cannot both be true.</term>

The **particularly affirmative**: "Some people are scribes." Its negation: "Some people are not scribes." If one places these two opposite each other, it is called a **Minor Contrast**. <term>Minor Contrast <gloss>Klein-Gegensatz</gloss>; likely referring to sub-contraries, where both statements can be true at the same time.</term>

If two affirmative or two negative judgments stand opposite one another, they are called **mutually negating** (canceling); compare: "Some humans are animals" and "All humans are animals"; or "Some humans are not birds" and "No human is a bird."

Two **mutually attached judgments** are those which agree in meaning but differ in expression; compare: "All fire is hot" and "No fire is cold"; or "Some people are scribes" and "Almost all people are idiots." <note>The term "idiots" here likely refers to the "unlearned" or "laypeople" in a technical logical context, rather than a modern slur.</note>

---

The description is called the **related** (carried), while the *described* is the **established** (set). <note>These terms correspond to the Arabic logical concepts of the predicate (maḥmūl, "that which is carried") and the subject (mawḍūʿ, "that which is placed").</note>

**Vocabulary:** Judgment, Two-part judgment, Three-part judgment, Four-part judgment, Negation, Affirmation, Major Contrast, Minor Contrast, Ramadan, Said, Logic

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of logical judgments, moving from the definitions of contrast to how subjects and predicates are combined. It also establishes the hierarchical relationship between the necessary, the possible, and the impossible, and introduces the concept of converting logical propositions.</meta>

If there are many subjects, yet only one description, then there are many judgments; compare: "Said is a scribe," "Amr is a scribe," "Bakr is a scribe."

If there are many descriptions but the subject being described is only one, then there are likewise many judgments; for example: "Said is a scribe," "Said is a blacksmith," "Said is a carpenter," "Said is a tailor." — If the descriptions are many according to the wording, but their meaning is only one, then the judgment is only one: "Said is sensible, discerning, knowledgeable, and clever."

Judgments differ, firstly in negation and affirmation—that is, the difference according to **quality**; at other times they differ in the part and the whole—that is, the difference according to **quantity**.

If two judgments differ in both "how much" (quantity) and "how" (quality), they are called mutually **contradictory**. If they differ only in "how much," they are called **opposite** to one another. Contradictory judgments are stronger in their contrast than opposite ones; compare: contradictory: "Every person is a scribe" and "No person is a scribe," opposite: "Every person is a scribe" and "Not every person is a scribe." <note>In modern formal logic, "Every is" and "No is" are called contraries, while "Every is" and "Not every is" are called contradictories. The author here reverses these labels or follows a different tradition of terminology.</note>

The **Necessary Being** is by nature prior to the **Possible**, and the Possible is prior to the **Impossible**. For if the Necessary Being did not exist, one would not know the Possible, and if the Possible did not exist, one would not know the Impossible. <note>This refers to the ontological argument that we understand "possible" only as a deviation from what "must be," and "impossible" as that which cannot even be "possible."</note>

Every judgment, whether it be universal or particular, whether it be affirmative or negative, is composed of two boundaries (<term>terms <note>original Latin: "termini"</note></term>). One term is called the "borne" (or related), and the other is the "originally set." <note>These correspond to the Predicate and the Subject.</note> Compare: "Fire is hot." Here, "fire" is the *set* [subject] and "hot" is the *related* [predicate].

Sometimes one places the subject as the predicate and the predicate as the subject; compare: "Fire is hot" and "The hot thing is fire." This is called the **conversion of the judgment**.

Often the judgment is correct before the conversion, but after—

In formal logic, this is "conversion"—the process of swapping the subject and the predicate of a proposition to see if the statement remains true.</note></term> is false; compare: "Every fire is hot" (true), but "Everything hot is fire" (false). Often, however, it is false before the reversal and true after the same; compare: "Every animal is a human" <note>A false premise, as not all animals are humans.</note> and "Every human is an animal" <note>A true statement after reversal.</note>.
