<meta>This page transitions from the logic of conversion (reversing propositions) to the logic of the syllogism (combining propositions). It defines the basic structure of a syllogism—the premises and the conclusion—and introduces the three "figures" of logic based on the position of the middle term.</meta>

## Prior Analytics <note>Aristotle's *Prior Analytics* is the foundational text of formal logic, introducing the theory of the syllogism.</note> *).

---

When one joins two <term>judgments<note>German: *Urtheile*. In formal logic, these are propositions or statements that assert something to be true or false.</note></term>, another judgment necessarily arises from these two. One calls those two judgments <term>premises<note>German: *Vordersätze*.</note></term> and this latter one (the offspring of the former) the <term>conclusion<note>German: *Schlusssatz*.</note></term>.

<margin>compare</margin> Every human is an animal
Every animal sleeps
Every human sleeps.

The two premises are only joined when they share a <term>term<note>German: *Terminus*. A "term" is a component part of a proposition, such as the subject or the predicate.</note></term> in common, while remaining distinct from each other in their other two terms. This (shared) term must necessarily be <term>the subject<note>German: *gesetzt*, literally "placed" or "set." In this logical context, it refers to the subject of the sentence.</note></term> in one of the two premises and <term>the predicate<note>German: *bezogen*, literally "referred to" or "related." In this logical context, it refers to the property being attributed to the subject (the predicate).</note></term> in the other, or it must be the predicate in both, or the subject in both.

If the term is the subject in one of them and the predicate in the other, this is called the **first figure**.

<margin>compare</margin> Every human is an animal
Every animal is in motion.

"Animal" is the shared term in both premises; it is the predicate in the first and the subject in the second. —

If the term is the predicate in both, this is called the **second figure**.

<margin>compare</margin> Every human is an animal
Every bird is an animal.

"Animal," the shared term, is the predicate in both.

If the term is the subject in both, this is called the **third figure**.

<margin>compare</margin> Every human is an animal
Every human is a laugher. <note>A "laugher" refers to a being capable of laughter, often used in logic as a unique property of humans.</note>

---

*) Headed *The First Analytics* <note>original: *Analutika al ūla*. This is the Arabic title for the *Prior Analytics*.</note> as the fourth logical treatise, though it does not form a separate section on its own.

<summary>This page explains the structure of a syllogism, where two premises sharing a "middle term" lead to a conclusion, and defines the three logical figures based on the middle term's position.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, logic, premises, conclusion, middle term, first figure, second figure, third figure, Aristotle, Prior Analytics</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion of the "first figure" of logic, where the middle term acts as a subject in one premise and a predicate in the other, this page expands on the necessity of a shared term for a valid syllogism and introduces the limitations of the second and third figures.</meta>

If one connects <term>premises<note>German: *Vordersätze*.</note></term> under these conditions and any <term>judgment<note>German: *Urtheil*.</note></term> arises from them, the entire figure is called a **syllogism**, that is, a meaningful analogy or <term>conclusion<note>German: *Schluss*.</note></term>.

The premises either result in a conclusion or they do not. Those which result in one have already been mentioned; those resulting in no conclusion are the premises which have no common <term>term<note>German: *Terminus*. The shared concept that links two statements.</note></term>.

compare:
->Every human is an animal<-
->Every stone is dry.<-

These two premises, although they are both true, yield nothing, for they have no common term. For in premises, a common term is necessary if a connection between the two is to take place, so that a "product" (the conclusion) may emerge from them. This is the goal when setting up two premises, just as the goal in the union of male and female creatures is that children, who resemble both parents, should be born from them. So it is with premises and their connections. 

<note>The author uses a biological metaphor here, comparing logical deduction to procreation; the "common term" acts as the link that allows two separate ideas to "breed" a new piece of knowledge.</note>

The connection takes place so that a judgment may emerge from both premises concerning something that is otherwise not visible to the <term>reason<note>German: *Vernunft*.</note></term>, and to achieve this, one must connect the premises with each other. But not every connection is "fruitful," just as children do not emerge from every marriage. If one says, 

for example:
->Every human is an animal<-
and
->Every bird is an animal,<-

then these two premises—although they have a common term (that is, in the <term>predicate<note>German: *im Bezogenen*. Literally "in the referred," meaning the property being attributed to the subject.</note></term>)—do not yield a correct conclusion, for they belong to the **second figure**.

The same applies when one says:
->No human is a bird<-
and
->No human is a stone,<-

for even if these premises have a common <term>subject<note>German: *Gesetztes*. Literally "the placed," referring to the subject of the proposition.</note></term>, nothing emerges from their connection, *as both belong to the **third figure**.*

<summary>This page explains that a syllogism requires a shared "middle term" to produce a valid conclusion, using a biological metaphor of "fruitful" vs. "unfruitful" unions to describe logical structures. It introduces how the arrangement of terms into the second and third figures can fail to produce a valid logical result.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, logic, middle term, premises, first figure, second figure, third figure, Aristotelian logic, reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion on the structure of syllogisms, the author now contrasts the "fruitless" connections mentioned at the end of the previous page with the "fruitful" ones of the first figure, providing specific examples of how different types of logical judgments (universal and particular) interact to form valid conclusions.</meta>

These two figures <note>Referring to the second and third figures of the syllogism mentioned in the broader logical tradition.</note> are not capable of producing a <term>conclusion<note>German: *Schlusssatz*. The final statement that follows logically from the preceding ones.</note></term>. One should only follow the analogy according to the first figure, as it is presented in the *Book of Logic*.

The fruitful <term>premises<note>German: *Vordersätze*. The starting statements of an argument upon which a conclusion is based.</note></term> of the first form produce all types of <term>judgments<note>German: *Urtheile*. Formal logical statements that claim something to be true or false.</note></term>: universal affirmative, universal negative, particular affirmative, and particular negative.

If one says: Every human is an animal: this is universal affirmative, true—
Every animal is a moving thing: universal affirmative, true;
Conclusion: Every human is a moving thing: universal affirmative, true.

If one says: No human is a stone. This is universal negative, true—
Every stone is a solid (lifeless): universal affirmative, true;
Conclusion: No human is a lifeless thing: universal negative, true.

If one says: Every rational being is a human: universal affirmative, true—
Some humans are scribes: particular affirmative, true;
Conclusion: Some rational beings are scribes: particular affirmative, true.

If one says: No stone is rational: universal negative, true—
Some rational beings are scribes: particular affirmative, true;
Conclusion: No stone is a scribe: universal negative, true.

If one says: Some humans are not scribes: particular negative, true—
Some scribes are not calculators: particular negative, true.
Conclusion: Some humans are not calculators: particular negative, true.

<summary>This page illustrates the "fruitful" nature of the first figure of the syllogism by providing examples of how universal and particular premises lead to valid conclusions. It categorizes these logical arguments into four types: universal affirmative, universal negative, particular affirmative, and particular negative.</summary>
<keywords>logic, syllogism, first figure, premises, conclusions, universal affirmative, universal negative, particular affirmative, particular negative, rational being</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which detailed the "fruitful" connections of the first logical figure, the author now transitions into a warning about the potential for error and intentional deception when using these logical structures. The discussion shifts from the mechanics of valid syllogisms to the dangers of false reasoning.</meta>

The <term>premises<note>German: *Vordersätze*. The initial statements in a logical argument from which a conclusion is drawn.</note></term> and <term>conclusions<note>German: *Schlusssätze*. The final statement that follows logically from the premises.</note></term> of this figure are clear; one must keep them well in mind and remember their different applications in <term>syllogisms<note>German: *Analogieen*. While "analogy" has a different modern meaning, in this historical context of logic, it refers to the syllogism—a form of reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two given premises.</note></term>, and one must also see how their conclusions emerge. One must guard oneself carefully against mistakes and oversights, for "ruinous accidents" can befall them, just as happens with all <term>scales<note>German: *Wagen*. A reference to the "Scale of Logic" (*al-Mizān*), a common metaphor in Arabic and Medieval logic where logic acts as a balance to weigh truth against falsehood.</note></term> and syllogisms, whether this occurs intentionally on the part of scholars or through a creeping carelessness. It often happens that the premises are correct, yet the conclusion is false; and just as often, the premises are false but the conclusion is correct; and often they are all correct or all false.

One must investigate this chapter thoroughly and pay close attention to the points where errors occur in order to guard against them, for those who wish to corrupt the rules of logic draw only from this chapter. When Aristotle established the correct logical syllogisms—into which neither error nor mistake can penetrate—he mentioned that this was the "scale" by which one could distinguish truth from lies, the right from the wrong, the true from the untrue, and the good from the evil. Many at that time strove for this; they sought to grasp it and therefore set aside other books on the <term>art of disputation<note>German: *Disputirkunst*. Referring to Dialectics or the formal practice of debating.</note></term> and rhetoric. This provoked a crowd of "philosophizers" <note>German: *Philosophirer*. Here used pejoratively to refer to Sophists—thinkers who used clever but fallacious arguments to deceive others.</note> to envy, and they sought to weaken the logic of others by presenting correct premises with false conclusions, or false premises with correct conclusions, presenting these to the students of Aristotle in order to confuse them and lead them away from their goal.

For example, if one says:
No human is a stone (universal negative), true—
No stone is an animal (universal negative), true;
Conclusion:
No human is an animal (universal negative), false.
<note>This is a classic example of a "logical trap" where two true statements are joined in a way that does not logically lead to the conclusion provided.</note>

Or:
Every human is a bird (universal affirmative), false—
Every bird is a stone (universal affirmative), false—

Every **stone** is an **animal** (universal) affirmative, **false**;
**Conclusion**:
Every **human** is an **animal** (universal) affirmative, **correct**.

---

Such errors enter into every art from two sides:

a) when the person dedicated to it is entirely ignorant, or only deficiently knowledgeable, of the **fundamental rules** of that art; then he errs and does not know from where, how, or why this happens. This is also how he acts who—without understanding **calculation**, **measure**, or **weight**—calculates, measures, or weighs.

b) when those who are indeed knowledgeable in the art nevertheless have another **intention** and, out of **bribery**, **cunning**, or **deceit**, pursue a different goal than the calculators, weighers, and surveyors.

Because of these errors which people are accustomed to making, **Aristotle** established for his **students** that they should never make a demonstrative **conclusion** from two **negative**, nor from two **universal** or two **partial premises**. Likewise, one may never draw **conclusions** from two **unbounded** <note>German: *Unbegrenzten*. In logic, these are "indefinite" propositions that do not specify a quantity like "all" or "some."</note>, nor from one **partial** and one **special premise**. — These would be the **premises** of those who make errors. **Aristotle** limits himself to the use of correct **premises** with correct **conclusions**, which people often neglect to mention. However, the **premises** which are correct and which are followed by a correct **consequent** in every **subject matter** and at every **time**, before and after **conversion** <note>German: *Umkehr*. A logical process where the subject and predicate of a proposition are swapped.</note>, are clearly presented in the second **Analytics** <note>original: "Analyticis". Referring to Aristotle's *Posterior Analytics*, which deals with the requirements for scientific demonstration.</note>.

### **The causes which prompt the establishment of logical norms.**

The ancient **sages** and **scholars** observed the various **sciences** and defined them. They created this wonderful **art** and handled it with certainty. In doing so, they presented for every **science** and **art** a **primary root** from which its **branches** diverged. They established a **norm** for it, through which one could recognize the **branches** and, as if with a **scale**, distinguish the **excessive** and the **deficient**.

...and could represent what is proportionate; just as one recognizes correct and faulty verses through metrics—that is, the scales of poetry—and likewise demonstrates faulty and correct sentences through grammar—the scales of <note>The original German uses "Etymologie" here, but the parenthetical "Construction" indicates it refers to syntax or the formal structure of sentences.</note> etymology (construction). — Similarly, one uses the <term>astrolabe: an ancient instrument used to solve problems relating to time and the position of the sun and stars</term> as a scale to determine the time at an observatory. These norms and scales are the arbiters between people. God placed them within His creation as judges and mediators to decide what is right amidst conjecture and doubt, so that when one falls into dispute over measurements and quantities, one can find the truth, decide the matter, and settle the discourse so that no further discord remains. Since the learned logicians observed the diversity in the pronouncements and judgments of scholars regarding objects of knowledge, and recognized the doubt, the conjecture, the false opinions, and the disputes over them; and since they also noticed how one accused the other of lying and each claimed his own judgment was correct and the other’s false; and finally, since they found no judge whose verdict was acceptable to both—for the judge himself was a biased party—they deemed it right and wise to establish a just scale and a correct norm through the principles of their reason. This would, like a judge, decide the matters that were doubtful to them, leaving no room for uncertainty. If they judged according to this norm, the truth would be decided.

This is the norm which is called <term>logical proof: a formal demonstration where a conclusion follows necessarily from true premises</term>, which corresponds to geometric proof, and this in turn is equal to arithmetic proof.

The norm and scale of every art is derived from the things...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described logic as a universal "scale" or "standard" analogous to tools used by merchants, astronomers, and craftsmen, this page elaborates on how the specific works of Aristotelian logic function as the component parts of this intellectual measuring tool.</meta>

...are taken from among those objects set for that very purpose. Thus, the weight-scale, through which heaviness is recognized, consists of scale-pans that hold weights; and likewise, the "distance-scale" <note>Referring to tools for linear measurement.</note>, which indicates distances, consists of things with fixed lengths, such as the ell, the rod, or the measuring cord. The same applies to the ruler, by which straight things are recognized, and exactly the same applies to the standard of those who produced the logical proof.

The various opinions that scholars hold regarding the true and the untrue, the correct or the false in their thoughts, become clear to us only through their correct or false expressions. However, true and false pronouncements emerge as such only through the scale and the standard with which they are weighed and measured. Just as a physical scale consists only of things that, in a specific arrangement, became a scale, so it is possible that similar things are weighed and measured with it. The weight-scale is composed of two arms, a central pillar, strings, and pans. The same path was taken to create the "logical scale"—that is, the proof; first, the things of which the scale and the weighed matter consist were highlighted together in the <term>Categories: Aristotle’s treatise classifying the different kinds of predicates (properties) that can be attributed to a subject</term>.

In the <term>Hermeneutics: Referring to Aristotle’s 'De Interpretatione,' which deals with the relationship between language and logic</term>, it was shown how these things are joined and placed together so that a scale and standard might arise.

In the <term>Prior Analytics: Referred to here as 'Analyticis I,' this work explains the formal structure of the syllogism, or logical argument</term>, it is then emphasized how one tests this scale to ensure it is correct and that no distortion occurs within it. Then, the quality of the scale was considered—ensuring it is correct and that neither errors nor defects occur—in the <term>Posterior Analytics: 'Analyticis II,' which focuses on how to reach true scientific or demonstrative knowledge</term>.

Man can indeed say the opposite of what he knows; however, he can know nothing other than what he has mentally grasped. He can state of <note>original: "Said," a common name in Arabic logical texts (similar to the use of "Socrates" in Western logic), likely indicating this German text is a translation of an Arabic work on logic, such as those by Al-Ghazali.</note> Said that he is sitting or standing, and this for the very same moment, yet he cannot *know* this as such, for his reason tells him that it is not so. Since this is the case, one does not need to decide based on a spoken word, but rather one must decide based on the judgment of reason—

...distinguish; for just as craftsmen must be very careful to avoid errors and defects in their art, so too do scholars act, seeking to grasp what is correct. Our school must use logic to protect itself from flaws in its pronouncements from beginning to end. The disputants <note>referring to Sophists, or those who use clever but misleading arguments</note>, by contrast, take care during one or a few sessions, but few maintain all their pronouncements from start to finish in such a way that one does not contradict the other.

Thus, someone might say in one book: "It belongs to the essence of the soul that it follows the physical temperament <note>original: "Constitution"</note> of the body"; in another, however, he claims: "the soul is actually the temperament of the body," and in a third, he says: "I do not know what the soul is."

Another says: "God created the creature <note>meaning humanity or the created world</note> for its own benefit," but then he believes: "God does not forgive the creature and does not let it out of Hell."\*)

Another asserts: "space is a body" or "it is an accident <term>Accident: In philosophy, a property or state that is not essential to the nature of a thing</term> of a body," then he believes again that "if the body perishes, the place remains empty." Or someone thinks: "a part <note>referring to an atom</note> can no longer be divided," and yet he thinks: "that same part has six sides." — Logic and proof do not concern themselves with such contradictory opinions and false views that any person might harbor in their soul. — The practitioners of every art or science must derive their knowledge from the correct root and possess the right standard to measure everything they know, just as occurs in arithmetic. Only in this way can one protect oneself from errors and stay away from the futile. Otherwise, one does not notice the flaws in one's pronouncements, and if one trusts mere views and opinions, one comes into conflict with oneself and cannot reach an agreement with others—

Above all, however, one must know how to guard one's pronouncements from contradiction; if one does this, one is secure in logic.

In short: to become like God as far as man is able. For God speaks only what is true and does only what is good. Strive, therefore, to become like God.

<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophical goal of "becoming like God" to the specific study of the "Posterior Analytics." # Posterior Analytics <note>original: "Analytika posteriora"</note>

The *Posterior Analytics* are intended (determined) for demonstration <term>Beweis/Demonstration: In Aristotelian logic, this refers to a syllogism that produces scientific knowledge—proving not just that something is true, but *why* it is necessarily true</term>. In the *Categories*, the *De Interpretatione* <note>original: "Hermeneuticis"</note>, and the *Prior Analytics* <note>original: "Analyticis I"</note>, the text dealt with the set of categories, the manner of their connection, and their various results (the concluding statement). Here, the task is to represent what the standard of proof is, how many types it has, how it is composed and applied, and how its results emerge.

First, we must represent the goal of the philosophers in applying the standard of proof:

There are many ways to knowledge, cognition, comprehension, and proclamation. These have been dealt with in the treatise on sensory perception (XXIII), the one on the intellect (XXXIV), and the one on the categories of the sciences (VII).

The path taken by the philosophers is that of instruction, and their goal is the knowledge of the true essence of things.

This knowledge is divided into four types: **division**, **resolution** <term>Auflösung/Resolution: The process of analyzing a complex idea by breaking it down into its simpler, constituent parts</term>, **definition**, and **demonstration** <note>original: "Theilung, Auflösung, Definition, Beweis"</note>. Each of these shall now be highlighted, their ways (modes) indicated, and it will be shown how what is known is recognized through them, and why there are exactly four ways for both the smallest and the largest matters.

The primary cause of this is that from the categories, by means of division, it clearly emerges that all existing things...

---

*) This is the XIII. treatise, and thus the fifth logical one: *Isagoge*, *Categories*, *De Interpretatione* <note>original: "Hermeneutica"</note>, *Prior Analytics*, *Posterior Analytics*.

It situates the work within a broader curriculum of logic and the philosophical pursuit of the "essence of things."</summary>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four logical methods mentioned at the end of page 60. It establishes a hierarchy of knowledge, connecting specific logical processes—division, resolution, definition, and demonstration—to the different levels of existence (genus, species, and individual).</meta>

...things are divided into genus, species, and individuals, it necessarily follows that the path to knowledge for each of these is different.

Through **division**, the true meaning of **genera** <term>Genus/Genera: The broad categories or "families" of things (e.g., "Animal")</term> is recognized before that of the **species**, and that of the species before that of the **individuals** (particular things).

Through **resolution** <term>Resolution: Also known as analysis; the process of breaking a complex object down into its simplest parts</term>, one recognizes the true meaning of the **individuals** (particular things)—that is, each single one of them—what it is composed of, from which things it was joined together, and into what it may be dissolved.

Through **definition** (delimitation), one recognizes the true meaning of the **species** <term>Species: A specific group within a genus defined by a shared "difference" (e.g., "Human" within the genus "Animal")</term>, from which genus each of them stems and by how many differences it may be distinguished from others.

Through **demonstration** (proof), one recognizes the true meaning of the **genera**, which are universal and intellectually graspable <note>original: "geistig fassbare"</note> truths.

First, the path of **resolution** shall be considered here, since the path of **division** has already been treated in the *Categories* <note>Aristotle's *Categories*, the first book of his logical organon</note>; furthermore, the path of resolution is closest to the thoughts of the students. This is therefore the path by which the true value of particular things is recognized. These particular things are all graspable "part-things," as will be shown later.

In contrast, the path of **definition** and that of **demonstration** are more delicate and refined, for in both, one recognizes intellectual <note>original: "geistigen"</note> things, i.e., species and genera. An "individual" <note>original: "Einzelding"</note> is a designation for a totality collected from various things and composed of parts that are otherwise often isolated from one another and separated from other existing things.

Particular things fall into two types:

a) Those which are composed of parts similar to one another, such as: this piece of gold, this stone, this wood, and similar particular things whose parts are all of one **substance** <term>Substance: The fundamental "stuff" or essence of a thing that exists in itself</term>.

b) Those which consist of parts made of different substances and of **accidents** <term>Accidents: The non-essential qualities of a thing, such as color, size, or location, which can change without the thing becoming something else</term> that differ from one another, such as: this body, this tree, this city.

<vocab>Genus, Species, Individual, Particular things, Division, Resolution, Definition, Demonstration/Proof, Categories, Substance, Accidents</vocab>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "resolution" (analysis) introduced on page 61. It applies this method of breaking down complex objects into their constituent parts to three specific categories of existence: the natural, the artificial, and the spiritual.</meta>

If we wish to recognize the true meaning of one of these particular things, we first consider the things from which they are composed; we investigate what these are, from which parts they are joined, and how many of them there are.

There are many kinds of composite things. Only God can know the number of their kinds, yet all are encompassed by three genera <term>Genera: The plural of genus; broad categories or classifications</term>. They are a) physical and natural, b) artificial and corporeal, and c) spiritual (pertaining to the soul).

We provide an example of each as a standard:

a) Physical and natural is, for example, the human body. It is a whole composed of limbs of various shapes—head, hand, foot, and so on. Each of its limbs consists, in turn, of parts made of different substances and <term>accidents</term> <note>In philosophy, "accidents" (accidensen) are non-essential properties, such as color or size, that can change without changing the thing's basic nature</note>, such as bones, tendons, veins, flesh, skin, and the like. All these components consist of the four mixtures <note>A reference to the "four humors" of ancient and medieval medicine: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note>, and each mixture is a blend of nutritive juice <note>original: "Speisesaft"; likely referring to chyle, the fluid produced during digestion</note> which arises from the pure contents of food. Food comes from the core of plants, and plants arise from the most delicate parts of the elements; the elements stem from the universal body with its specific properties. The body is joined from form and matter. These two are simple primary substances, and the body is the composite result that follows them. Everything else is only relatively simple or composite.

b) Artificial and corporeal is, for example, "the city." We use this word to designate a whole consisting of markets and sites. Each of these is, in turn, a collection of settlements and dwellings. These are again joined from plaster, stone, wood, and the like. All of this stems from the elements, and the elements arise from the universal body, which consists of matter and form.

c) Spiritual (pertaining to the soul) is, for example, song. This word designates joined melodies; the melody is then joined from tones and verses that correspond to one another; the verses are composed of metrical feet, and these consist again—

...made of moving and resting (vocalized and unvocalized) letters <note>In Arabic grammar, which likely underlies this text, "moving" (haraka) refers to a consonant followed by a vowel, while "resting" (sukun) refers to a consonant without a vowel</note>. Thus, one proceeds by means of resolution <note>The process of breaking a complex idea down into its simplest parts</note> until it becomes clear what composite things consist of; in this way, one recognizes their true value.

By means of definition <note>original: "Begrenzung"</note>, one seeks to recognize the true meaning of the species and the manner in which they must be investigated. Specifically, one designates one of the species, then searches for its genus <note>The broader category to which a species belongs</note> and the sum of its differences <note>The specific characteristics that distinguish one species from another within the same genus</note>; these are ultimately summarized in words and stated when the question is asked.

One asks, for example: What is the definition of Man?
Answer: Man is a rational, mortal creature.
What is the definition of a creature?
Answer: A creature is a self-moving, sensory-perceptible body.
What is the definition of a body?
Answer: A body is a composite long, wide, and deep substance.
What is the definition of substance?
Answer: Substance has no definition, but rather a characteristic marker. One says: Substance is an independent existence that takes on opposing properties.
What are opposing properties?
Answer: Opposing properties are the <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "Accidens"; in philosophy, these are qualities like color or weight that can change without the object becoming something else</note> of a state within the substance, yet they are not part of its essence.

In this manner, one should judge the method of definition; we have dealt with definition and characteristic markers above on page 7.

It is said that things are either connected to one another or separated from one another. Connected things are recognized through division into genera and species, while separated things are recognized through resolution as com- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "zusam-," likely for "zusammengesetzt" (composed)</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of substances and how we understand them. The previous page ended while defining "opposing properties," and this page begins by explaining how composite things are analyzed through resolution and division.</meta>

...composed and joined, thus resolution <note>The analytical process of breaking a complex idea or object down into its simplest parts</note> is for these what division is for those. — In the procedure of proof, the desired goal is the knowledge of the **constitutive forms** <term>constitutive forms: also known as "essential forms," these are the core qualities that make a thing what it is (e.g., "rationality" for a human)</term>, which are the existing essences themselves. — A distinction exists between these and the **perfecting forms** <term>perfecting forms: secondary qualities or "accidents" that complete a thing's description but are not part of its core essence (e.g., being tall or musical)</term>, which as a whole are merely descriptions, qualities, and states that follow one another (upon the essential forms) and by which those very forms are described. Sensory perception, however, does not distinguish the constitutive forms from the perfecting forms, for the former are covered and thus veiled by these qualities. It requires subtle contemplation and clear research to recognize them and to distinguish between them and the (perfecting forms) that follow in a concluding sequence. — This occurs, however, by virtue of analogy and proof.

## Analogy.

Since most human science is acquired by means of analogy <note>original: "Analogie"; in the context of this medieval-style logic, this often refers to *qiyas*, which includes both analogy and the formal syllogism</note> and because it is sometimes false and at other times correct, the reason for this shall be emphasized here, so that one can guard against errors in the application of analogy.

If we ask what analogy is, we say: Analogy is the assembly of premises <note>The starting statements or "vordersätze" of a logical argument</note> and its application is the production of conclusions. — The premises are taken from what is already known, which lies in the fundamental principles of reason, and the primary foundations thereof are gained by means of sensory perception. — The treatise on sensory perception discusses how this takes place.

### Why Must Man Constantly Apply This Analogy?

The senses grasp only individual things, which consist of simple substances located in separate places and partial accidents <term>accidents: properties that can change without changing the nature of the thing, such as color, size, or location</term> found in distinct locations. One knows of these only that they are from a—

<meta>catchword: a- (original: ein-)</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between "constitutive" (essential) and "perfecting" (accidental) forms, noting that sensory perception cannot tell them apart without the use of logical analogy. It defines analogy as the process of using known premises to reach new conclusions.</summary>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the human mind moves beyond simple sensory perception to understand the hidden nature of things. While the previous page defined analogy as a tool for assembly and proof, this page applies that tool to measurement and the development of reasoning from childhood.</meta>

...are different existing beings, but how many of them there are, how and why they are, one only knows exactly through artful, well-structured analogy <note>In this context, "analogy" refers to the logical process of *qiyas*, which includes both reasoning by similarity and formal syllogisms</note>. — If, for example, a person perceives through the senses that some bodies are heavy, large, and full, he can still only find the degree of their weight through scales, their size only through measurement and linear measure, and their volume <note>original: "Füllung" (filling); here referring to capacity or volume</note> only through volumetric measure and calculation. These are all weighings, measurements, and dimensions. Through these, a person knows what he cannot know through conjecture and opinion.

### How many errors can occur in analogy.

Errors enter into analogy in three ways:
a) The analogy is crooked, too large, or too small.
b) The one applying it is ignorant of how it should be applied.
c) The analogy is correct and the user is knowledgeable, yet he errs intentionally in order to deceive and play the hypocrite.

The errors of ignorant users of analogy stem from the fact that man is destined by nature to apply analogy since early childhood. As soon as the child grows a little, reaches a state of proportion <note>likely referring to reaching the "age of reason" or physical maturity</note>, and begins to perceive and contemplate the sensory world, looking then at its parents, recognizing them, and making a distinction between them and itself, it begins to employ its belief, opinion, and conjecture. If it then catches sight of another child and observes it, it knows that this child likewise has two parents, even if it does not see them with its senses; it knows this by analogy to itself. — This is a correct, faultless analogy, for the witness of the **effect** <term>effect: original "Verursachten" (the caused thing); the logical result that points back to a necessary cause</term> leads to the establishment of the **cause** <term>cause: original "Ursache"; the underlying reason or origin of a thing</term>. — Now, if the little boy has siblings whom he perceived with his senses, he begins to think, believe, and conjecture that the other little boy likewise has the same; he does this by analogy to himself. This analogy can be true or false, for it seeks, through the witness of the effect, [to establish] something of the same kind...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how human beings naturally develop the use of analogy—reasoning from the known to the unknown—starting from childhood. It explores the transition from instinctive, often flawed childhood assumptions to the refined investigations of the rational adult.</meta>

— 66 —

<note>original: "Verursachtes"; literally "that which is caused"</note> (i.e., the effect), but cannot yet identify the cause of that effect. Likewise, when that child sees a woman or a man, it believes they must have a child, even though it does not perceive one through its senses; it does this by analogy <note>Refers to the Arabic logical concept of *qiyas*, which involves reasoning by similarity</note> based on its own two parents. Sometimes it is right in this analogy of its own, but sometimes it is wrong, for it seeks to establish the same effect by evidencing a similar cause.

In this way, man applies analogy in his youth. Whenever he finds a certain state or an intermediate cause within himself, his parents, or his siblings, he believes and assumes the same for other children, parents, and siblings. Thus, whenever hunger, thirst, or nakedness strikes him; whenever he feels heat or cold; whenever he enjoys a food that tastes pleasant to him, drinks a beverage that does him good, or puts on a garment that seems beautiful to him; or whenever he is sad over something lost or rejoices over something—he believes that what befell him in these states must also have happened to other children, who are, after all, just like him. 

This is the case with every belief and conjecture regarding sensory-perceptible things. For instance, if the child notices an animal, an object, a tool, or a well with good water in its parents' house, it believes and assumes that such things are to be found in the dwellings of other children, who are indeed like him. This continues with the child until it grows up, becomes rational, investigates the sensory world, and precisely observes existing individual things in their actual states. Then it learns the true value of what it believed and assumed in its youth, and it becomes clear, one thing after another, whether its belief was right or wrong.

The same applies to all the judgments, beliefs, and imaginings of rational adults regarding things before they have researched them, inquired about them, or revealed their true nature.

For example, most people have the false impression <note>original: "Wahn"; here meaning an unexamined assumption or illusion</note> that if they perceive wind, rain, heat, night or day, winter or summer in their own homeland, the situation must be exactly the same in other regions <note>original: "Kreisen"; likely referring to the geographical 'climes' or 'zones' of the world</note>.

This is analogous to how they, in their **boyhood**, imagined that it must look the same in the homes of other children as it did in their own father's house, until they later discovered through the experience of truth whether their assumption was right or wrong.

It is the same with the judgment of rational **human beings**, with their opinions and beliefs, until the true value of what they suspected became clear to them after they pursued the <term>propaedeutic<note>Preparatory or introductory studies, typically referring to the basic sciences like logic and mathematics that precede higher philosophy or theology.</note> and theological sciences.

However, even the wise, who surely possess knowledge, as well as the learned, who are well-practiced—even philosophers—often do not give up their beliefs and opinions. Thus we find that many who devote themselves to philosophy, spiritual matters, and logical proofs, imagine that the Earth—although it remains in the specific place designated for it—is heavy. They do this by analogy, because they find every part of the Earth, whatever it may be, to be heavy. But it is not certain that the whole behaves analogously to these parts.*)

This would therefore be an example of the weakness of **analogy** and of false proof.

Likewise, many people believe that those who are directly opposite their own regions, on the other side of the Earth (the <term>Antipodes<note>original: "Antipoden"; from the Greek words for "opposite feet," referring to those living on the other side of the globe.</note>), would be standing upside down. This is because they observe that if people were on opposite sides of a flat surface on which they were standing, so that their feet were directly opposite one another, they would indeed be positioned that way.

Furthermore, many believe that outside of the world there is an infinite expanse, either empty or full. This happens by analogy, because they see other houses and sites next to their own, and find other cities outside their own cities. In this way, they believe, there must be another sphere-world <note>original: "Sphärenwelt"; the medieval concept of the universe as a series of nested celestial spheres.</note> existing outside of this world.

Furthermore, many believe that God created the world in a specific place and at a specific time; this belief arises from the **analogy** of their own deeds and creations, which necessarily occur within space and time. For this reason, some also believe that the **Creator** is a physical body, for they find that there is no acting **subject** unless it is a body, and the Creator is indeed an actor.

However, if one practices the **theological sciences**, it becomes clear that the matter is quite the opposite; one should compare the theological treatises on this subject<note>original: "theologischen Abhandlungen". The footnote indicates these treatises likely refer to works by Neoplatonists who argued for a non-physical, eternal origin of the world.</note>. A person rises to no new level of science or knowledge unless he grasps things with his intellect about which—before he clearly revealed them—he held an opinion corresponding to his belief in **sensory-perceptible** things, much like the views he held in his childhood before he recognized their true value.

That which is **sensory-perceptible**, which man grasps with his five senses, stands in a manifold relationship to that which follows from the **principles of reason**, just as articulated letters have a manifold relationship to that which is composed from them.

The same is true for the relationship between the objects of knowledge residing within the **principles of reason** and that which emerges from them through proofs and analogies in the form of diverse knowledge. They stand in the same relationship as names do to the utterances, addresses, and statements composed from them.

A proof for the correctness of our assertion regarding the knowledge residing within the **principles of reason** is that which is mentioned in the book of **Euclid**. At the beginning of each section, about ten—sometimes more, sometimes fewer—objects of knowledge residing in the principles of reason are stated, and from these follow hundreds of proven objects of knowledge (propositions). The same applies to the book ***The Almagest***<note>original: "al Magisti"; the Arabic title for Ptolemy's influential astronomical work, derived from the Greek "megistē" meaning "greatest."</note> and most philosophical works.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methodology of knowledge, transitioning from the common errors made by beginners to the formal requirements of logic established by Aristotle. These would be the errors that arise in **analogy** through the ignorance of beginners and incorrect application. Others arise through the distortion inherent in the analogy itself. This results in a great many errors, the mention of which would lead too far here. They are specified in the books of **logic**. Here, on the contrary, the conditions of correct analogy shall be cited and their application in **proof** emphasized. In contrast, we pass over those analogies which cannot be trusted due to errors and deficiencies, as well as those which can be both true and false, as they are habitually applied in examples; this would be the **conjectural analogy** regarding measure<note>original: "Vermuthungsanalogie über das Maass". This refers to speculative reasoning based on estimation rather than exact logical deduction.</note>.

The analogy in which neither error nor delusion takes place is that in which, during its assembly and application, the conditions are observed to which the wise **Aristotle** bound his students.

In every piece of knowledge gained from analogy within any teaching, two known senses must be found that lie within the **principle of reason**, namely: *whether* something is and *what* it is. He makes this a condition because it is impossible to grasp something unknown through something else that is unknown; nor can one gain something known from something unknown through analogy. Rather, one must conclude through analogy from that which is known through the principles of reason to the other thing which is sought through proof.

## The Principles.

Within the principles of reason, however, there are two aspects: **That-ness**<note>original: "Dassheit". In medieval philosophy, this refers to the fact of a thing's existence (*quia* or *existentia*).</note> and **What-ness**<note>original: "Washeit". Also known as "quiddity," referring to the essence or definition of a thing (*quiditas*).</note>. The **That-ness** of things comes to the soul by means of the **senses**, but the **What-ness** through reflection, contemplation, and discrimination; cf. the treatise on the **Sensory-Perceptible** (XXIII). If the **That-ness** of the sensory-perceptible reaches the soul through the senses, and the soul grasps its **What-ness** by means of thought and contemplation, then the soul is called “ra-

<summary>This page outlines the Aristotelian requirements for valid logical analogy, emphasizing that true knowledge must proceed from the known to the unknown by distinguishing between a thing's existence (That-ness) and its essence (What-ness).</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, logic, analogy, That-ness (existence), What-ness (quiddity), principles of reason, sensory perception, soul</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the foundations of logic and proof. It builds on the previous page's distinction between "That-ness" (existence) and "What-ness" (essence) as the two primary modes of knowledge required for any valid logical analogy.</meta>

"rational." If one asks what human reason is, the answer is: it is nothing but the **human soul**, which has become **knowing in actuality**<note>original: "wissend geworden in der That". This is the Aristotelian concept of *energeia* or *actu*, where potential knowledge becomes active understanding.</note>, after it was **knowing in potentiality**<note>original: "wissend war im Vermögen". Referring to *potentia*, the capacity to know before the knowledge is actually acquired.</note>. It only becomes knowing in actuality, however, after the forms of the **that-ness**<note>original: "Dassheit". In medieval philosophy, this refers to *existence* (that a thing is).</note> of things by means of the senses, and the forms of the **what-ness**<note>original: "Washeit". Also known as *quiddity* or *essence* (what a thing is).</note> of the same by means of reflection and contemplation, have come to it. On these two types of knowledge—namely, **that** something is and **what** something is—all **proofs by analogy** are based.

This also includes what is emphasized in **Euclid's** book at the beginning of the first section, namely: seven things are known as self-evident within the **principles of reason**, while the rest are only mediated through proof.

a) If two things are equal to a third, they are also equal to each other.

b) If each of them is the half of a third, they are also equal to each other.

c) If the dimensions of two things coincide so that nothing of the one protrudes beyond the other, they are also equal to each other.

d) The whole is greater than the part*).

These propositions are all taken from the knowledge that is equally grounded in the **principles of reason**; rational people disagree on none of these, and subsequently, that which is disagreed upon is measured against them.

These known propositions and those similar to them are called **principles of reason**, because all rational beings know them and, whenever they consider them, do not disagree; they refuse to treat them theoretically<note>meaning: they refuse to feel the need to "prove" them because they are self-evident.</note> (to prove them); for one only disagrees in things that are grasped by means of **induction** and **measurement**. The reason for their difference of opinion here is the various methods and the manifold tools and measures, as well as the manner of their application.

---

*) Euclid has twelve principles: d) would be the 9th, c) the 8th, and a) and b) applications of the principles of equality.

<meta>This page continues the epistemological discussion from page 70, moving from the mathematical and logical axioms of Euclid to the practical acquisition of knowledge. It transitions from a list of self-evident principles to an explanation of how the soul learns through the senses.</meta>

To treat this matter further would lead us too far afield; moreover, it has already been discussed in the books of **logic** and **disputation**<note>The "disputation" (German: *Disputation*) was a formal method of debate used in medieval schools to test the validity of an argument.</note>.

Here, the goal is to show how the actual nature of this knowledge enters into the souls of rational beings.

This knowledge, which is called the **principles of reason**, comes to the souls of rational beings only through this: that sensory objects are established one after another (in the mind), investigated part by part, and considered individual by individual.

If one then finds many individuals that are encompassed by a single description, it follows for the soul—through this comparison—that everything belonging to the genus of these individuals, or to the genus of this part, behaves in the same way; this is true even if one has not seen every part of this genus, nor every individual of this species.

As a boy grows up and develops, observing individual animals one after another, he finds that they all possess sensory perception and movement; he then knows that all individuals of this genus behave in this way. The same occurs when he subjects a small particle of water to his observation: he finds that it is liquid and moist, whereas a particle of fire is hot and burning, and a small stone is hard and dry. He then knows that everything belonging to these genera behaves similarly. Analogous to this **induction**<note>original: "Induction". The logical process of arriving at a general conclusion by observing specific instances.</note>, the principle enters the reason—that is, by virtue of **sensory perception**.

The levels on which rational beings stand regarding this **knowledge**—which comes to the reason via the senses—vary by degree. He who is skilled in observation, treats everything with theoretical rigor, reflects well, possesses keen intuition, and is practiced in drawing conclusions, will possess more of those things known to the soul at the very "beginning of reason" than will be found in the souls of those who, throughout their lives, were indolent and foolish, occupied only with eating, drinking, games, pleasure, and bodily matters.

<summary>This page explains that human knowledge and the "principles of reason" are acquired through induction—the process of observing individual sensory objects and forming general conclusions about their nature. It emphasizes that those who exercise their minds through study and reflection possess a greater capacity for this fundamental understanding than those focused solely on physical pleasures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limitations of induction and sensory experience, moving from the general concept of observing species to the specific errors that arise when one relies on a single sense to identify the "essence" of an object.</meta>

Those who wish to grasp the true nature of sensory objects fall most frequently into error when they attempt to apprehend them with only one sense, for not all things can be understood in their true nature through a single sense alone. This is the case with someone who sees a **mirage** in the desert<note>original: "Wüstenspiegelung". A common optical illusion in arid environments where light refraction creates the appearance of water.</note>; he believes he sees rivers and ponds because he tries to grasp their nature through the sense of sight alone, yet the true nature of a thing is never apprehended by one sense by itself. With the eye, for example, one perceives only colors and shapes, but the nature of water is not grasped by its color or by the sense of touch alone, but by taste as well. This is because certain liquid bodies resemble the color of water, such as **acetic acid**<term>Acetic acid: the chemical component that gives vinegar its sour taste and pungent smell.</term>, **white naphtha**<note>original: "weisse Naphta". A clear, flammable liquid hydrocarbon used since antiquity for lamps and medicine.</note>, and the like. — For every kind of sensory object, there is a specific sense required to grasp its true nature. — Liquid bodies are distinguished from others through the sense of touch; some are then distinguished from others through taste, and their color is distinguished by the eye. The observer must always employ the specifically designated sense, as this category of sensory objects can only be defined through that particular sense; compare the *Treatise on Sensory Perception XXIII*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methodology of logic and proof, specifically finishing the previous thought on establishing known principles before moving into the rules for proofs by analogy and the pitfalls of circular reasoning in natural science.</meta>

principles of reason. That which is sought is unknown, so that one might recognize or create it—namely, the triangle.

One must proceed in the same way with the proof by analogy; one must first find things that are established in the principles of reason, then create a kind of synthesis, and thereby strive toward the unknown—which is neither established in the principles of reason nor can be grasped through sensory perception.

b) The principle: One must never posit a thing as its own cause in a proof, is clear from the principles of reason; that is, the thing caused is never its own cause. — Yet often, the person conducting the proof posits the effect as its own cause without noticing it during a lengthy discourse.

In natural science, one asks, for example:

**Where does so much rain come from in some parts of the year?**
**Answer: From the amount of mist.**
**Where does the amount of mist come from?**
**From the vapors that rise into the air from the sea and the marsh.**
**Where then does the amount of rising vapors come from?**
**From the many swellings and floodings of the streams and the runoff of the brooks that flow toward the sea.**
**Where then do the quantities of water, streams, and floodings come from?**
**From the amount of rain.**

Thus, the cause of the amount of rain would be the **amount of rain**. Therefore, whoever wishes to conduct a proof must say: the first cause is such and such, then the second, third, and fourth are such and such, in order to be safe from this objection. Furthermore, as far as this example is concerned, there is often much **mist** and little **rain**. However, everything that is caused has four causes<note>This is a reference to the Aristotelian doctrine of the four causes: material, formal, efficient, and final causes.</note>; compare the treatise **Cause and Effect**, **XXXIX**.

c) That which is caused cannot exist before the cause. **This is also clear from the principles of reason; however, since cause and effect belong to the category of relatives**<term>Relatives: A philosophical term (from the Greek "pros ti") referring to things that can only be defined in relation to one another, such as "double" and "half" or "master" and "slave."</term>**, one finds in sensory perception both to-**

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of logical fallacies and the proper relationship between cause and effect, specifically concluding a point about how the mind perceives their sequence even when they appear simultaneously.</meta>

together, even though in reason the cause exists before the effect. Both are often so similar to one another that cause and effect cannot be clearly distinguished from one another.

If one asks an astronomer, for instance: Why is the length of the day in one city different from another? He answers: because the sun stays above the earth longer there. If one reverses this and says: In every city where the sun stays above the earth longer, the day length is also greater, that is correct. However, for many untrained people, it remains hidden that the sun's presence above the earth is the reason for the length of the day, and that the longest day is the one on which the sun stays above the earth the longest.

The same applies to fire and smoke; one often finds both together, yet more often one sees one before the other. Frequently, one is directed toward the fire by the smoke and posits the fire as the <term>intermediate cause</term><note>German: *Mittelursache*. This refers to a cause that connects two things but might not be the ultimate origin.</note> for the existence of the smoke. But one does not know if one is the fundamental cause of the other. However, regarding fire and smoke, neither is the fundamental cause of the other; rather, their fundamental cause is materiality—that is, they are changing bodies. Their producing cause is heat, but the two are different in form. For when heat performs a complete action on changeable bodies, they become fire; but if the heat is incomplete due to moisture, they become smoke and vapor.

d) In a proof, one must not employ inherent <term>accidents</term><note>Original: *Accidensen*. In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property or quality of a thing that is not essential to its nature—for example, the color of a person's hair is an accident, while being a "rational animal" is their essence.</note>.

Aristotle says this only because inherent accidents separate themselves from the things they inhabit as little as the cause separates from the effect.

If one judges a thing to be "caused," it is necessary that it has a creating cause; however, even though inherent accidents cannot be separated, they are never the producing cause of the thing.

*Death, for example, is never detached from murder, yet neither is*

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of causality and proof. The previous page discussed how fire and smoke are related through heat and materiality, rather than one being the fundamental cause of the other. It ended by introducing the requirements for a valid logical proof, specifically that one should not rely on accidental properties.</meta>

...killing is the fundamental cause of death, nor is death the substantial fundamental cause of killing, for many a death occurs without a killing, and there never exists an effect without a cause.

**e) The cause must be essential to the thing.**
Aristotle says this because there are many <term>accidental causes</term><note>German: *accidentelle Ursachen*. In logic, an "accident" is a quality that isn't necessary to the definition of a thing—like a person being tall or short.</note> for a thing, yet these apply neither to all species of a genus nor to all individuals of the species within that genus. Murder is an accidental cause of death, but it does not hold true for all types of death \*) <margin>All that is murdered is dead, but not all that is dead is murdered.</margin>, whereas the fundamental cause must necessarily be an essential one, so that the judgment remains correct both before and after <term>conversion</term><note>German: *Umkehr*. A logical process where the subject and predicate of a proposition are swapped.</note>; for example: Everything that has color is a body; one finds no colored thing unless it be a body, and thus "bodiliness" is the essential cause for everything colored.

**f) The premise must be a universal one.**
For <term>particular premises</term><note>German: *particulare Vordersätze*. These are statements that apply only to some members of a group (e.g., "Some dogs are brown") rather than all of them.</note> do not have a necessary conclusion, but only a possible one; compare:
->Some scribes are viziers<-
->Said is a scribe<-
->Said can possibly be a vizier.<-
If one says, on the other hand:
->Every scribe can read<-
->Said is a scribe,<-
it necessarily follows:
->Said can read.<-

**g) The existence of the related attribute must be an original existence within the subject.**
The related attribute exists in the subject in two ways: it is either a primary (initial) or a secondary (derived) existence. For example, the fact that there are three angles in every triangle is a primary (initial) existence, for this is the very form that creates the triangle. The fact that it can be acute, right, or obtuse-angled is a secondary existence. It is now clear that in a proof, one must only use the essential, substantial descriptions—that is, the form that creates the thing...

<summary>This page outlines three requirements for a logical proof: the cause must be essential rather than accidental, the premises must be universal to ensure a necessary conclusion, and the attributes used must be primary to the subject's form.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, causality, accidental causes, universal premises, syllogism, geometry, essential form, logic</keywords>

...is applied. In it rests the sought-after <term>judgment</term><note>German: *Urtheil*. In logic, a judgment is the mental act of affirming or denying a predicate of a subject.</note>, which emerges in the <term>conclusion</term><note>German: *Schlusssatz*. The final statement in a syllogism that follows logically from the preceding premises.</note> as a true one. Essential, substantial descriptions are divided into three parts: generic, specific, and individual (see the <term>Isagoge</term><note>The *Isagoge* or "Introduction" was written by Porphyry in the 3rd century; it was the standard introductory text to Aristotle’s logic for over a thousand years.</note>). It is certain and beyond doubt that every generic description must necessarily fit all species of that genus; likewise, every specific description applies to all individuals of that species. These are the descriptions that emerge as true in the conclusion and are applicable in proofs. —

whole species, and the specific description does not need to apply to the whole

Every art has its followers, and they have established certain fundamental rules for it. In these, they agree with one another, and they do not hold differing views on the principles of their science, for the principles of every art are taken from another that precedes it in arrangement. The beginnings of proof are taken from the principles of reason, and the principles of reason rely in their beginnings on the mode of sensory perception. The <term>art of proof</term><note>German: *Beweiskunst*.</note> is divided into two types: mathematical and logical proof. — *The beginnings of geometry are taken from an art preceding it (that is, sensory perception); this applies to the propositions of Euclid. The point is something that has no parts. The line is a length without width. The surface has width and length, and similar principles mentioned at the beginning of the sections.*

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on logical proofs. While the previous page addressed how generic and specific descriptions are used to reach certain judgments, this page lists the fundamental axioms—the starting assumptions—that a student must master before practicing the "art of proof" itself. It also argues for the pedagogical value of geometry as a bridge to abstract logic.</meta>

...which the students must address before the art of proof.
**The logician presupposes:**
1. Everything that exists besides the Creator is either <term>substance</term><note>In traditional philosophy, a substance is something that exists in itself rather than in another (like a tree or a person).</note> or <term>accident</term><note>An "accident" is a quality or attribute that exists only within a substance, such as the color of a leaf or the heat of water.</note>.
2. Substance is that which exists in itself and accepts that which is otherwise opposed to it.
3. An accident is that which is in a thing, yet not as a part of it; it can therefore perish without the thing itself perishing.
4. Substance is either simple, such as matter and form, or it is composite, such as a body.
5. Every substance is either an (active) <term>efficient cause</term><note>The agent or force that brings something into being.</note> or a (passive) effect being acted upon.
6. Every efficient cause is more sublime than the effect it produces.
7. Between negation and affirmation there is no middle ground, and between being and non-being there is no intermediate stage.
8. **The accident exerts no effect.**

These and similar propositions are introductions which the student must understand before the proof. — Whoever wishes to engage with logical proof must first practice geometrical proof and adopt something from it; for the latter lies closer to the student’s understanding and is more easily grasped by beginners, since the examples in mathematics are tangibly perceptible and reachable with the eye, even if their content is only heard and perceived through the mind. Perceptible things, however, lie closer to the student's understanding.

Proofs—whether they be geometrical or logical—are only obtained through correct <term>conclusions</term><note>German: *Schlusssätze*. The final statement of a syllogism.</note>, and every conclusion must have two or more correct <term>premises</term><note>German: *Vordersätze*. The starting statements from which a conclusion is drawn.</note>. The **proof** of the proposition that the three angles of a triangle are equal to two right angles is only possible after thirty-two figures (**propositions**)<note>This refers to Proposition 32 in Book I of Euclid's *Elements*.</note>, and that proposition stating that the square of the side opposite the right angle is equal to the two squares of the two sides is only possible after forty-six figures (**propositions**)<note>This refers to the Pythagorean Theorem, which is Proposition 47 in Euclid's *Elements*. The text notes 46, perhaps referring to the number of steps or a specific manuscript tradition.</note>. The same applies to other propositions.

<note>original vocabulary list: Logic, Substance, Accident, Cause, Effect, Proof, Geometry, Conclusion, Premise, Triangle, Square</note>

<summary>This page outlines eight fundamental axioms of logic concerning substance and accidents, while arguing that students should study geometry first because its visual nature makes the structure of logical proof easier to grasp.</summary>
<keywords>logic, substance, accident, causality, geometry, Euclid, premises, syllogism, Pythagorean theorem, pedagogy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general axioms of logic to their specific application in proving the nature of the soul and the physical world. It builds directly on the definitions of "substance" and "accident" established on page 77 to demonstrate how logical premises lead to philosophical conclusions.</meta>

The same applies now to logical proof; often two <term>premises</term><note>A "premise" (Vordersatz) is a statement that forms the basis for a logical conclusion.</note> suffice, while sometimes a whole number of them are necessary. — To prove the existence of the soul, for example, three premises are sufficient:

a) Every body has six sides. This premise is a <term>universal affirmative</term><note>A logical statement that asserts something about every member of a category (e.g., "All humans are mortal").</note>, is true, and is grounded in the fundamental principles of reason.
b) It is impossible for a body to move in all directions at once. This premise is a <term>universal negative</term><note>A statement that denies a quality to an entire category (e.g., "No circles are squares").</note>, is true, and is clear according to the principles of reason.
c) Every body moves in one direction because of a cause that moves it. This premise is a universal affirmative, is true, and lies within the principles of reason.

From these three premises, the existence of the soul emerges. It remains to be shown that the soul is a <term>substance</term> and not an <term>accident</term>, and this proof is based on the aforementioned premises as follows:

Every cause that moves a body must urge it along a path in one direction; thus, the motive force of "heavy" things pushes downward, and that of "light" things pushes upward. This is called a **natural cause**.

However, since movement in different directions and in different ways occurs according to **will and choice**—such as the movement of an animal—this movement is called **psychical**<note>The term "psychical" (seelisch) here refers to the *psyche* or soul as the animating principle of life, distinguishing intentional movement from mechanical or "natural" forces like gravity.</note>. — This would be a rational, tangibly graspable classification. But every cause that moves bodies by virtue of will and choice is a substance, for an **accident has no effect**<note>This refers to the rule established on the previous page: a mere quality (like color) cannot cause a physical change; only a "substance" has the power to act as an efficient cause.</note>. These premises lie within the principles of reason, and thus it follows that the soul is a substance.

### Proof that there is no void in the world.
**Void**<note>Or "vacuum." The author argues against the possibility of a completely empty space.</note> signifies a desolate space in which nothing possible exists. — In the world, no place is thinkable in which there is neither light nor shadow; this is a universal negative, true premise, which is grounded in the principle of reason.

<summary>This page demonstrates how to apply logical syllogisms to prove that the soul exists as a "substance" (an active agent) rather than an "accident" (a passive quality), and argues against the existence of a vacuum.</summary>
<keywords>logical proof, premises, existence of the soul, substance vs accident, natural cause, psychical movement, vacuum, universal affirmative, universal negative</keywords>
