<meta>This page continues the application of logical premises to physical reality. While the previous page used logic to argue for the existence of the soul, this page applies the concepts of "substance" and "accident" to the nature of light, space, and the composition of the physical universe (the "sub-lunar" and "celestial" spheres).</meta>

Another <term>logical axiom</term><note>In German, *Vernunftsatz*. A fundamental principle or proposition of reason used to build an argument.</note> would be: Light and shadow must be either two <term>substances</term><note>original: "Substanzen". In this philosophical context, a substance is something that exists independently.</note> or two <term>accidents</term><note>original: "Accidens". An accident is a property or quality—like color or shape—that cannot exist on its own and must "belong" to a substance.</note>, or one must be a substance and the other an accident.

These are the correct divisions of reason.

Other <term>premises</term><note>original: "Vordersätze". The starting statements of a logical argument.</note> would be:

If light and shadow were two substances, the <term>void</term><note>original: "Leere". A vacuum or empty space containing no matter.</note> would not exist.

If they are two accidents, then it holds true that an accident only exists within a substance; in that case, there is likewise no void.

If one is a substance and the other an accident, the same conclusion follows.

Outside of the world, there is neither void nor fullness.

Void and fullness are two accidents of space.

Space is one of the descriptions of the body.

Now, if there were another body outside of the heavens, our expression "world" would encompass this body together with the world; from where, then, should something else exist outside of the world?

Regarding the saying: the world is something ancient or something newly-arisen. If one uses the expression "ancient" to mean that a long time has passed over it, then the saying is correct; however, if it is meant to signify that it never ceases to remain exactly as it is now, then it is false; for the world does not persist in the same state for even a moment, much less never ceasing to persist in its current state.

This is because scholars, in their use of the term "world," only refer to the physical world; this, however, is divided into two types: the celestial (spherical) and the natural.

Natural bodies are those located below the <term>lunar sphere</term><note>Following the ancient Aristotelian model, the universe was divided into the "sub-lunar" realm (Earth) and the "celestial" realm (the stars and planets).</note>; they are likewise divided into two types: into <term>universal elements</term><note>original: "Allelemente". The four basic building blocks of the material world: water, earth, air, and fire.</note> and <term>compounded products</term><note>original: "Theilproducte". Complex things made from a mixture of elements, such as animals, plants, or minerals.</note>. These latter are constantly in a state of <term>becoming and passing away</term><note>original: "Entstehen und Vergehen". A standard philosophical phrase (generation and corruption) describing the cycle of birth, growth, and death.</note>, and the former are constantly undergoing change and transformation. But even the celestial—

<summary>This page uses Aristotelian logic to argue against the existence of a vacuum (void) and discusses the nature of the physical world, distinguishing between the changing sub-lunar realm of elements and the celestial realm.</summary>
<keywords>substance, accident, void, cosmology, elements, generation and corruption, celestial sphere, logic</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the composition of the universe, this section shifts from the nature of light and space to the dynamic movement of the celestial spheres and the philosophical inevitability of the world's end and the "Great Resurrection."</meta>

Bodies are constantly in motion, undergoing transfer and displacement into their opposites. How, then, could they ever remain in a single, unchanging state? If, on the other hand, one refers to their persistent existence in a specific form, it must be understood that spherical shape and circular motion do not belong to bodies simply because they are "bodies" by nature. These two qualities do not create the essence of a thing, but rather perfect its form, which occurs according to an intended purpose. (See: *Matter and Form*, XIV).

Every form originates within the <term>Creator</term><note>original: *Bildner*, referring to the "Shaper" or "Fashioner" of the universe—a common term in medieval philosophical translations of Arabic texts.</note> and corresponds to His intended purpose. Therefore, form is neither permanently fixed nor does it possess eternal existence.

What maintains the world in its current form is the speed of the <term>surrounding sphere</term><note>The outermost celestial sphere in Aristotelian and Ptolemaic cosmology that encompasses all other spheres and keeps the universe in motion.</note>, whose <term>Mover</term><note>In medieval philosophy, the "Unmoved Mover" or God, who provides the impulse that keeps the universe rotating.</note> exists outside of it. Should this Mover halt the surrounding sphere’s movement, the world would be annihilated, even if the pause lasted only a single moment:

See: <note>The text cites Quran 16:79, but in modern numbering, this is Quran 16:77.</note> "The matter [of the Hour] is not but as the blink of an eye, or even swifter."

But when the universal heaven stops its rotation and the stars stand still—when the <term>heavenly mansions</term><note>original: *Himmelsburgen*. This refers to the *buruj*, the constellations of the zodiac or the "fortresses" of the stars.</note> no longer rise or set—then the form of this world will end. It will cease to exist, and the Great Resurrection will begin. This will undoubtedly happen one day, for everything that currently exists as a <term>possibility</term><note>original: *Möglichkeit*. In Aristotelian terms, this is "potentiality"—the capacity for something to happen that has not yet occurred.</note> must, when its appointed time arrives, certainly and necessarily become <term>actuality</term><note>original: *That/Wirklichkeit*. The transition from potential to reality.</note>.

It is possible for the universal heaven to stop circling; for whatever is in motion has the potential to come to rest—which is, in fact, easier than remaining in motion. The ultimate examples of this are the heavens and the earth.

In the *Treatise on Beginnings*, the cause of the physical world's origin is provided (Chapter XXXI); in the *Treatise on the Visitation and Resurrection*, the cause of the physical world’s destruction is discussed (**Chapter XXXVII**).

<summary>This page argues that the physical world is maintained only by the constant motion of the celestial spheres driven by God; if this motion stops, the world will vanish. It positions the "Great Resurrection" as a logical necessity where the potential for the world's end must eventually become a reality.</summary>

<keywords>celestial spheres, Mover, cosmology, potentiality and actuality, Great Resurrection, Islamic philosophy, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>Building upon the previous page's discussion of the cosmos and the "Great Resurrection," this page transitions from the macrocosm of the universe to the microcosm of the human experience. If a human being progresses in the journey of their soul and the changing of its states in the same manner as occurred during the creation of their body and the shaping of their physical form, they will reach the highest human goal; they will then stand close to the angels and approach their Creator. The most beautiful reward, which is beyond description, shall be their portion; compare Quran 32:17: "No soul knows what delight of the eyes is kept hidden for them." <note>original: *Die Seele weiss nicht, welch eine Augenweide ihr verborgen blieb.* This verse is often cited in Islamic philosophy to describe the inconceivable nature of the afterlife.</note>

In the creation of the body, the human being took the following path: they began from a drop of humble water, then it became a clot of blood in a secure place, then it was shaped and became a fully formed embryo; after that, they were a moving child endowed with senses, then a perceptive, intelligent young boy, then a bustling, vigorous youth. From there, they grew older, yet also more experienced and knowledgeable, until finally they were a wise <term>Sheikh</term><note>original: *Schaich*. While often meaning a tribal leader, here it refers to a "venerable elder" or "spiritual master" who has attained wisdom through age.</note>, a philosopher, and devoted to the Lord. Ultimately, they become a heavenly, spiritual angel of eternal being, delighting and rejoicing in constant joy and existing forever. A human being never rises from one of these stages to another unless they cast off defective states and <term>accidents</term><note>original: *Accidensen*. In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that a thing possesses but which are not essential to its nature (like hair color or height).</note> and put on better and more sublime ones in their place. Likewise, it is necessary that when one rises to a new level of knowledge and recognition, one must cast off the soul's predispositions, habits, views, and ways of teaching and acting to which one has been accustomed since childhood—which existed without true insight or correct perception—and continue this way until it becomes possible for the human being to separate themselves from the human form, to instead accept the heavenly form and ascend to the kingdom of the <term>sphere-world</term><note>original: *Sphärenwelt*. This refers to the celestial realms or the series of concentric heavens in medieval cosmology.</note>.

There, one day, the most beautiful recompense and the richest reward will be yours; you will live the most pleasant life with the children of your kind, alongside the wise, the chosen, the faithful, and the pure, to whom God shows favor, and the prophets, the true witnesses, and the righteous. How beautiful are such companions!

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the soul’s progression from physical birth to spiritual mastery, this section focuses on the intellectual tools—specifically the use of analogy—that the developing human uses to understand the world and move toward higher stages of knowledge.</meta>

Human beings are destined to apply <term>analogy</term><note>original: *Analogie*. In this context, it refers to the logical process of "qiyas" in Islamic philosophy, where one reasons from a known example to an unknown one based on similarity.</note> from early youth, just as it is naturally inherent in them to use their senses without further thought or reflection. The fundamental rules of analogy vary, as they are specified in books of logic where the conditions for <term>disputation</term><note>original: *Disputation*. A formal method of academic debate used to test the validity of an argument.</note> are extensively presented; however, let us mention something here as an example.

Young boys establish their own conditions, or those of their fathers and brothers and their relationship to things, as the basic norms for their analogies. They use what is found in their own homes as a basis for judging the circumstances of all other boys and the relationships of their fathers. What they find in their own houses serves as a standard for the dwellings of those they have never seen and whose circumstances they have never personally observed; this happens because they take what they know of their own conditions as the norm.

Sensible and educated people, on the other hand, only use things whose relationships they truly know and whose states they have well-tested as a basis for measuring all things they have neither seen nor experienced according to a general analogy. Scholars, who compete with one another in refined contemplation, take that which they agree upon with their opponents as the basis and <term>premise</term><note>original: *Vordersatz*. In logic, a premise is a previous statement from which another is inferred or follows as a conclusion.</note> for their analogy. They then measure those things upon which they differ, regardless of whether the point of agreement is true or false, correct or flawed.

Mathematicians and logicians, by contrast, take those things which lie within the principles of reason as the basis of their analogy. They establish these as premises and foundations and then conclude from these deductions other knowledge that was previously neither perceived by the senses nor known as a principle of reason, thus being gained through compelling proofs. Then they take what has been gained and known and use it again as a premise and analogy, deducing from those results other <term>objects of knowledge</term><note>original: *Wissensobjecte*. The specific truths or concepts that the mind seeks to grasp through study.</note> that are more subtle and refined than the previous ones. Thus they continue to do as long as they live; and if a human were to live for the entire duration of this world, they would always find new room for such progress.

<summary>This page describes the human developmental path of reasoning, explaining how individuals progress from the simple, self-centered analogies of childhood to the sophisticated, iterative logical proofs used by mathematicians and scholars.</summary>
<keywords>analogy, logic, premises, childhood development, mathematics, reasoning, disputation, epistemology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the rational soul uses perception and logic—specifically sensory data and analogy—to ascend toward spiritual perfection. Among the animals, some have only one sense, others have two, still others three, four, or all five; see <term>Zoology, XXII</term><note>original: *Zoologie, XXII*. The more senses a creature has, the more sensory objects it perceives. Man possesses all five senses in their perfection. Depending on how much a person observes sensory objects and reflects on their conditions, the more <term>objects of knowledge</term><note>original: *Wissensobjecte*. Also known as "intelligibles" in medieval philosophy; these are concepts understood by the mind rather than just perceived by the eyes.</note>—which lie within the principles of reason—he masters.

The more objects of knowledge a person masters—that is, the more he can establish the derived propositions as premises and analogies and draw conclusions from them—the more knowledge he possesses in his soul. However, the more true knowledge someone has, the more similar he becomes to the angels and the closer he stands to his Lord.

Thus, when the rational, insightful person contemplates what is perceptible through the senses, then clearly distinguishes the known within his soul, applies it to analogies, and draws conclusions, he gains more and more proven intellectual matters in his soul. The more a soul encompasses of these, the greater is its capacity to imagine things in a spiritual manner—that is, in a form free from matter.

Through this, man becomes increasingly similar to those forms and equal to them in potential. When the soul then separates from the body at death, it becomes like that which was thought in the intellect and exists independently in its essence. It escapes <term>Gehenna</term><note>original: *Gehenna*. If only the children of this world—who strive for this life and wish to remain in it for a thousand years—knew that they will never escape the punishment.

Beware of belonging to them; rather, hold yourself to the children of the other world, who stand near to God. Having concluded the ethical and spiritual exhortations, the text now shifts to a more systematic classification of knowledge, specifically introducing the "eighth treatise" which focuses on practical sciences and the manifestation of spiritual causes in the physical world.</meta>

# Practical Science, or Art*).

Physical substances have been described above in their matter and form, as well as the composition of both; it was also shown there which <term>accidents</term><note>In classical philosophy, "accidents" (accidensen) are properties or characteristics—like color, size, or location—that can change without altering the essential nature of a substance.</note> belong to such a composition. (VII.) <note>Likely referring to the preceding seventh treatise in this series of philosophical works.</note>

In the treatises on natural science, it was then emphasized how these substances are perceived by the senses through their accidents.

In the treatises on the <term>intellect</term><note>original: *Vernunft*. In this Neoplatonic context, it refers to the higher faculty of the soul capable of grasping universal truths beyond the physical world.</note>, finally, spiritual substances were treated.

Everything in existence—whether it is spiritual or perceptible to the senses—consists of substances or accidents, or it is composed of both; of forms or matters, or it is joined from both; it is physical or spiritual, or a combination of the two. The physical substances are altogether <term>effects</term><note>original: *bewirkt*. This implies they are the result of higher causes and do not move or exist independently.</note> and are perceptible by means of the senses, while the spiritual substances are active causes <note>original: *wirkend*.</note> and cannot be grasped through the senses.

These latter are recognized only through the intellect and by the deeds and effects proceeding from them which take place in physical substances.

We wish to emphasize here what the works of man are and how they manifest in the materials assigned to them; this may then serve as clear proof to establish the existence of the influencing, spiritual, and active beings, and to highlight their substances, their various movements, their wondrous capacity, rare knowledge, their excellent work, and their diverse operations.

---

*) This is the eighth treatise of the entire series. <note>This confirms the text is part of a larger encyclopedia of knowledge, most likely the Epistles of the Brethren of Purity (Ikhwan al-Safa).</note>

<summary>This page introduces the eighth treatise of a philosophical series, moving from the study of spiritual substances to the study of "practical science" and human art. It establishes a hierarchy where physical substances are seen as the effects of invisible, spiritual, and active causes which can only be understood through their impact on the material world.</summary>
<keywords>practical science, substance and accident, matter and form, spiritual substances, active causes, human works, eighth treatise, philosophy of art</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of sciences introduced in the previous section. While the previous page established the distinction between physical substances (perceived by senses) and spiritual substances (perceived by the intellect), this page specifically defines the nature of knowledge (epistemology) and categorizes all created things based on their origin—from human art to divine creation.</meta>

Human works fall into two types: those of knowledge and those of practice.

**What are the sciences?**

The sciences are forms of that which is known, residing within the soul of the knower.

Knowledge occurs only as a result of instruction and the process of being instructed.

Instruction is the act of awakening a soul that knows in <term>actuality</term><note>original: *in der That*. In medieval philosophy, this refers to a state where a capacity is fully realized or active.</note> to a soul that knows only by <term>potential</term><note>original: *dem Vermögen nach*. This refers to a latent capacity that has not yet been put into practice.</note>.

Being instructed is the impression of the "known form" upon the soul.

The soul, however, can grasp the forms of the known in only three ways.

These are a) the way of the senses, b) that of proof, and c) that of reflection and inner intuition.

Each of these ways has been examined by us in a specific treatise.

Practical knowledge (art) consists of the skilled person bringing forth the forms that exist in their faculty of thought and placing them into <term>matter</term><note>original: *Stoff*. In this context, "matter" refers to the physical substance that receives a "form" or design.</note>.

That which is produced is a composite of matter and form. It falls into four categories: human, natural, spiritual, and divine.

**Human products** are those things which artists produce by giving figure, drawing, and coloring to natural bodies, such as occurs in the markets of cities and in other places.

**Natural products** are the forms found in the structure of creatures, the shaping of plants, and the coloring of substances and minerals.

**Spiritual products** (that is, what the spirits created) are, for example, the arrangement of the four elements beneath the <term>lunar sphere</term><note>In ancient and medieval cosmology, the "lunar sphere" was the boundary between the changing Earth (made of fire, air, water, and earth) and the eternal heavens.</note>—fire, air, water, and earth—the joining of the celestial spheres, and the order of the world-form as a whole.

**Divine products**, by contrast, are the forms and primary matters that are newly created and originated. They emerge all at once from non-being into being, timeless, spaceless, without mea- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "measure" (Maß).</note>

<vocab>knowledge, practice, sciences, instruction, being instructed, soul, senses, proof, intuition, art, matter, form, product, creature</vocab>

It establishes a Neoplatonic framework where instruction awakens the soul's latent potential to understand the universe's structure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of creation and the hierarchy of beings. Having previously defined knowledge and the soul's perception, the text now transitions into a systematic analysis of the requirements for creation across four levels: the human artist, Nature, spiritual beings, and the Divine Creator.</meta>

...matter, form, and motion; for they are all newly conceived and newly brought forth by the Creator.

Every human artist requires six things to complete their work, and seven movements in seven directions <note>In medieval cosmology, this often refers to the six directions of space (up, down, left, right, forward, backward) plus the internal movement or the "rest" at the center.</note>. He requires matter, place, time, equipment, tools, and motion. Nature requires four of these: matter, place, time, and motion. Spiritual beings require only two: matter and motion. The Creator Himself requires none of these, for all six were first newly conceived and brought forth by Him.

One and the same body is called "form" at one time, a "product" <note>original: *Product*. This refers to the finished result of a process.</note> at another, "matter" at a third, and "raw material" <note>original: *gesetzt (Rohstoff)*. The German term *gesetzt* literally means "posited" or "set," referring to the substance as it is prepared or laid out to be worked upon.</note> at a fourth; it is also called a "tool" or "equipment." — A body is called "matter" because of the form it takes; this latter consists of shaping, drawing, coloring, and the like. A body is also called "raw material" for the artist, who creates their work from it or upon it through shaping, coloring, and drawing. Once the body has taken these on, it is called a "product." However, if the artist or someone else uses it for their work, this body is called a "tool"; for example, a piece of iron is called "matter" for every form it takes; one also says it is "raw material" for the blacksmith who creates his work within it. If the blacksmith then makes a knife, axe, drill, saw, or a cooling vessel from it, it is called a "product." If the butcher or someone else then uses the knife, it is called a "tool." The same applies to the axe, drill, saw, and so forth.

The works of nature fall into two types: a) simple, and b) composite. The simple works fall into four types: Fire, Water, Air, and Earth <note>These are the four classical elements believed to be the building blocks of all physical matter.</note>; the composite works fall into three: mineral, vegetable (plants), and animal.

These are the products of Nature, just as everything "posited" <note>original: *Gesetzte*. Refers to the underlying substrate or foundation.</note> for Nature is a product of the **Soul**, and everything "posited" for the **Soul** is a product of the **Divine**. Every human requires for their trade one or more pieces of equipment and a...

<summary>This page outlines a philosophical hierarchy of creation, explaining how the number of requirements for making things decreases from human artists to the Divine Creator. it also provides a linguistic framework for how a physical object can change its classification—from raw material to product to tool—depending on its use.</summary>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the six requirements for human creation (matter, place, time, equipment, tools, and motion). It specifically defines the distinction between "equipment" (the body) and "tools" (external objects) and introduces the cosmological significance of the seven types of motion used in craftsmanship.</meta>

...or several tools that he applies to the same work. Between a tool and equipment, the difference is that equipment consists of the hand, foot, eye—in short, the limbs of the body; a tool, however, lies outside the essence of the artist, such as the carpenter’s ax, the tailor’s needle, the writer’s pen, or the shoemaker’s awl.

Every craftsman uses tools of various shapes and positions in his work. The carpenter hews trunks smooth; in this, his movement is from top to bottom. He saws with a saw; in this, his movement is from front to back. With the drill, he bores; in this, his movement is circular, as well as to the right and left, but the movement of the drill itself is in a circle. Thus, every workman has seven movements: one circular and six straight <note>The "six straight" movements refer to the three dimensions of space: up/down, front/back, and left/right.</note>.

Divine Wisdom established this as a necessity, for the movements of the celestial bodies fall into seven types: one circular movement toward the original goal and six accidental movements; see *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: *Himmel und Welt*. Likely a reference to Aristotle’s *De Caelo* or a related medieval commentary.</note>, XV.

Individual bodies below the lunar sphere <note>original: *Mondkreis*. In medieval science, the "sublunary" realm (everything below the moon) is the earthly world of change, which is influenced by the "perfect" and unchanging movements of the stars and planets.</note> are similar in their movements to those celestial bodies, for the latter are the causes, and the individual bodies are the effects. It is in the nature of an effect that a similarity to its cause and the cause's influence be found within it. As the Sages say: secondary things resemble primary things, just as children in their games imitate the arts of their fathers, mothers, and teachers.

Every person who practices a craft must move one or more limbs, such as the hand, foot, back, shoulder, or knee. In short, there is no limb of the body unless the soul exerts one or more influences upon it, which are different from those exerted on other limbs. The limbs are therefore tools for the soul; see the treatises: *The Composition of the Body*, XXII; *The Senses and the Sensible*, XXIII; *The Intellect and the Intelligible*, XXXIII; *Man as a Small* **World** <note>original: *Der Mensch eine kleine Welt*. This refers to the concept of the "microcosm," the idea that the human being is a miniature map of the entire universe.</note>, XXV.

<note>Vocabulary: Tool, Equipment, Carpenter, Tailor, Scribe, Shoemaker, Celestial bodies, Lunar sphere, Soul, Craft</note>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of craftsmanship within the framework of medieval science. Having previously defined the distinction between the artisan's body (equipment) and external tools, as well as the cosmological significance of their movements, the text now transitions to the first of the six requirements for creation: the "matter" or substance (Stoff) upon which the artist works.</meta>

Every art must have a "given" <note>original: *Gesetztes*. In this context, it refers to the underlying substance or "matter" provided by nature for the artist to shape.</note> matter, upon which and out of which the artist creates his work.

In the art of man, this matter is divided into two types: the spiritual and the physical.

The spiritual matter is intended for the spiritual arts; compare the treatises on Logic <note>In the medieval curriculum, Logic was considered a "spiritual craft" because it provides the "matter" (concepts and premises) for constructing arguments.</note>. The physical matter, however, is intended for the practical arts. This latter type is divided into two categories: the simple and the composite.

Simple matter consists of Fire, Air, Water, and Earth <note>These are the four classical elements of antiquity.</note>. Composite matter includes the bodies of minerals <note>original: *Stein-*, literally "stone-bodies".</note>, plants, and animals.

## The Individual Crafts.

Crafts that have water alone as their matter are sailors, irrigators, water-carriers <note>original: *Tränker*, those who provide water for people or livestock.</note>, swimmers, and the like.

Other crafts have earth alone as their matter, such as well-diggers, ditch-diggers, canal-diggers, grave-diggers, and mineral workers, and likewise all those who transport earth and stone.

Still other crafts have air alone as their matter, such as flutists, trombonists, and all wind players.

Finally, others have fire alone as their matter, such as torch-bearers, burners, and lamp-bearers.

In other crafts, earth and water together form the matter. This applies to fullers <note>original: *Walker*. These craftsmen cleaned and thickened cloth using water and "fuller's earth" (a type of clay).</note>, vessel and cauldron makers, and stone-moulders—in short, all those who moisten dust.

In others, "pit-bodies" <note>original: *Grubenkörper*. A medieval term for minerals or metals extracted from the ground.</note> (minerals) are the matter, as with smiths, brass-founders <note>original: *Gelbgiesser*, literally "yellow-casters".</note>, lead-founders, jewelers, and the like.

Others again originally have trees, plants, reeds, and leaves as their matter, such as carpenters and palm-leaf workers.

Others have the husks of plants (that is, the plant body) as their matter, such as flax-spinners, reed-weavers, and papermakers.

In others, the leaves, flowers, blossoms, roots, and bark of trees and plants form the matter.

Others have tree-fruit or stalks as their matter, such as merchants of flour, rice, juice, syrup, or linseed, as well as everyone who produces oil from tree fruits or plant seeds.

<summary>This page categorizes various human crafts based on the type of "matter" they utilize, ranging from the four simple elements to complex organic materials like plants and minerals. It distinguishes between "spiritual arts" like logic and "practical arts" involving physical labor.</summary>

<keywords>classification of crafts, four elements, spiritual arts, practical arts, material science, medieval trades, minerals, plant-based industry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of trades and crafts based on their "matter" (Stoff). Having previously covered the four elements and mineral/plant matter, the text now addresses crafts working with animals, humans, and abstract concepts like value and the soul. It then transitions into a new classification based on the physical requirements and tools of the craftsmen.</meta>

In others, animals serve as the matter, such as for hunters, shepherds of small and large livestock, bird-catchers, and the like.

In other trades, a part of the animal body is the matter, such as the meat, the bones, the skin, the hair, the wool, or the silk. This applies to butchers, roasters, cooks, leatherworkers, strap-cutters, and sour-milk sellers <note>original: *Dickmilchhändler*. These were merchants who specialized in fermented milk products, considered here a "part" or product of the animal body.</note>.

In others, physical measurements are the matter, such as for weighers and measurers.

In others, the human body is the matter, such as for physicians and barbers <note>original: *Barbiren*. In historical contexts, barbers often served as "barber-surgeons," performing minor surgeries, bloodletting, and tooth extractions.</note>.

In others, the value of things is the matter, such as for money-changers, traders, and exhibitors.

In others, the souls of men are the matter, as in the art of all scholars; this is divided into theoretical and practical science <note>This classification reflects the medieval view that the "matter" of education is the human soul, which the scholar shapes through knowledge.</note>.

---

Among craftsmen, some require only one limb of their body, while others require two. Some require only one tool for their work, such as plowmen and planters; others require many, like saddlers and spinners. Every one of these requires tools outside of himself, as well as the movement of hand and foot in his trade.

Others do not require an external tool; a limb of their body is sufficient for them, such as the orator, poet, reader, physician, and the like. For each of these, the tongue alone is sufficient.

Others, such as overseers, watchmen, and controllers, need only their eyes.

In contrast, others require two of their limbs in the exercise of their art, such as the storyteller and the mourner <note>original: *Wehklager*. Professional mourners were common in many ancient and medieval cultures, using both voice and hand gestures (like beating the chest) to lead public grieving.</note>; they require the tongue and the hands.

Finally, others require the whole body, such as dancers and swimmers.

Some must walk during the exercise of their art, such as runners, messengers, and surveyors; others must sit, like tailors and flax-beaters.

Of the craftsmen, some require only one tool for their work, such as trombonists, flutists, and drummers;

<meta>This page continues the classification of crafts and trades, moving from the physical requirements of the craftsman to the tools used and the specific application of fire in various industries.</meta>

...others require two, such as the tailor, who needs the needle and the scissors, or the scribe, who requires the pen and the ink. If the latter also employs a knife, this does not belong to the art of writing itself, but rather to the preparation <note>original: *Zurichtung*. This refers to the preparation of the parchment or the sharpening of the quill, which the text considers a separate mechanical task from the "art" of calligraphy.</note>.

Some craftsmen stand continually during their work, such as cotton cleaners, rice millers, and those who turn a wheel with their foot.

Many craftsmen must apply fire during their work. This occurs for one of three reasons:

a) One applies it to the raw material <note>original: *dem Gesetzten (Stoff)*. This refers to the primary matter or substrate before it has been shaped.</note>, as do blacksmiths, brass-workers, glass-workers, and those who work with lime and pitch. The intention here is to prepare the matter so that it may take on a form and shape; for since the material for these trades is hard stone <note>The text classifies minerals and ores as "stone" in their unrefined state.</note>, it only accepts forms after it has been softened by fire. Only then can the craftsman give the material the form he has in mind, and once the material has accepted this form, it becomes a finished product.

b) Other craftsmen apply fire to the finished product, such as silk-workers, kettle-makers, gypsum-workers <note>original: *Gadhaarbeiter*. This likely refers to workers of *al-qaḍḍa*, a type of mortar or plaster used in construction and decorative arts.</note>, and stone molders. These seek to bind and strengthen the forms to the matter through fire, so that the form does not quickly depart from the matter; for it is in the nature of matter to repel forms from its essence and return to its original state, becoming once again a simple substance without composition or quality <note>This follows the Aristotelian view that "prime matter" is inherently formless and that human "art" imposes a form that the material naturally tends to lose over time.</note>.

c) Other craftsmen apply fire both to the raw material and the finished product, such as the cook, the baker, the roaster, and the like; they intend thereby to complete and ripen <note>original: *zeitigen*. To bring to maturity or a state of readiness.</note> the work, so that its utility is made complete.

---

The trades are, in part, those called forth by necessity as their primary goal; others arose in their wake and service; others serve the completion and perfection of the former; still others are intended for decoration and ornament.

<meta>This page continues the classification of crafts, moving from the physical labor and tools discussed previously to a hierarchical theory of how various trades evolved from man's basic needs for clothing, food, and shelter.</meta>

Three serve the original goal: the plowman, the farmer, and the weaver. The others arose as a consequence of these; they either served them or brought them to perfection.

Since man was created with soft skin, without fur or wool—which is not the case with other creatures—necessity forced him to make clothes for himself, and this was achieved through the art of weaving.

Now, since weaving is not possible without well-prepared flax, and this requires hackling <note>original: *Hecheln*. A process of combing flax or hemp with a metal-toothed brush to separate and straighten the fibers.</note>, and since the latter only takes place after the separation of the seeds, these three steps exist in the service of the former. Furthermore, since clothing is not produced by weaving alone, but through tailoring, fulling <note>original: *Walkerei*. The process of cleansing and thickening cloth, often by beating it in liquid, to make it more durable.</note>, sewing, and embroidery, these are the trades that bring weaving to perfection.

Furthermore, since man was created as a being in need of nourishment and food—and nourishment and food are only obtained from plant grains and tree fruits—necessity forced humans to till the soil and to plant. But since tilling requires breaking the land and digging irrigation channels, and this can only be done through the plow and the art of measurement <note>original: *Messkunst*. This refers to land surveying or the geometric principles required for agricultural planning.</note>, and because the plow in turn requires blacksmithing and woodworking, necessity forced these into existence. Blacksmithing, however, required mining (the art of the pits), and this in turn required many other crafts; therefore, they all exist as the successors and servants of the arts of tilling and planting.

Since the grain and tree fruit must then be crushed and ground, this called forth the creation of the arts of milling and pressing <note>original: *Kelterkunst*. This specifically refers to the use of a wine or oil press, though used here as a general term for processing harvests.</note>; further, since the ground material is only suitable for food through baking, the arts of baking and cooking necessarily arose.

Thus, every art requires another for its perfection and its service.

Then man required protection against heat and cold, as well as defense against wild animals, and he also had to store his food. Thus, necessity drove him to the art of building; this, however, required woodworkers and

<meta>This page continues the philosophical classification of human labor. Having previously established the necessity of primary crafts like agriculture and weaving, the text now moves into the hierarchy of secondary trades and the spiritual purpose of work, distinguishing between material survival and the refinement of the soul.</meta>

Blacksmithing ~~and~~ every one of these arts requires others for its support and completion.

For decoration and finery, there is brocade and silk, perfume, and the like.

In every trade, ingenuity lies in allowing the forms to reach the materials <note>The author uses a Platonic framework here: the craftsman has an idea (the "form") and imposes it upon raw physical "matter."</note> and completing and perfecting them in such a way that one achieves utility for this life.

Mankind is divided into craftsmen and merchants, the rich and the poor. The craftsmen work with their bodies and tools upon materials to grant them form, design, color, and shape; they seek payment for their labor to sustain their lives. — The merchants, by contrast, sell by taking and giving; their goal is to take more than they have given. — The wealthy possess objects that are natural as well as produced or artistic; they seek to hold onto and preserve them. This they ~~do~~ out of fear of poverty, while the poor, by contrast, lack these things and strive for wealth. — Most people strive to ward off poverty, and out of this fear of poverty, the wealthy seek to acquire and hold onto finished works and goods. In doing so, they wish to satisfy their needs and enjoy these things for a time. — Yet these are only pleasures for a time; the pleasures for eternity lie in the **perfection of the soul**, which occurs through true knowledge, noble character traits, right views, and virtuous actions. The goal of this perfection of the soul is to enable it to ascend from this "depth of the transitory world" to the "heavenly world"—that is, to effect its escape from the **sea of matter**. <note>original: *Meere der Materie*. A philosophical metaphor common in Neoplatonic and Islamic philosophy (like the Epistles of the Brethren of Purity), describing the physical world as a drowning or entangling force from which the soul must be rescued.</note>

All works, every action, and every skill are performed by man through Reason, the gift of discernment, and reflection. All these are spiritual powers of Reason, which is why we have linked this treatise with the one concerning Reason and its object. Every rational person recognizes, as soon as they observe the works and deeds that arise from human—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the soul and the physical world. While the previous page concluded with the soul's escape from the transitory world, this page grounds that philosophy by explaining how the soul is the true "actor" behind human physical labor and crafts.</meta>

...emerging from the hand, it is immediately perceived that another substance is connected to this body, and it is precisely this substance that produces good works and reliable deeds from this body. This is clear from the fact that a dead body, after the separation of the soul, is capable of no movement, let alone any action.

It is therefore an error to call the body the actor, for life, determination, and knowledge are <term>accidents</term><note>In classical philosophy, an "accident" (original: *Accidens*) is a property or quality that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature—for example, the color of a person's hair is an accident, while being a rational being is essential.</note>; they do not have their place in the body, but are spiritual accidents that reside in the soul-substance, since man is a total being formed from a mortal body and a living (unsterblich) <note>original: "unsterblich," meaning immortal</note> soul. These accidents are only found in the state of life, but are absent in the state of death. — Life consists only in the soul using the body for its work, and death is nothing other than the soul ceasing this application. Waking is nothing but the soul’s use of the five senses, and sleep is nothing but the cessation of this use.

Among the works of man, one distinguishes itself from the other in various respects. First, in terms of the material provided for them; second, in relation to their product; third, in relation to the urgent necessity that compelled their execution; fourth, in terms of general utility; and finally, fifth, in terms of the labor of the soul.

Precedence in terms of urgent necessity falls to the three categories: weaving, building, and farming. — Precedence regarding the material designated for the trade belongs to the art of the goldsmiths, spice-preparers, and the like. Regarding craftsmanship, precedence belongs to the art of those who prepare astronomical instruments, such as <term>astrolabes</term><note>An ancient device used by astronomers and navigators to measure the altitude of celestial bodies and determine time or location.</note>, rings, and spheres modeled after the shapes of the <term>spheres</term><note>original: "Sphären," referring to the celestial orbs of the heavens in medieval cosmology.</note>.

A piece of brass is worth about five <term>dirhams</term><note>A historical silver coin used throughout the Islamic world.</note>; if it is crafted into an astrolabe, it is worth a hundred dirhams. This...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on human labor and the classification of crafts. While the previous page introduced five criteria for ranking human work (material, product, necessity, utility, and labor of the soul), this page explores how "value" is derived from form and compares the essential nature of public service jobs against the luxury of the fine arts.</meta>

**Value**, therefore, does not arise from the material itself, but from the sculpted form. In the case of the goldsmith or the minter, who uses gold and silver as the raw material for their craft, there is never such a large discrepancy in value between the material and the finished product.

Regarding general utility, bath attendants, cleaners, and street sweepers hold the precedence. For bathing brings much benefit to the great and small, the high and low, the near and far, in the same way and without distinction.

In contrast, crafts often differ in terms of their utility, just as many differences are found in clothing, food, housing, and the like. The rich man has different needs than the poor man. Only butchers, barbers, and their like are the same for everyone.

The cleaners and sweepers, if they were to neglect their business even once, would cause great harm to all city dwellers. In contrast, the herbalists <note>original: "Kräuterkrämer," referring to sellers of medicinal herbs and spices</note>—who stand directly opposite the former in terms of the material of their trade—could easily close their shops and markets for a whole month without such a disadvantage arising for the city dwellers as if the former were to stop their work for even a single week. The city would then be filled with filth, dung, carcasses, and refuse, and the lives of the inhabitants would be shortened as a result.

The arts which deserve preference in themselves are sleight of hand <note>original: "Taschenspielerkunst," referring to the art of a conjurer or street magician</note>, painting, music, and the like. Sleight of hand is nothing but speed of movement and the concealment of the primary causes which the artist employs, so that while the fools laugh, the wise man marvels at the cleverness of the artist.

The art of the painter is merely the imitation of the forms of existing things, whether they be products of art or nature, belonging to the world of humans or animals. Sometimes the painter reaches such a level that those who look at his picture turn away from the contemplation of real things, because they delight in the beauty and brilliance of the painted ones. Yet there are great differences in this. Sometimes the forms and structures are made of pure tints and colors and so

The merit of music is of a twofold nature: firstly, regarding the art in itself, and secondly, regarding its influence on the souls; the artists also differ in many ways. Thus, one of them strikes a melody and some of the listeners are moved; then another strikes the same one and all are gripped; as was the case with that man who entered a gathering dressed like a monk <note>likely a reference to a Sufi or an ascetic whose spiritual state amplified the power of the music</note>; see above the treatise on music, V.

The perfection of art is the becoming-like the wise artist, that is, the Creator. It is said: "God loves the skillful artist," and it is said of the Prophet: "God loves the artist who is secure in his art," just as it is also said of philosophy: that it is the becoming-like God <note>original: "Aehnlichwerden Gottes," a reference to the Platonic concept of *homoiosis theoi*—the idea that the goal of wisdom is to resemble the divine as much as humanly possible</note>, as far as this is possible for man, i.e., similar in knowledge, art, and the bestowal of goodness. For God is the most knowing among the knowing and the wisest among the wise, the (actual) artist and the most excellent of the good. Everyone who rises by a step in these things comes closer to God. Thus God says regarding the angels, who are indeed the selection of His servants: They strive for the proximity of God, which of them would be closest to God, and hope for His mercy. But the proximity of God is only grasped through action, knowledge, or divine service. For the servants (of God) have nothing but their striving, as God mentions in the Koran 53:40: "Truly, man has nothing but his striving, truly his striving will one day be recognized."

...different natures and their various conditions at birth differently. This is presented in the treatise on the influence of the stars on births, XXIV, yet something of it shall also be highlighted here.

Among humans, some have the disposition to grasp one or several arts with easy acceptance, so that many people learn an art through the goodness of nature as soon as they observe it in the master craftsmen, even though they apply only the slightest attention to it. Others, on the other hand, can dwell on it for a long time and require a constant drive and continuous stimulation. For some, an art succeeds only poorly, as it does not correspond to their nature; others do not learn an art at all, as their nature is entirely devoid of it.

The intermediate cause of this is that an art succeeds for a child only according to the indication of a star, namely the one that rules the tenth <term>Sternburg|literally "star castle," a medieval German term referring to a House or Sign of the Zodiac</term> from the rising star <term>Ascendant|the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</term>. If this house is ruled by one by one of the three stars Mars, Venus, or Mercury, the child must practice an art, for no art is possible without movement, liveliness, and insight. Movement is characteristic of Mars, liveliness of Venus, and insight of Mercury.

Four celestial bodies, however, as indicators in the hour of birth, do not bestow an art; rather, the one born acts in accordance with them. These are the Sun, Saturn, Jupiter, and the Moon. He in whose chart the Sun rules the tenth zodiac sign learns no trade because of his greatness of soul <note>original: "Seelengrösse," referring to the magnanimity and high status associated with solar influence</note>, as is the case with princes. He in whose chart Jupiter rules allows no trade to be taught to him, and he does not learn such a thing because of his abstinence and humility, because he does not value the things of this world, but has instead turned toward the striving of the future <note>likely referring to the afterlife or spiritual salvation</note>, like the noble prophets and their disciples. He whose birth is ruled by Saturn allows no trade to be taught to him; he does not learn it because of his laziness, because his nature is so heavy and immobile. He delights in lowliness and rest, by which he seeks to earn his living. This applies to camel drivers and beggars. The one who is thus influenced by the Moon...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a person’s nature and birth hour determine their suitability for a profession. While the previous page focused on the specific planetary alignments (Mars, Venus, Mercury) required for artistic success, this page transitions into the cultural and political systems that historically enforced these professional roles, specifically citing the Sabians of Harran and the Sassanid Persians.</meta>

A subject performs no craft, because his station is so low, his nature so sluggish, and his understanding so slight; such are women and the men who resemble them. The Harranians*) <note>The people of Harran, an ancient city in Upper Mesopotamia known as a center of Hellenistic philosophy and planetary worship.</note> of ancient times therefore used to choose a specific day when they wished to dedicate a boy to a craft, bringing him into the temple of their idols. They would offer a sacrifice to the idol of the star that pointed toward that specific craft and consecrate the boy to it, as they had learned of his suitability through his hour of birth. If, however, they did not know the hour of birth, they would present the boy with the crafted works found in the temple; they then consecrated him to the art for which he showed desire, equipping him for that same craft.

The craft of fathers and ancestors lends great perfection to that of the children, especially if their hour of birth also points toward it. For this reason, in the reign of Ardashir I <note>Ardashir Babakan, the founder of the Sassanid Empire in Persia (reigned 224–240 CE).</note>, it was determined for every rank of people that they must necessarily practice the craft of their fathers and ancestors and should not deviate from it. It was believed that this belonged to the decrees of God according to the Book of Zoroaster. This was done for the preservation of the kingdom, so that only those of the royal family might strive for the throne; for if there were many who desired it, the struggle for the kingship among them would be great, and in the great struggle, order would perish and ruin would take hold.

Kingship, however, serves the purpose of maintaining the <term>Nomos|the fundamental law or foundational social order</term>; for if the people of the religious law, the prophets, and the philosophers did not have fear of the Sultan <note>The word "Sultan" here is used generally to mean a "ruler" or "sovereign power."</note>, they would not submit themselves to the decision of the fundamental law, nor to its provisions and statutes. The observation of this fundamental law, however, aims at the welfare of religion and the world as a whole.

If one neglects to follow the instructions of this law, then everything perishes and wisdom is rendered void. Yet divine guidance does not allow this, as it the [be-]

<meta>This page transitions from the sociological discussion of hereditary crafts and the Sassanid caste system to the underlying Neoplatonic metaphysics that justifies this order. It moves from the "artist" as a human worker to the "Creator" or "Architect" of the universe, establishing the philosophical relationship between the body, the soul, and the World Soul.</meta>

...is the founding cause for its existence, its endurance, its order, and its perfection; for every form that is created exists first in the thoughts of the creator <note>German: *Bildner*. This refers to a divine architect or craftsman, similar to the Platonic Demiurge.</note>.

The materials that the artist works with, his actions, his tools, and his body are all, without exception, bodies. The body, as a body, is not self-moving; rather, actions arise only through movement. That which moves within the body is another substance called the soul. Souls, as such, are a substance, just as bodies, as such, are a substance. Souls differ according to their varying powers, and the variation of their powers corresponds to the variation of their actions, knowledge, and natures—just as the variation of bodies corresponds to the variation of their shapes, and the variation of their shapes corresponds to the variation of their <term>accidents</term> <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are properties that a thing has (like color or weight) which are not essential to its nature as a substance.</note>. The <term>World Soul</term> <note>The *Anima Mundi*, an intrinsic spirit believed by ancient philosophers to animate all nature in the same way the individual soul animates the human body.</note> is only one soul, just as the world-body, with all its spheres, stars, elements, and products, is only one body.

The World Soul can perform universal actions by virtue of its universal powers, genus actions by virtue of its genus powers, species actions by virtue of its species powers, and individual (special) actions by virtue of its individual powers. These powers are then named according to their deeds as genus-, species-, or individual-souls. There are many such souls according to their manifold powers, and their powers are many according to their diverse actions—just as the world-body is manifold according to its various shapes, and the shapes are manifold according to their various accidents.

The action of the Universal World Soul is that which causes the spheres and stars to revolve from East to West, according to the primal goal, while leaving them to rest in their specific centers. Its genus actions, which are unique to every sphere and every star, are the six accidental movements; compare the treatise *Heaven and Earth* *), to which also belong the natural...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the World Soul's hierarchy of actions, shifting from the cosmic scale of the elements to the specific operations of the individual human soul. It bridges the gap between metaphysical "substance" and the practical process of education, framing learning as an ontological transition from potentiality to actuality.</meta>

...movements belonging to the elements beneath the sublunary sphere <note>German: *Mondkreis*. In the Aristotelian cosmos, the area "under the moon" is the realm of the four elements (earth, water, air, fire) where things are born and die, unlike the eternal heavens.</note> belong here; see the treatise *On Generation and Corruption* <note>Aristotle’s *De Generatione et Corruptione*, a foundational text on how physical things are formed and dissolved.</note>, Book XVI. Their species-actions are those which specifically belong to created beings—the "products" known as animal, plant, and mineral. Their individual actions, finally, consist of what emerges from individual animals and from the works that, as we mentioned above, proceed from the hands of mortals.

The soul is, by its essence, a spiritual substance; when it unites with a body, it makes that body living just as it is itself, exactly as fire—which is by its essence a spiritual-corporeal substance—makes a body as hot as itself when it comes near it.

The soul possesses two powers: one is the cognitive power <note>German: *wissend*, literally "knowing."</note> and the other is the effective power <note>German: *wirkend*, literally "working" or "acting."</note>. With its cognitive power, it abstracts the characteristics of the object of knowledge away from the matter and impresses them upon its own essence, so that the essence of its substance serves as the "matter" for these characteristics, and they exist within it as "forms."

With its effective power, it then allows these forms, which lie within its thoughts, to emerge and impresses them upon physical matter; thus, through the soul, the body becomes a product.

Of everyone who is taught a science, it can be said that they possess the form of the object of knowledge in their soul potentially <note>German: *dem Vermögen nach*. This is a central concept in medieval philosophy: the "potential" (what could be) versus the "actual" (what is currently real).</note>; when they then learn that science, it becomes theirs in actuality.

Learning, or being instructed, is therefore nothing other than the path from potentiality to actuality. Teaching is nothing other than the pointing out of that path; teachers are the guides, their teaching is the guidance, and learning is the journey itself. The object of knowledge is the pointed-out destination.

The souls of children and students are knowing in potentiality, and the souls of teachers are knowing in actuality. Every soul that is knowing potentially requires a soul that is knowing in actuality to lead its potentiality forth into action.

<note>Vocabulary: Soul, Substance, Cognitive power, Effective power, Learning, Instruction, Product, Destination, Species-actions.</note>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the soul's cognitive and effective powers, this page explores the source of human knowledge and the hierarchy of learning. It moves from the mechanics of the individual soul to the metaphysical origin of all arts and sciences, tracing them back to the Universal Intellect and the Creator.</meta>

Every artist among men likewise requires a teacher from whom he learns his craft or his knowledge. This teacher, in turn, had another as a teacher, and that one another still, and so it continues back to one who did not receive his knowledge from any mortal. In the case of this last person, one of two things must have occurred: Either he produced it through the power of his soul—through his thoughts, intuition, or zeal, as the philosophers believe—or he received it from a non-human assistance, as the prophets claim.

Know, however, that no mortal—be he prophet, philosopher, or anyone else—masters any science except insofar as He, whose throne encompasses heaven and earth <note>This is a reference to the "Throne Verse" (Ayat al-Kursi) from the Quran (2:255), often cited in medieval texts to emphasize God's absolute sovereignty over knowledge.</note>, wills it. Namely, those who believe they have produced a science or art through the power of their own spirit, the excellence of their thinking, and their intuition, only achieve this by observing and witnessing the artworks of nature; they then reflect and draw conclusions, so that a "teaching of nature" takes place here, which points the true way toward something derived from her. Nature, however, receives her power from the <term>Universal Soul</term> <note>German: *Allseele*. In Neoplatonic thought, the Universal Soul is the bridge between the spiritual realm and the physical world, giving life and movement to the cosmos.</note>, and this in turn from the <term>Universal Intellect</term> <note>German: *Allvernunft*. Also known as the First Intellect, the first emanation from the Creator, containing the "forms" or blueprints of all things.</note>, the first existence which proceeded from the praised <term>Creator</term>. He strengthens the "all in the all" as He wills; He created the intermediary causes and strengthened the hearts of the insightful.

By considering human works of art—their material, their <term>accidents</term> <note>German: *Accidens*. In philosophy, "accidents" are properties that a thing possesses (like color or shape) which can change without the thing losing its essential nature.</note>, their sublimity, and their utility—we have thus shown that the highest stage to which a human can bring it is the establishment of the divine fundamental law. We presented the quality and conditions of this in the treatise on the <term>Nomos</term> <note>Greek for "Law." In this context, it refers to the Divine Law or the social and religious order that governs human behavior, discussed in Treatise XLVI of this collection.</note>, XLVI.

Strive, O reader, to grasp the secrets thereof; perhaps your soul will awaken from the sleep of carelessness, and you will escape in spirit from the sea of matter and the bonds of nature—the deep abyss of bodies that thus come into being and pass away.

# Psychology

## On the Various Characters

For these philosophers <note>This likely refers to the "Brethren of Purity" (Ikhwan al-Safa), a 10th-century secret society whose encyclopedic works combined Neoplatonism with Islamic theology.</note>, the discourse on the diversity of characters is closely related to another, the 24th treatise, in which the development of the child in the mother's womb—from the arrival of the **drop of seed** <note>original: "Saamentropfen." In medieval embryology, the "drop" refers to the initial fertilizing fluid, often interpreted through the Quranic concept of the *nutfa*.</note> until the day of birth—is described. It is emphasized how the spiritual powers of the **celestial bodies** <note>original: "Gestirne." This refers to the belief that the planets influenced the formation of the soul and body at different stages of pregnancy.</note> intervene during the creation of the embryo, thereby establishing the fundamental disposition of various characters in nature, which are then impressed upon the individual month by month over the course of nine months. This duration is, after all, the natural period until the birth of the small child and the "beginning" of the human being. 

The duration of natural life, by contrast, is said to be 120 years <note>A lifespan of 120 years was frequently cited in medieval Islamic and Jewish traditions as the ideal or maximal natural limit for human life, famously associated with the age of Moses.</note>. 

On the other hand, the intention here is to show which character traits are newly acquired only after birth through ongoing habits and the "instrumental causes" <term>instrumental causes</term> <note>German: *Mittelursachen*. In philosophical terms, these are the secondary factors—such as environment, education, or social circles—that God or Nature uses to bring about a specific result.</note> that arise and act upon them. These traits are added to that fundamental disposition, either enhancing it or diminishing it throughout the changes of life until the day of death.

Such development occurs until the day of death—that is, the separation of the soul from the body, which is the **second birth** or the **second beginning**.

Compare the Quran <note>original: "Kor."</note> 56:62: "You already know the first beginning; if only you would remember!" <note>original: "Ihr kennt schon den ersten Anfang, wenn ihr nicht eingedenk wärt!"</note>—this refers to the second beginning.

56:61 <note>The OCR lists 52:61, but the context and text match 56:61.</note>: "We brought you into being while you knew nothing." <note>original: "Wir liessen euch beginnen, während ihr nichts wusstet."</note>

29:20 <note>The OCR lists 29:19.</note>: "God brings about the other beginning; indeed, God has power over all things." <note>original: "Gott lässt euch den anderen Anfang beginnen, ja Gott ist aller Dinge mächtig."</note>

<summary>This page explains that human character is formed by a combination of innate traits established in the womb via celestial influence and traits acquired after birth through habit. It concludes by framing death not as an end, but as a "second birth" or "second beginning," supported by Quranic citations.</summary>

When God placed man upon the earth as His representative <note>original: "Stellvertreter." The Lord built for His representative a body of dust with wondrous craftsmanship, fine shape, various limbs, and many powers. Then He joined these together and formed him in the most beautiful shape of all creatures, so that he might be distinguished as His representative on earth and rule over it, acting freely within it as he willed. — Thereupon He breathed into him of His breath and united this body of dust with a spiritual soul, which is excellent and exalted above all animal souls. This happened so that through it, he might move, feel, perceive, know, and act as he willed.

Then God strengthened the human soul with the spiritual powers of all the celestial bodies in the firmament, so that he might be well-prepared and capable of adopting all characters and acquiring every **science and education**, every **spiritual exercise**, **knowledge and leadership**; just as God, through the variously shaped limbs and postures of the body, enabled him to perform all human **arts**, **deeds, and glorious actions**.

God united in the structure of the human form all **mixtures** of the four elements (temperaments)**) and all **nine blends**, so that man could adopt all the characters of creatures and the peculiarities of their nature. **This happened**, so that it would be easy for him and he would be ready to,

The purpose of all this is that man may be capable and well-prepared to become like God, his Creator; for he is His representative <note>original: "Stellvertreter." This translates the Arabic term *Khalifa* (Caliph or Vicegerent), implying that humans are God's stewards or deputies on Earth.</note> on the earth; he cultivates God's world and rules over all that is within it. Through this, he is capable of becoming one of the angels brought near to God <note>original: "Gott nahgestellten Engeln." This refers to the *al-Muqarrabun*, the highest rank of angels in Islamic tradition.</note> and attaining eternity in Paradise.

In the books of some of the prophets of Israel, God says:
> O Son of Adam, I have created you for eternity, for I am a living one who does not die. This is the famous "Kun fa-yakūn" (Be, and it is), a phrase used in the Qur'an to describe God's creative power. Here, it is attributed to a tradition from the Israelite prophets, a common practice in medieval Islamic literature (Isra'iliyyat) to find common ground between faiths.</note>

Through what has been mentioned thus far, the goal and intention is clear as to why there are various character traits in the fundamental disposition and nature of man. Now, however, the primary and intermediate causes shall also be stated—why and for what reason the characters and dispositions of man differ. Then it shall be shown how many of these there are, and what and how they are, now that the previous section has shown *why* they exist.

The characters and natures of men differ for four reasons:

1. Because of the mixtures of their bodies <term>mixtures: the "humors" or temperaments (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that were believed to determine health and personality</term> and the blending of these mixtures.
2. Because of the different qualities of the earth and the air in various regions.
3. Because man [is raised] in the statutes and the faith...

### **I. The Basis and Intermediate Causes of the Body’s Humors and the Alteration of their Mixture. Their Balance, Excess, and Deficiency, and the Resulting Diverse and Opposing Characters and Dispositions.**

People who are of a hot nature, especially in the mixture of the heart <term>mixture of the heart: In medieval Galenic medicine, the "mixture" or "complexion" (Latin: *complexio*) of the heart was believed to dictate a person's temperament and vitality.</term>, are mostly brave, generous, and bold in fearsome undertakings, yet they possess little persistence and patience in business; they are quick of movement and prone to violent anger, easily hopeful, but harbor little hatred, being sharp in thought and possessing a good imagination.

Those who are cold are mostly of a more sluggish disposition, of a coarse nature, of a ponderous spirit, and of an unreliable character.

Those of a moist mixture are mild, generous, good-natured, and quick to learn, yet also quick to forget.

Those of a dry mixture are mostly persistent in business and firm in their opinions; they are slow to accept things, possess both patience and envy, hold fast, preserve what they have, and are stingy.

In the books of the prophets of Israel <note>The text refers to the *Isrā'īliyyāt*, a body of traditions in Islamic literature derived from Jewish and Christian sources to provide more detail on biblical figures like Adam.</note>, the following description of the creation and origin of the human body is found:

> “When the glorious God called creation into being anew, He said: Truly, I have created Adam and joined his body from four things; these I established as an inheritance <note>original: "Erbtheil." Referring here to the biological and spiritual traits passed down to all of humanity.</note> for his children and his offspring. These take their beginning in their bodies and then continue to increase until the Day of Resurrection; I joined his body from the moist, the dry, the warm, and the cold, for I created him from dust and water and breathed into him spirit and soul.” Thus, dryness stems from the dust, and moisture from the water. “Then, after this, I placed four other types into the body as the pillars of the frame.” Only through these does the body exist, and each individual part exists only through the others. Of these, one is—

"Thereupon I brought the one to rest through the other and made the black bile the seat of dryness, placed heat into the yellow bile, moisture into the blood, and cold into the phlegm." <note>This quoted passage likely originates from a foundational medical or religious text—possibly of Arabic origin—describing the divine creation of the human "complexion" or biological balance.</note> — In every body in which these four mixtures <term>mixtures: Also known as "humors," these were the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that pre-modern medicine believed controlled health and temperament.</term> stand in such balance as support and preserver that each of them constitutes one quarter, neither more nor less, health is complete and the structure is in perfect proportion. If, however, one of them is in excess compared to the others, it becomes victorious and gains the upper hand, and then, from its side, illness seizes the body, specifically by as much as this mixture gained the advantage. — Likewise, if one of them suffers a deficiency and is too weak to endure, illness enters the body from its side, specifically by as much as it lacks the strength for its existence. The insightful physician knows very well from whence the evil came, whether from too little or too much, in order to supplement the lacking and take away the superfluous until the nature of the body is again well established and the four forces are in balance in their combination. Then those mixtures arise as nature and fundamental dispositions from which the body is joined, and upon them the characters of the children of Adam <note>Children of Adam: A common theological term in both Islamic and Christian traditions for humanity.</note> are founded. From the dust <note>original: "Staube." Here representing the element of Earth, associated with dryness and black bile.</note> comes firm resolve, from water comes gentleness, from heat comes intensity, and from cold comes serenity. If the dryness of the dust gains the upper hand, firm resolve becomes hardness and mercilessness; if the moisture of water gains the upper hand, gentleness becomes weakness and softness. If heat gains the upper hand, intensity becomes wild rage and folly, and with the predominance of cold, serenity becomes lethargy and powerlessness.

If, on the other hand, the mixtures are in balance, so too are...

### **11. The differences of soil and climate in the zones as the causes influencing the diversity of characters.**

The soil as well as the air (the climate) are manifoldly different in the various regions, villages, and cities. This difference depends on whether they lie in the south, north, east, or west; whether they are on high mountains or in deep valleys and hollows; whether they are on the sea coast, on the banks of rivers, or in desolate desert places, in thickets or in depressions; whether they are in saline lands or in stretches with rocks and stones, sand or pebbles; or whether they are in flat lands with mild soil, between streams, crops, trees, and gardens that shine with flowers and blossoms.

Furthermore, the climate of the regions and plots of land varies according to the change of the four winds and their inclinations, according to the rising of the zodiac signs <term>zodiac signs: *Sternzeichen*; in medieval science, the position of the stars over a specific region was believed to physically alter the air and the "humors" of the people living there.</term> over them and the radiation of the stars, as they strike them from their horizons. All of this affects the various mixtures in the temperaments <term>temperaments: *Temperamente*; referring to the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) which were thought to determine a person's physical and mental health.</term> of the inhabitants, and these in turn affect the character, nature, color, language, habit, opinion, on...

It then introduces a geographical and astrological theory of character, arguing that soil, climate, and the stars determine the physical and moral nature of different peoples.</summary>
