<meta>This page continues the discussion on how geographical location and climate influence the physical and moral characteristics of different peoples, expanding on the list of terrains mentioned at the end of the previous page.</meta>

...their activities and their arts, the manner of their arrangement and conduct, so that one does not resemble the other.

Every people is unlike another in some of the mentioned things and distinguishes itself from the others in this regard.

— For example, those people who are born in hot regions and grow up in this climate have heat as the primary component of their internal <term>bodily mixture<note>original German: "Körpermischung"; this refers to the medieval theory of humors, where the balance of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness determines a person's physical health and temperament.</note></term>.

Heat and cold are two opposites that never coincide in one state, at one place, and at one time; for when one comes forward, the other hides and veils itself, so that both may maintain their existence for all time—for both are products without independent existence and without permanence. As proof of this, consider the bodily mixture of the inhabitants of southern districts: the Abyssinians, the <term>Zanj<note>original German: "Zeng"; a historical term for peoples of the East African coast.</note></term>, the Nubians, the Sindhu, and the Hindus. In their climate, heat prevails because the sun passes over the zenith of these regions twice a year; thus their climate becomes hot and the air heated, the exterior of their bodies is scorched, their skin turns black, and their hair becomes thick as a result. Conversely, the interior of their bodies is cool; their bones and teeth become white, and their eyes, nostrils, and mouths become wide.

It is exactly the opposite with the inhabitants of the northern regions. The sun remains far from their zenith; it crosses it neither in winter nor in summer. Thus, cold gains the upper hand in these climates, and the skin of the inhabitants becomes white, their bodies remain fresh, their hair is sparse, and their eyes are narrow. The heat hides in the interior of their bodies; as a result, their bones and teeth become reddish, and their bravery and boldness are consequently great.

Due to such intermediate causes, and according to this analogy, the characteristics of the inhabitants of different regions are of an opposite nature. Just as the climates differ, these peoples also differ in character and temperament, as well as in most things and conditions.

**Vocabulary:**
Climate, bodily mixture, Abyssinians, Zanj, Nubians, Sindhu, Hindus, bravery, boldness, temperament

## III. The determining influences of the celestial bodies.

For those who are born under the **fire signs** and in the times in which the "fire stars" such as Mars, the **Lion’s Heart** <note>original: "Löwenherz"; refers to the star Regulus, the brightest star in the constellation Leo, traditionally associated with power and heat.</note> and similar stars rule, heat and the power of <term>yellow bile<note>Also called choler; one of the four humors, believed to make a person ambitious or quick-tempered.</note></term> are predominant.

For those who are born under the **water signs** and at the times in which "water stars" such as Venus and **Canopus** (Sirius) <note>Though Canopus and Sirius are different stars, they were both associated with the flooding of the Nile and seasonal moisture in various ancient traditions.</note> prevail, moisture and **phlegm** are predominant in the <term>bodily mixture<note>original: "Körpermischung"; the specific balance of the four humors within an individual that determines health and personality.</note></term>. Likewise, for those born under the **earth signs** and at the time in which Saturn and fixed stars similar to it rule, dryness and **black bile** <note>Also called melancholy; associated with a serious or somber temperament.</note> are predominant. Finally, for those born under the **air signs** and at the times when Jupiter and similar fixed stars rule, **blood** and a moderate temperature have the upper hand in the bodily mixture.

Those who occupy themselves with astrology and the hours of birth have clearly demonstrated this.

## IV. Concerning the character traits rooted in the innate disposition and those acquired through habit, the difference between the two, and the essence of character.

a) Character rooted in the **innate disposition** <note>original: "Grundanlage"; the natural physical and mental makeup a person is born with.</note> is a certain readiness in one of the limbs of the body, through which it becomes easy for the soul to produce a deed, an action, a craft, or to learn a branch of knowledge, a custom, an arrangement, or leadership without any special deliberation or observation.

If, for example, a person is **courageous by nature**, it becomes easy for him to undertake dangerous deeds without further reflection or deliberation. The same applies if he is **generous by nature**; then it becomes easy for him to give gifts without further deliberation or observation. Likewise, for one who is **chaste by nature**, it is easy to keep away from the forbidden, and if someone is **just by nature**, he practices justice without further ado. Thus it is with all characters and dispositions established in the innate makeup. These are only intended so that it is easy for the soul to produce ac-

Thus, it is true of all **modes of action** grounded in nature that for their opposite, a person requires **reflection** and **intuition**, **commands** and **prohibitions**, and **zeal** and **desire**. For this reason, most of the **commands** and **prohibitions** of the religious law <note>original: "Religionsgesetzes"; likely referring to the Sharia or divine law in an Islamic context.</note> appeared, and for this cause, **promise** and **threat**, **desire** and **preparation** arose. If a human being were naturally destined for all character traits, it would be no effort for him to accomplish all deeds and every art. The **absolute universal man** <note>This refers to the philosophical and mystical concept of *Al-Insān al-Kāmil*, the "Perfect" or "Universal Human," who embodies all divine attributes and serves as the bridge between God and creation.</note> is destined by nature to adopt all character traits and to produce all arts and actions; but this is not so for the **partial man** <note>The individual human being, who possesses only a fraction of the potential of the Universal Man.</note>.

**All humans** are individuals of this absolute man (as a species), but it is to this latter concept that the word of the **Koran** refers when it says that he is the (**Caliph**) <note>original: "Chalif"; meaning a successor, vicegerent, or representative of God on Earth.</note> representative of God on his **Earth**, specifically from the day of the **Creation of Adam**, the **father of humanity**, until the day of the great **Resurrection**. This is the **human World-Soul**, which resides in all individual humans (collectively); thus it is written in 31:28 <note>The text cites 31:27, but in modern numbering, this is verse 28.</note>:

> Your creation and your resurrection are but as a single soul — cf. *Awakening and Resurrection*, Section XXXVII.

Of this **universal absolute man**, of whom we said that he is the representative of God on His earth, it is true that he is destined by nature to adopt all human **characters**, all human **science**, and every wise **art**; he is likewise present at all hours and times within all individuals (collectively). From him proceed all **deeds**, **sciences**, **characters**, and **arts**; however, among individuals, some are more or less **prepared** for this or that art, science, or this or that...

b) Persistent, enduring **habits** strengthen the character traits that correspond to them, just as the study of the **sciences** and constant **research**, **learning**, and **memory** strengthen those same faculties for **shrewdness** and **certainty**. Similarly, the constant practice of an **art** and becoming accustomed to it increases one’s insight and **mastery** in it. The same principle applies to all **characters** and **dispositions**.

**Boys**, when they grow up among brave **knights** and **warriors**, very often adopt their character as if it were their own nature and become like them. Other boys, however, who grow up among **weaklings** <note>The German "Weichlingen" refers to those lacking physical or moral fortitude, often contrasted in medieval texts with the "knightly" ideal.</note> and **women**, adopt their character—if not in its entirety, then at least in specific details—as if it were a natural trait. This is the case with all **characters** and **dispositions**; from their childhood, children adopt the characters of their **fathers** and **mothers**, **brothers** and **sisters**, **companions** and **friends**, **teachers** and **masters**, according to whom they encounter as their circumstances change. The same holds true for **opinions**, **methods of teaching**, and **beliefs** as a whole.

For one part of humanity, **faith** follows their **character**; for others, their **character** appears as a consequence of their **faith**. The **soul** of one who has remained true to their **original nature** <note>original: "Urnatur"; likely referring to the concept of *fitra*, the innate human disposition toward virtue and the divine as described in Islamic philosophy.</note> inclines toward those **opinions** and **teachings** in which **abstinence**, **chastity**, and **mildness** predominate. Accordingly, most of the opinions and teachings of men follow their inherent characters. However, that **character** then follows **faith** is due to the fact that when someone holds an opinion or follows a teaching and testifies to its truth, most of their character traits and dispositions become similar to that teaching and that faith; for such a person devotes his greatest care to bringing his teaching to victory and to proving his faith true in all his conduct, so that it becomes his very **character** and **disposition**...

## A Narrative <note>original: "Eine Erzählung"</note> *)

It is told that once, two people met while on a journey; one was a Magian <term>Magian: a follower of Zoroastrianism, often referred to in Arabic texts as "Majus"</term> from Kirman <note>A historic city in southeastern Iran</note> and the other was a Jew from Ispahan <note>Isfahan, a major city in central Iran</note>. The Magian rode a mule and had everything required for the journey—provisions, supplies, and clothing—and traveled in comfort. The Jew, by contrast, went on foot and had neither provisions nor supplies. As they were both engaged in conversation, the Magian asked the Jew about his faith and his doctrine. He replied: "I believe that in this heaven there is a God whom the children of Israel serve, as do I; to Him I pray and toward Him I strive. From Him comes abundance, long life, and health, protection from misfortune, and victory over enemies. I hope for the salvation of my soul from Him for myself and for those who agree with me in faith and doctrine. But regarding those who do not agree with me, I do not think of them; I believe, rather, that the possessions and the blood of those who are my opponents in faith and doctrine are at my disposal, and it is even forbidden for me to grant them counsel, assistance, or mercy."

Thereupon the Jew asked the Magian about his faith and his doctrine. The Magian replied: "My faith and my view is this: that I desire good for myself and all my fellow men alike; I wish no evil upon any creature, whether he be of my faith or not."

<meta>This page continues the parable introduced at the end of page 113. While the previous page established the Jew's exclusionary faith (caring only for his own kind), this page demonstrates the Magian’s universalist ethics—which emphasize doing good even to those who harm you—and the immediate, harsh consequences of these two conflicting worldviews in a desert setting.</meta>

"Even if he treats you unjustly and sins against you?" asked the Jew.
"Yes," the Magian replied.
"And why is that?" asked the Jew.
Magian: "Because I know that in this heaven there is a good, excellent, righteous, wise, and all-knowing God, from whom nothing of the things of His creation <note>original: "Creatur"</note> remains hidden; He rewards the good for their good deeds and the wicked for their malice."

"But I do not see," said the Jew, "that you follow your own teaching or prove your faith."
Magian: "How so?"
Jew: "I, too, am one of your fellow creatures <note>original: "Deinesgleichn" - literally "one of your kind" or "your equal"</note>, and yet you see me walking, tired and hungry, while you ride, are sated, and travel in comfort."
Magian: "You are right. What would you have me do?"
Jew: "Give me food and drink, and let me ride for a little while."

So the Magian dismounted from his mule, opened his travel bag, and gave him enough food and drink; then he let him mount the animal and walked beside him for a while as they conversed. But once the Jew was riding and noticed that the Magian was growing tired, he spurred the mule ahead of him. The Magian began to run, but he could not catch the Jew. Then he called out: "Stop! Get down, for I am tired!" But the Jew replied to him: "Did I not tell you my doctrine and my faith, just as you stated yours to me? I now wish to uphold my doctrine and prove my faith <note>The Jew's doctrine, implied from the previous page, suggests he is only obligated to be good to those within his own faith.</note>." With that, he drove the mule quickly forward. The Magian ran after him for a time and cried: "Woe to you! Please stop and take me with you; do not leave me in this desert where wild beasts will devour me and I shall die of hunger and thirst. Have mercy on me, as I did on you!"

But the Jew did not heed his calls, nor did he look back at him, until he had ridden away and vanished from the Magian’s sight. But as the Magian despaired— <note>The original German "verzei-" is truncated; it likely completes as "verzweifelte" (despaired).</note>

...exhausted and near to perishing, he remembered his faith, which he had professed: that in this heaven there is a kind, excellent, all-knowing God, from whom nothing regarding the things of His creation <note>original: "Creatur"</note> is hidden. Then he lifted his head to heaven and spoke:
> God, You know that I hold this faith and prove it; I made it known as You heard it; so prove upon the Jew the truth of that which I confessed.

After this, the Magian walked only a little further, and there he saw the Jew, whom the mule had thrown off and struck in the neck, while the animal itself stood far from him, awaiting its master.

When the Magian had come to his mule, he rode on his way and left the Jew behind. But the Jew was overcome by the fear of death and called out to him:
> Have mercy on me and take me with you; oh, do not leave me in the desert, where wild beasts will devour me or hunger and thirst will kill me; oh, do prove your faith and confirm your teaching.

Then the Magian replied: "I did that twice already, but you did not understand what I said to you and did not recognize what I made known to you." But the Jew replied: "How should I have? I did state my teaching to you, yet you did not believe my word to be true until the deed confirmed it for you."

"But why," asked the Magian, "did you not understand what I said to you, and what prevented you from allowing yourself to be admonished by my word?" "What prevented me," answered the Jew, "was the faith in which I was raised and the teaching which I held to be true; through the length of time and frequent practice, it became habit and disposition <note>original: "Anlage" – referring to one's inherent character or a deeply ingrained tendency</note> to me. Since I imitated my father and my mother, the masters and teachers of my religion, in their teaching, it became my basic disposition and second nature. It is quite difficult to let go of it and to renounce it."

Then the Magian took pity on him and brought him to the city to hand him over to his relatives. The Magian told of his encounter with the Jew to others, who marveled at it. "How could you forgive him," they said, "after he treated you so poorly?" But he ans—

---

We had seen that regarding the diversity of souls <note>original: "Seelenverschiedenheit"</note>, there are only four categories that serve as the primary and intermediate causes for their characters.

### Classification of Characters.

We maintain that characters all fall into two types:
a) imprinted upon the primal foundation of the souls and established within it,
b) acquired through ongoing habit and frequent application.

In another respect, characters further divide into:
a) original ones (roots) and basic forms,
b) branches and consequences derived from those.

One must distinguish the one from the other and recognize the one before the other; this recognition belongs to the most sublime and useful science.

When the Creator created the souls and allowed all that exists to emerge from the hidden, He arranged and ranked them according to the levels of the Units <note>original: "Stufen der Einer" — likely referring to a Neoplatonic or Pythagorean hierarchy of numbers or monads</note>; compare the speech of the angels in the Quran, Surah 37, Verse 164: "There is not one of us but has a known station; and indeed, we are those who range ourselves in order and glorify Him" <note>original German: "Es giebt keinen unter uns, er habe denn eine bekannte Stätte, fürwahr wir ordnen (die Dinge) und preisen ihn"</note>. The number of souls is so great that only God can count them; compare Surah 74, Verse 31 <note>The source text cites 74, 34, but this verse is traditionally numbered 31</note>: "None knows the hosts of your Lord except He alone."

---

We shall highlight some of the generic levels and statuses of souls, since no human can know their specific types and individuals; only God knows them.

Of the levels of the soul, there are only three kinds:

a) the level of the human soul, b) those standing above it, c) those standing below it.

Below it stand seven levels and just as many above; that makes fifteen in total. Five of these fifteen levels can be easily grasped by any person of understanding; these are the two below the human level: a) that of the animal soul <note>original: "Thierseele"</note>, and b) below that, the plant soul <note>original: "Pflanzenseele" — also known as the vegetative soul in classical philosophy</note>. These have been well-considered by sages, philosophers, and physicians. Above the human soul stand a) the soul of wisdom <note>original: "Weisheitsseele"</note>, and b) the soul of the original law (the Prophetic soul) <note>original: "Urgesetzliche (Prophetische)"</note>.

The Quran (95:4) speaks of the human level: "We have created man in the best of stations." The level above it is characterized by 12:22: "And when he (referring to the man—though in the Surah the subject is actually Joseph) reached his maturity, we granted him wisdom and knowledge." Furthermore, 6:122: "Is he who was dead and whom we then gave life, and for whom we made a light to walk with among the people, like him who is in darkness from which he cannot emerge?" This means we have brought the soul to life with the light of guidance. This is the level of the scholars, the knowing, and the certain.

Above that stands the level of the prophetic souls, who dedicated themselves to the divine original law. The Quran (58:11 <note>The German text cites 58:12, but this verse corresponds to 58:11 in modern numbering</note>) speaks of them: "God raised those of you who believed and those to whom knowledge was granted, by degrees."

This level stands close to the level of the holy angels. These five levels <note>The vegetative, animal, human, wise, and prophetic souls</note> can be well understood by man, but the recognition of the level above the prophets—namely that of the holy angels—is difficult to grasp even for those practiced in theology, let alone for others.

It is now our task to show what specific help and strengthening is granted by God to each of these souls.

We must now highlight the help and strengthening that pertains to each kind of soul.

First, we must mention the desires residing within the basic nature <note>original: "Grundnatur" — referring to the primordial or biological essence of a being</note> and the natural forces that assist it; for these are the root and the fundamental norm for all powers, characters, dispositions, deeds, and movements, as well as for all feeling and knowledge.

Of these characters and powers, some relate to the spirited **animal soul**, others to the appetitive **plant soul**, still others to the understanding **human soul**, others to the rational **soul of wisdom**, and yet others to the angelic or **soul of the original law** <note>The "soul of the original law" refers to the highest prophetic state of being</note>.

I. First, the disposition of the desire for nourishment—the craving for food and drink and that which is longed for; the desire and zeal to achieve these even through the endurance of toil and pressure; then joy and delight upon finding them, and rest and pleasure once they are reached and seized; followed by satiety upon being satisfied, and a dread and reluctance toward harm. — As for the specific forces belonging to this soul, they include: the attracting, the holding, the fermenting <note>original: "gährende" — in this context, the digestive or transformative process</note>, the expelling, the nourishing, the growth-granting, and the form-giving forces. Regarding knowledge and discernment, the plant soul possesses the recognition of the six directions <note>The six spatial directions: up, down, front, back, left, and right</note>; in terms of actions, it possesses the extension of roots toward these sides, reaching for moisture and soft soil; furthermore, it includes the turning of branches, boughs, and shoots toward these six directions, as well as the aversion to and turning away from narrow spaces and harmful bodies.

All these dispositions are found within the basic nature and they operate without further reflection and without the observation of man; all these are aids and strengthenings provided by nature for souls to pursue their desires, to
*attain their benefit and avoid their harm.*

For their desire is precisely the nourishment of their body, and the material for their existence is the instrumental cause <note>original: "Mittelursache" — a cause that acts as a means to an end</note> for their preservation. II. They also have memory and recollection to recognize what is similar or dissimilar to them, as well as the power to guard themselves from harm and to flee from the enemy. All of this is grounded in the primordial nature <note>original: "Urnatur"</note> of animals, which stands in a closer relationship to man. — The drive to mate is rooted in this primordial nature for the sake of procreation, and procreation serves to preserve the form <note>In classical philosophy, the "form" is the eternal blueprint of a species, while the "matter" of individual bodies is temporary</note> throughout successive individuals, for matter is constantly in flux and never remains the same for a single moment. — The desire for vengeance <note>original: "Rachegier" — here used in a biological sense of a defensive-aggressive response to threats</note> is established in the primordial nature to ward off injuries that would destroy their physical structure. This occurs in four ways: first, through force and overcoming; second, through flight and escape; third, through the fortification (of the camp or lair); and fourth, through deception and cunning; cf. the *Treatise on Animals*, XXI.

Leadership is established in the primordial nature to strengthen governance, for only when a leader is present is governance complete. Governance, however, aims for the well-being of all existing beings and the preservation of the most excellent state and the highest goal.

Then there is the magnificent **equipment** of the human **senses** and the wonderful **way** of **grasping** the **perceptible**; cf. sensory perception, Section XXIII.

### The Fundamental Desires for Each Genus of Souls.

**Common to all souls** is the **drive to maintain oneself in the most perfect state** and **in the highest perfection**, just as the **aversion to waning and deficiency** of the most excellent and perfect state is inherent to all.

Upon closer investigation into the **origins of being** and the **primal ground of existence**, it becomes clear that these two states—the **instinct for self-preservation** and the **fear of annihilation**—are the **root** and **fundamental rule** for all **desires of the soul** lying within its basic nature. These desires of the soul are, in turn, the **root and fundamental rule** for **characters and dispositions**, and these again are the **root and fundamental rule** for all **actions**, **deeds**, **arts, and knowledge**. However, those two states lie within the **basic nature** of all existing things and all being because the **Creator** is the **primal ground of everything present** and the **cause of all being**; He permitted the same—

God, however, is of eternal endurance; no transience ever touches Him, and for this reason, the love and drive for existence, and the aversion and hatred toward perishing, lie in the fundamental nature of all that is. For in the fundamental nature of the **caused** <term>the created world or "effect"</term> there are always found some characteristics of the **Cause** <term>the Creator</term> so as to point toward it. The Creator is never affected by fading or perishing, because His existence rests in His own essence and His endurance is grounded in His soul. 

By contrast, in everything else that exists and has being, existence has a foundation and **intermediary causes**. If any of this is missing or if something within them is deficient, it is affected by perishing and fading and cannot reach the most perfect state. All plants and animals may serve as an example of this. If they lack nourishment, which is the matter for their bodies and the substance for their existence, they perish and spoil; they change and wither away. It is exactly the same, however, with souls: if their substance is lacking—that is, if their knowledge and perception are deficient—they can manifest neither work nor effect. These souls do indeed exist, but they are in a deficient state.

On the other hand, it is clear from the **principles of reason** that existence in the most excellent state is lovelier, more sublime, and more excellent than existence in a deficient state. The Sages and Philosophers <note>This likely refers to the "Falafisa," the Islamic Neoplatonists who synthesized Aristotelian logic with theology.</note> have stated that every evil is loathed because of the good, but the good is sought for the sake of its own essence; the pure good, however, is (true) **happiness**, and happiness is sought for itself and not for the sake of anything else. Happiness is divided, however—as we presented in the treatise on faith—into two types: 
a) the **earthly**, 
b) the **future**. 

Earthly happiness consists in this: that every existing soul remains as long as possible in the most excellent state and in the most perfect completion; future happiness, however, is that which a...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on human character, moving from the physical composition of the body (the four humors) to the moral and spiritual implications of human action. ...of the reason why they must exist; in that case, they are called good. In the opposite case, they are called evil.

If their possessor exercises them with free choice and free will as is required, at the proper time, for the proper reason, and in the proper measure, then he is to be praised; in the opposite case, however, he is to be blamed. If his free choice and free will are combined with reflection and contemplation as we have described, the man is philosophical, wise, and excellent. In the opposite case, he is called foolish, ignorant, and base.

If his actions, will, and choice, as well as his reflection and observation, are commanded by the Primordial Law <term>Urgesetz: likely referring to the Divine Law or Sharia in the original Arabic context</term> and well-prepared by it—furthermore, if he does what is necessary, in the way it is necessary, and as that law requires—then the man will be rewarded and recompensed. In the opposite case, he will be punished for it.

From what has been mentioned so far, it is clear that the desires lying within the basic nature <term>Grundnatur</term>, the characters <note>dispositions or "akhlaq"</note> emerging from them, the actions that follow them, and all transformations exist only so that souls may be in their most excellent state, and that every kind of soul may reach its highest perfection and its ultimate goal.

Since the Creator ordered the souls according to their levels, He did this in accordance with the order of the Units <note>A reference to the mathematical or Monadic order of creation</note>; thus His wisdom required it. He placed their beginning in connection with their end, and joined their end to their beginning; the middle stages were then connected with both of these, so that the lower level might always be able to rise to the one above it, and all might reach their ultimate goal and completion. — God placed the **plant soul** <term>vegetative soul: responsible for growth and nutrition</term> below the **animal soul** as its servant; the animal soul likewise below the **rational human soul**; this, in turn, below the **wise rational soul**; and this one beneath the soul of the **Primordial Law**, which itself stands below the **angelic soul**. From this final level, the soul rises, and if it allows itself to be led by its guide, it becomes increasingly similar to that guide, reaches its height, and becomes like it in action. In the same way, after all, every *apprentice or student in science and art becomes like his teacher.*

Among the animal souls, the one most worthy to pass over to the human **level** is that which serves the same, **accustoms** itself to man, and lets itself be guided by him, as it **dedicates itself** to his service; this applies especially to the souls of those animals **which** are slaughtered as sacrifices. Among the human **levels**, the one worthy to ascend to the angelic level is that which, **in** the commands and prohibitions of the Primordial Law <term>Urgesetz: likely referring to the Sharia or Divine Law</term>, is subservient to that law, which lets **itself** be guided by its decrees, and well respects the elements **of the same**.

In their conduct in worldly affairs, human beings consist of so many ranks and levels that God **alone** knows their number.

cf. 71:13: He has created **you** in various ways, yet they are **all** encompassed by seven stages\*). <note>Original: "atwār". This refers to Quran 71:14: "While He has created you in stages."</note>

Some pursue crafts and trades; others are in the **commerce** and dealings of business; still others build, **cultivate**, and have property; yet others are kings, **rulers**, soldiers—they understand leadership. Others must bustle about day after day as devoted servants. Others live in **filth**, without education or occupation. Finally, others are people of religion and science, devoted to the Primordial Law. Each of these seven groups is divided into many **sub-types**; each has its own characters, natures, dispositions, and skills, which they have acquired through their work and which their conduct requires. None of them is **equal** to the other; only God may count them.

**The characters, dispositions, education, and science of the men of religion who hold to the Basic Law and preserve its elements.**

Through the practice of the Basic Law <term>Grundgesetz: another term for Divine Law</term>, one escapes **destruction**. cf. Cor. 12, 108: This is my way; I call to... <note>original: "Kor. 12, 108". Refers to Quran 12:108: "Say, 'This is my way; I invite to Allah with insight...'"</note>

**VII. Rank.** The abstinent ones, the servants in the places of prayer, monks and overseers of temples, and pulpit orators.

### VIII. Rank. 
The scholars for the explanation of revelation; they are secure in the divine sciences and glorious knowledge; they know the veiled secrets of the Basic Law. These are the rightly-guided Imams and the righteous Caliphs <note>original: "Chalifen"; successors or deputies of the Prophet</note>, who judge justly.

If one examines these ranks and their relationship to each other closely, one recognizes that the Basic Law, along with all its followers and their deeds, is a spiritual kingdom. Its founder sits upon his throne, and those guided by his commands and prohibitions are its bearers. They praise the Lord and pray for the earth. Every higher rank is to the lower like the heaven, and the lower is to it like the earth. Each of these eight ranks, each representing a pillar of the Basic Law, must possess certain conditions, good qualities, and character traits.

**I.** The readers and those who keep the Basic Law in their memory must have eloquent words, good delivery, and methods of chanting and reporting; they must retain information quickly, understand well, and not forget. They must be very careful not to add or omit anything. They must love the truth, yet avoid lies, and preserve the narrative well, so that it may pass on to those who ask for it and so that anything improper is kept away. ### III. 
The legal scholars, judges, and muftis <term>Mufti: a professional jurist who interprets Muslim law and provides legal opinions</term> require for their conduct, first of all, the basic rules such as those the Founder of the Law <note>original: "Grundgesetzgeber"; referring to the Divine source of the Sharia</note> has granted in command and prohibition, in statute and custom, in voluntary works, and in what is permitted and forbidden. Then they require definitions and judgments, analogy <term>Analogie: likely referring to 'qiyas', the process of legal deduction in Islamic jurisprudence</term>, and the derivation of secondary provisions from primary ones, in order to answer the questions that arise which are not contained within the basic provisions. — When issuing a legal decision, the scholar must grasp the question at hand with all its conditions, allow little room for doubt, and establish the definitions. He must not quarrel with his peers, nor be envious of his opponents; he must give sincere counsel to his brothers and be kind toward the ignorant. If he finds the right path, he must not behave proudly, nor maliciously revile the mistakes of other scholars; he must calmly endure the harm that the unjust inflict upon him. He must not long for the things of the world; he must be abstemious and live according to the Basic Law <term>Grundgesetz: the foundational religious law or Sharia</term>, so that his actions do not contradict his word.

### IV. 
The interpreters of revelation must first know the goal of Him who gave the Basic Law through the sending down of revelation. They must be well-acquainted with the use of words with different meanings and possess an exact knowledge of the usage of words and sayings, and which meanings they permit. This indeed reinforces the goal that God had for the Basic Law. Such a person must explore its depths to grasp the meanings and explain them well, so that the spirit of the listener may comprehend them and they may be brought close to the understanding of the students. With a watchful heart, he must take care that he is not deficient—neither in the words and presentations nor in the given meanings—when explaining the Basic Law; for if the interpreter is ignorant of the actual goal that matters in the Basic Law, he leads listeners and students toward a false sense and gives, without knowing it,

### V. 
The helpers and warriors of the Founder of the Law <note>original: "Grundgesetzgeber"</note> must defend the mountain passes and borders of those who follow Him; even if the protection of the faith becomes difficult for them, they must keep the enemies of God at bay. For this, they require a brave soul, speed in movement, and vigilance against the cunning of the enemy. They must guard against carelessness and achieve victory through strategy and, as far as possible, without bloodshed. Pushing forward boldly against the enemy, they must endure in battle and call upon God for help. They must guard against flight and shame, must not lust after loot, and must not dishonor women upon victory. They must thank God and show compassion toward prisoners, as well as make peace as soon as atonement and peace are offered. Finally, they must not become arrogant when they have much help and support.

### VI. 
The ascetics <term>Ascetics: those who practice self-denial for spiritual reasons</term> and servants of God remind humanity of the world to come and of the return to God. They must be content with little from this world, and their souls must not be deceived by desires nor strive greatly for the satisfaction of their needs. Instead, they are to occupy themselves with knowledge, divine service, fasting, and prayer alongside their own people. They must not associate with the greedy, but rather live in solitude and frequently reflect upon death and the transience of earthly things. They must look upon the past and allow themselves to be cautioned by it. They must study the books of the scholars and the wise, and proclaim the accounts of the conduct of earlier rebellious kings. They are to reflect on the sayings of experienced scholars regarding the way of the world, carefully consider the incidents of fate, and find their happiness in the certain return to God and the longing for the other world, where they shall one day dwell with the pure.

**VII. The Deputies (Caliphs) of the Lawgiver.** <note>original: "Grundgesetzgeber", referring to the founder of the religious law, likely the Prophet Muhammad.</note> They are divided into two classes: a) his representatives in the government and leadership of the world, and b) the rulers and leaders who follow the manifest decisions of the Fundamental Law <term>Fundamental Law: likely referring to the Sharia or the divinely revealed legal framework</term> regarding his followers. We have written a special article on this subject.

**VIII. The Deputies for the secret decrees of the Fundamental Law.** We mean the rightly-guided Imams <term>Imams: in this context, divinely guided spiritual leaders who possess inner knowledge of the law</term> and true Caliphs <term>Caliphs: literally "successors," referring to the heads of the Muslim community</term>, whose qualities, character, knowledge, and ways we described in the 51st section.

->How the followers of the Fundamental Law fare in the other world.<-

The transformation of the followers of the Fundamental Law in the other world is the ultimate goal in the establishment of that law, as well as in the traditions of the prophetic religion.

Every thing existing in this world is either visible like the shell and bone, or hidden like the heart and marrow <note>A metaphor for the "Zahir" (outward/exoteric) and "Batin" (inward/esoteric) aspects of reality</note>.

Since humanity has existed, the Fundamental Law has been present in the world. It has visible decrees and clearly defined boundaries. These are known to the people of the religious law as well as to the scholars of legal decrees, whether they have only a general or a specialized knowledge of them.

But then there are also secret and hidden decrees and boundaries, which are manifest only to those who possess specialized knowledge of them.

The Fundamental Law is an establishment (creation) for the welfare of the faith and the world as a whole.

This world and the other world are two realms standing in opposition to one another. While the names of both are related to each other, their meaning, their true sense, and their qualities stand in opposition and are contrary to one another. The one is, so to speak, the shell—that is, this world; the other is like the heart and marrow—that is, the other world.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the human journey begun on the previous page. Having discussed the roles of the spiritual leaders (Caliphs and Imams) who guide the community, the text now pivots to a metaphysical explanation of the "Two Worlds"—the earthly life and the hereafter—and why human existence in this world is inherently temporary.</meta>

- 132 -

This shall be demonstrated here in more detail, as it belongs to the most sublime of the sciences.

The word *Dunjā* <term>Dunjā: the Arabic term for the temporal world or earthly life</term>, this world, is derived from the verb *danā* <note>original: "danā"</note>, meaning to be near or close; *ākhira* <term>ākhira: the Arabic term for the life hereafter or the next world</term>, however, is from *akhira* <note>original: "aḥira"</note>, meaning to be later or distant. Accordingly, the true sense of the first is: the transformation of things as they encounter a person from the day of the body’s birth until the day of death—the birthday of the soul and its separation from the body. The meaning of the second word, on the other hand, is: the transformation of things that encounter a person from the day of death, i.e., the separation of the soul from the body, into the furthest eternity.

God calls the life of this world an accident <term>Accidens: a philosophical term for a temporary or non-essential quality that does not last</term>, a temporary benefit <note>original: "Niessbrauch", likely referring to the Quranic concept of 'matā‘', or the fleeting enjoyment of worldly goods</note>, because human existence in this world is a coincidence that affects him on the path to the next world. It is not the goal or intention for man to dwell long within it, just as it is not the primary intention for an embryo to remain long in the mother's womb; rather, life is only a path, a transition. — Just as a human cannot descend into this world without dwelling for a while in the mother’s womb for the completion of his structure and the perfection of his form, so too must his being in this world last for a time, for it is a path and a transition to that existence. Compare "Man as a Microcosm," Section XXV, and on "Death," Section XXVIII.

Thus, it is proclaimed from the pulpits during festivals and assemblies: "Know, O people, that you were created for eternity, yet you are being transferred from one place to another. From the loins (of the father) to the mother’s womb, from there into the world, from there to the intermediate realm <term>intermediate realm: the "Barzakh," a state of existence between death and the final resurrection; translated here by the author as "Fegefeuer" or Purgatory</term>, and from there to Paradise or to Hell."

See Quran 23:117: *Do you then think that we created you only as a joke and that you would not return (to God)?* <note>The verse numbers in older translations often differ slightly; this is likely Quran 23:115.</note> Furthermore, 28:83: *The abode of the next world we designated for those who did not seek high status on earth, nor instigated corruption; the good end belongs to those who guarded themselves.* Thus, there are many more passages concerning the abstention from this—

world; if only people knew the excellence of the other world and strove toward it!

God calls the other world life (*haiwån* <term>haiwān: An Arabic term meaning "living" or "animal life," used here in a philosophical sense to denote the realm of pure, eternal vitality</term>), because it is the world of spirits and the foundation (the treasure-trove) of souls. This world, however, is called the corporeal world. The substances of the body are dead by their very nature; it is only the souls and spirits that grant them life by being within and alongside them—just as the sun grants light and radiance to the air by radiating upon and into it.

The proof that souls grant life to bodies through their connection with them lies in the fact that, before death, bodies possess feeling, movement, knowledge, sound, and change; yet they lose these same things at death, which is nothing other than the separation of the soul from the body. This is not hidden from any rational person who draws the necessary conclusions. Most of those who followed and assisted the givers of the Fundamental Law <term>Grundgesetz: Likely referring to the *Nāmūs* or Divine Law brought by prophets</term> are connected to the future world and believe in it. However, they do not know what it actually is; they know neither its true meaning, nor its quality, nor its location, nor even when they shall arrive there. Likewise, many philosophers are closely connected to the spirit-world and the substances of the soul, yet they do not know how one should strive for it or how it is to be reached. We have sought to satisfy both parties in the treatises regarding the Fundamental Law and regarding Reason.

Having thus shown what this world and what the other world are, we now assert that all people are children of that world and its inhabitants; however, they are divided—just as is the case with the inhabitants of this world—into two groups: the fortunate and the unfortunate.

The fortunate and the unfortunate of this world are well-known and require no further mention, yet the signs, qualities, characters, views, and deeds of <margin>the</margin> <note>original: "er", reconstructed as "der" (the)</note> unfortunate of that world must be highlighted here, <margin>since</margin> <note>original: "3", reconstructed as "da" (since/as)</note> they are not known to everyone.

<meta>This page continues the categorization of human souls based on their relationship with the physical and spiritual realms. a) Happy in this world and the next together.
b) Happy in this world, unhappy in the next.
c) Unhappy in this world and the next together.
d) Unhappy in this world, yet happy in the next.

a) Happy in this world and the next are those who have a full share of money, possessions, and health, yet content themselves with little and use their abundance as a benefit for their souls in the other world; compare <note>Quran</note> 73:20: *Whatever good you send ahead for yourselves, you will find it with God;* and so many other verses of the Quran.

b) Happy in this world, yet unhappy in the next, are those who possess a full share of these goods and take excessive pleasure in them, becoming proud and greedy, and who do not concern themselves with the admonition of the Fundamental Law <term>Fundamental Law: Translated from the German *Grundgesetz*, referring to the *Nāmūs* or Divine Law revealed to prophets</term> regarding its commands and prohibitions. They have exceeded the proper measure. But God does not love the corruptors; <note>Quran</note> 46:20: *You consumed your good things during your worldly life and took your pleasure in them,* etc. <note>original: "Ihr habt euer Gutes schon in eurem irdischen Leben genossen und dessen euch gefreut"</note>

c) Unhappy in this world, yet happy in the next, are those who experienced many sad fates during their long lives, who toiled and had many worries, enjoying none of the pleasures of this world, yet nevertheless well observed the commands of the Fundamental Law and did not stray from its provisions; compare <note>Quran</note> 39:10: *The steadfast will be paid their reward without measure.* <note>The author cites this as 39:13, but in modern numbering, it is usually 39:10.</note>

d) Unhappy in both this world and the next are those to whom a meager lot fell here, who lived with an exhausted body and a soul full of worry, achieving no happiness, yet who also did not heed the commands and prohibitions of the Fundamental Law and did not allow themselves to be guided by it. They lost both this world and the next, and they are in a manifest loss.

### The Characters of the Children of This World and the Characters and Dispositions of the Children of That World.

The characters and natures of the children of this world lie by *nature* in the basic disposition of human beings. They are to him

Men obey these and act accordingly just as animals do, pursuing the benefits of their bodies and avoiding anything that might harm them. <note>Quran</note> 47:13: *They eat as cattle eat, and the Fire is their dwelling.* <note>original: "Sie essen wie das Vieh es thut, die Hölle ist ihre Stätte." The characters of the "children of that world" <note>the spiritual or hereafter-focused individuals</note>, on the other hand, are acquired by them through zeal and effort, whether as a result of reason, reflection, and observation, or as a result of education through the divine Fundamental Law. <term>Fundamental Law: Translated from the German *Grundgesetz*, referring here to the *Nāmūs* or the revealed Divine Law/Sharia.</term> Through the passage of time and frequent application, this character becomes a habit; they are recompensed and rewarded accordingly; compare <note>Quran</note> 53:40: *Nothing belongs to man except that for which he strives, and his striving will surely be seen.*

Upon closer observation and reflection, one finds that most of the commands and prohibitions, decisions and provisions, warnings and threats of the Fundamental Law are contrary to the inclinations for pleasure and enjoyment implanted in us by nature. Thus, when fasting, one is to avoid food and drink despite hunger and thirst; in cold and frost, one is to perform prayers and forgo sleep; one is to restrain anger, be abstinent despite possessing wealth, have patience during heavy misfortune, find comfort in accidents, renounce the world, and the like—all of which are the exact opposite of what lies in the basic nature of man.

In the Tradition <note>Hadith</note> it is reported that the Prophet, when asked about the Quranic passage 7:198: *Practice forgiveness, command what is right, and turn away from the ignorant (the pagans),* replied: God has summarized the noblest of characters in this passage, of which there are seven. Forgiveness for the one who did wrong; kindness toward the one who sinned against you; union with the one who broke away from you; doing good to the one who caused you evil; sincere exhortation to the one who deceived you; pardon for the one who betrayed you; and mildness toward the one who provoked you.

These are the fundamental character traits of the noble ones who stand near to God; compare <note>Quran</note> 25:64: *The servants of the All-Merciful are those,*

<vocab>Prophet, Koranstelle (Quranic passage), Grundgesetz (Fundamental Law), Allerbarmers (All-Merciful), Charaktere (Character traits), Fasten (Fasting), Vernunft (Reason), Ueberlieferung (Tradition/Hadith)</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that while animalistic instincts are innate, true virtuous character is an acquired struggle against one's own nature through the guidance of Divine Law. So let one strive to belong to those for whom the good became a habit, so that it remains imprinted upon the soul for the other world; yet one should abhor the character traits of the Satanic companions.

When at death the soul leaves the body, of everything it possessed in the world, only the acquired character traits and the actions, knowledge, insight, and views corresponding to them follow it.

The character traits of the children of this world and their dispositions are grounded in their fundamental nature because they descended into this world ignorant and unprepared, and therefore these traits were bestowed upon them. The character traits of the children of that world <note>those focused on the spiritual life or the hereafter</note>, by contrast, are acquired and gained as habit through action, after the content of the Fundamental Law <term>Fundamental Law: The Divine Law or Nāmūs, providing a framework for moral conduct.</term> has become clear.

### **The acquired praiseworthy and blameworthy character traits.**

Praiseworthy character traits are those that correspond to Reason and its judgments; likewise, they correspond to the decisions and commands of the Fundamental Law. The opposite applies to the blameworthy ones. One must recognize these clearly, and in this regard, there are those who are "near" and "far," masters and subordinates. Just as it is impossible in the physical world for a man of the common people, without education and knowledge, to reach the court of kings, so too can no human enter the Kingdom of Heaven—the expanse of the spheres—without having purified his soul through right faith and true knowledge and having cast off the blameworthy character traits. The study of the *rationality found in the books of philosophical guidance* serves this purpose.

# The Characteristics of the Believers.

There are signs for the believers by which they may be recognized, and some of these shall be highlighted here.*)

Among the servants of God, there are those who stand particularly close to God. These are faithful, knowledgeable, and discerning; they interact with God with sincerity and certainty. They consider the state of their souls by day and night. It is as if they have God before their eyes and find their reward every hour; not for a single moment is it withheld from them. This is the "glad tidings" for them in this life, even before they reach the next. They recognize the retribution for evil deeds, which follows closely upon the works themselves; very little remains hidden from them.

**Quran 7:200:** <note>original: "Kor. 7, 200"</note> Truly, those who are mindful of God remember Him when a partisan of devils touches them; <note>In modern numbering, this is typically Quran 7:201. It describes the "Taqwa" or God-consciousness that allows a believer to see through temptation.</note> cf. also 15:42 and many other passages.

It is told that one of these servants of God, while on a pilgrimage, once passed by the cell of a monk on the peak of a mountain. He stopped opposite the cell and called out to him. Then the monk stretched his head out of the cell and spoke: "Who is there?"

**"One like you from the sons of Adam."**

**"What do you want?"**

**"How does one find the way to God?"**

**"Beyond the air."** <note>This likely refers to transcending the material world or the "breath" of worldly desires.</note>

**"What is the best provision for the journey?"**

**"The fear of God."** <term>Fear of God: Translating the German 'Gottesfurcht,' which corresponds to the Arabic 'Taqwa.' This is not mere terror, but a protective shield of piety and constant awareness of the Divine.</term>

**"Why do you distance yourself from people and hide yourself in this cell?"**

"Because I feared for my heart due to temptations, and because I worried about the confusion of my spirit that arises from bad company; also out of concern that my soul would find no rest from the deceit and malice of the world, I surrendered myself to religion and fled from them."

**"How do you followers of the Messiah find your interaction with—"**

They are tossed back and forth between opposites: light and darkness, frost and heat, rest and exhaustion, strength and weakness, life and death. God, however, wanted to lead us to a happiness without misfortune, to security without fear, health without illness, and to a life without death, so that we might dwell in a light without darkness. However, it was not possible for man, with this body of flesh—which is composed of opposing elements and mixtures—to attain these pure and lasting qualities. Therefore, divine providence, in accordance with its wisdom, decided upon a second beginning <term>second beginning: likely referring to the Resurrection or the spiritual rebirth through revelation</term>.

### Faith.

Nature surrounds us and our soul just as the mother’s womb surrounds the embryo and the shell surrounds the interior of the egg. This occurs for the purpose of completing and perfecting them, and furthermore to protect them from potential harms until the appointed time. When, after the completion of the structure and the perfecting of the form, the time of departure arrives, it moves its limbs, kicks with its feet, and strikes with its hands until the casing tears, the bands and supports that held it until now are loosened, and it can go forth. The chick in the egg does exactly the same.

This is now an analogy for the souls that renounce this world and wish to reach the spirit world from the physical world. We must exert ourselves to remove the blameworthy natural inclinations inherent in our basic nature, as these prevent souls from ascending from the world of transient existence to the celestial world <note>original: "Sphärenwelt," referring to the heavenly spheres of medieval cosmology</note>, the seat of souls.

Since it is not within the constitution of man to understand this glorious matter, God in His goodness sent prophets to teach such things in order to impel people toward them, whether willingly or unwillingly. This is the great grace which God bestows upon everyone collectively, and in which no one individual is specifically favored. — On the other hand, there are benevolences and graces that are [granted] only to individual servants for their zeal—

to come, while others are barred from them; for their striving did not resemble that of the others, since, although the grace and justice of God apply to all, the zeal of all is not equal.

When God sent prophets to the unbelieving nations, He only assigned to them what they could truly achieve in speech and in action. The first thing the prophets required was <term>Faith</term> <note>original: "Imān," the Islamic concept of sincere belief and conviction</note>, namely, that people should confess with their tongues what the prophets and the chosen ones told them about things hidden from their senses, and that they must refrain from considering those things to be untrue or incorrect. 
Compare Quran 7:158: "Say: O people, I am indeed the Messenger of God to you all (that is, Muhammad); to God belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, there is no god but Him; He gives life and brings death; so believe in God and His Messenger, the unlettered prophet who trusts in God and His words; follow him, perhaps you will be rightly guided."

Now, for whoever confesses and perseveres in it, their reward follows immediately in that God leads them to a certain light regarding the hidden things that were proclaimed to them; He frees them from the fear of falsehood and dissolves their doubts and confusion. Compare Quran 47:17 <note>The text cites 47:19, but the quote corresponds to verse 17 in modern numbering</note>: "For those who allow themselves to be guided, God increases their guidance," that is, their security and clarity.

Whoever confesses with the mouth but does not hold it as true in the heart begins to doubt and falls into confusion; this is pain for the heart and torment for the soul. But God wants to free those who confess the proclamation of the prophet from this torment. He therefore commanded them to do certain things and to refrain from others, and this was done to test them. Whoever accepts these commandments, fulfills them, and perseveres in them already has the reward here, in that their soul is guided in the right light and their breast is freed from doubt. But whoever turns away from this and does not keep the commandments falls into deception and fraud; for in their inner self, they harbor the opposite of what they confess. They are deceived by the promise, and their punishment is that they are tossed back and forth in doubt.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual progression of the soul. - 144 -

Compare Quran 9:78: "So God allowed hypocrisy to follow in their hearts until a day when they meet that which they cheated Him of in their promise"; compare also 6:110. God established commands and prohibitions to test the believers and made them the ground and cause for them to rise from one state to another until they reached the most perfect.

If a person reaches a level or degree and does not turn their back on it, but acts as they must, grants it its right, and fulfills its conditions, then God ordains for them, as a reward, to ascend to a higher and more exalted level. But whoever, through the power of grace, misjudges this level and is not thankful for it, nor desires to climb higher, their retribution is that they remain on that level and only <margin>80</margin> reach as far as their knowledge extends; the higher things lying beyond escape them, and that is their punishment. In many places in the Quran, God has specified the signs of the sincere believers—their deeds, characters, and dispositions—and likewise the signs of the hypocrites, deceivers, and doubters; such as Surah 9 and Surah 33. It is said of Umar ibn al-Khattab <note>Omar ibn Chattāb: the second Caliph of Islam (r. 634–644 AD), known for his piety and justice</note> that he commanded his people to read, learn, and remember these <term>Surahs</term> <note>Chapters of the Quran</note>, to maintain their souls as mentioned there, and to stay far from the doubters, hypocrites, and deceivers.

Let what is mentioned here serve us as a hint and analogy for our interaction with the Lord, if we wish to participate in His mercy, reach a higher level, and attain the highest goal.

Compare Quran 58:12 <note>In modern editions, this verse is usually numbered 58:11</note>: "But God raises those of you who believe and are endowed with knowledge by degrees."

### Knowledge. <note>original: "Das Wissen"</note>

God has commanded and forbidden many things to the believers, but among all the commands of religious law and the decisions of the fundamental law, there is none more excellent after the <term>Profession of Faith</term> <note>original: "Bekenntniß," likely referring to the Shahada</note>, nor more useful for the servant of God, than that of <term>Science</term> and study. <note>The German terms "Wissenschaft" (science) and "Studium" (study) here refer to the pursuit of *'ilm*, the sacred knowledge of divine truths and law.</note> Thus it is said...

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual ladder of the believer, where fulfilling the duties of one's current state leads to divine elevation, while stagnation or hypocrisy leads to spiritual punishment. It emphasizes that after the initial confession of faith, the pursuit of knowledge (Wissenschaft) is the highest and most beneficial command for a believer.</summary>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of spiritual growth and the acquisition of divine favor. While the previous page warned against the stagnation of hypocrisy and the importance of acting upon one's current spiritual level to ascend higher, this page introduces the specific role of knowledge (Wissenschaft) as the primary vehicle for that ascension, citing prophetic tradition to illustrate its sacred value.</meta>

A tradition from the Prophet <note>The Prophet Muhammad</note>, says that he spoke: Let yourselves be taught knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft"; here referring to the Arabic concept of *'ilm*, which encompasses religious, ethical, and natural learning</note>, for it belongs to God; to strive for it is an act of praise, and to research it is religious zeal; to teach the ignorant is an act of almsgiving. Knowledge is the path to the Good, a friend in loneliness, a companion in a foreign land, a shield against the enemy, and an ornament among friends. Through knowledge, God exalts nations; it is a guide that leads to God.

Even the angels desire to associate with those who revere knowledge; they touch them with their wings and plead for them in prayer. All things, whether wet or dry, even the fish in the sea, the wild and tame beasts of the field, and the very heavens and stars, are devoted to them. For knowledge revives the heart against folly, serves as a torch of light for the eyes against darkness, and acts as a strength for the body against weakness. Through it, the servant reaches the station of the free and of kings; through it, he attains eminence in this world and the next. Through knowledge, a person remains in obedience to God; through it, he serves his Lord, performs good deeds, and becomes one with God. Through it, he achieves mercy, attains happiness, and remains far from misery.

### The Scholars.

Study, however, requires seven qualities: **a)** questioning, **b)** listening with silence, **c)** reflection, **d)** acting according to it, **e)** striving for truth from within oneself, **f)** frequent remembrance that this knowledge is a grace from God, and **g)** not seeking to incite wonder at the good one performs (avoiding boasting).

Knowledge acquires ten praiseworthy qualities for the one who possesses it: **a)** eminence, even for the lowly, **b)** honor, even for the despised, **c)** wealth, even in poverty, **d)** strength, even for the weak, **e)** attainment of goals, even for the humble, **f)** proximity (to God), even for the distant, **g)** goodness (generosity), even for the stingy, **h)** a sense of shame <note>meaning modesty or moral integrity</note>, even for the boaster, **i)** reverence, even for the lowly, and **k)** salvation (peace), even for the fool.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of knowledge from the previous page, which concluded with the seven qualities required for study. It moves from the benefits of knowledge to the ethical responsibilities and spiritual dangers facing the scholars who possess it, emphasizing humility over pride.</meta>

Compare Quran 39:9 <note>The text cites 39, 12, following an older numbering system</note>: Are those who know and those who do not know equal? Only those of understanding take heed of God.

35:28 <note>original: 35, 25</note>: Of His servants, only the scholars truly fear God.

2:269 <note>original: 2, 272</note>: To whomever wisdom is granted, much good has been given.

Despite these great advantages, scholars must guard themselves against destructive faults and poor character traits. These are arrogance, vanity, and pride.

Thus it is said of the Prophet <note>Muhammad</note>: "Whoever increases in knowledge, yet does not at the same time increase in humility toward God, only increases in distance from God." From this comes the frequent strife and quarreling, the lust for power, the obstinacy, the enmity, and the hatred found among them.

Luqman <note>A legendary wise figure in Islamic tradition</note> spoke to his son in exhortation: "O son, associate with the scholars and stay close to them, for God revives their hearts with the light of knowledge <note>original: *Wissenschaft*; here referring to sacred and ethical learning</note>, just as He awakens the dead earth with the downpour of rain from heaven. However, beware of the quarreling of scholars, for wisdom descends as if from heaven. But when people come to know it, they turn it to their own advantage."

Their great addiction to the world also contributes greatly to the ruin of scholars, as does their great striving for worldly things, their excessive preoccupation with doubtful matters, their indulgence in scruples, and their failure to live according to the dictates of wisdom.

As it is said in the parable: "Love for the world is the root of all danger; greed is a sickness of the soul and an ailment."

In the decisions of the fundamental law <note>Referring to *Sharia* or religious jurisprudence</note>, the scholars are the physicians of the soul. If one of them yields to the desires of the world, he is like a doctor who wants to heal another while he himself is hopelessly ill; how then should the patient recover through his treatment?

It is also said that the scholar who abstains from the world is the most knowledgeable in the religion of God and sees the path to the next world more clearly than a thousand greedy men. The Messiah <note>Jesus, known as *Isa* in the Islamic tradition</note> spoke in an exhortation to the scholars of the Children of Israel:

<meta>This page continues the warning to scholars initiated on the previous page, shifting from the general dangers of pride to the specific spiritual consequences for those who possess knowledge but use it only for worldly gain. It emphasizes that knowledge without piety becomes a burden rather than a blessing on the Day of Resurrection.</meta>

O you wise ones and scholars <note>original: *Schriftgelehrten*; refers here to those learned in religious law and scripture</note>, you sit at the path to the other world, yet you neither walk upon it yourselves to enter Paradise, nor do you let anyone else pass by you to reach it. The ignorant are more easily excused than the knowledgeable. Not one of you has an excuse.

Every form of knowledge and every education that does not drive one to strive for the other world, and does not lead there, is a harm to the one who possesses it; it will become evidence against him on the Day of Resurrection <note>In Islamic theology, one’s own knowledge can "testify" against them if they failed to act upon it</note>. Thus the rulers, the powerful, the Pharaohs, and the heroes of old possessed reason and education, discernment, wisdom, and wonderful arts—as did those who associated with them. But because they applied these powers to indulge in the desires of this world and did not care for the next world, these gifts of grace turned to their detriment. Since they did not seek the other world through them, they were in manifest loss. The Quran censures them for this reason and, through them, warns later nations.

> compare 57:20 <note>The source cites 57, 19, following an older numbering system</note>: Know that the life of this world is but play, amusement, finery, and boasting, a rivalry for more wealth and children. It is like a downpour of rain; the plants that grow because of it fill the unbelievers with wonder, but then they wither and you see them turn yellow, becoming straw. In the other world, there is a severe punishment.

> 3:14 <note>The source cites 3, 12</note>: Beautified <note>Presented as an allure or temptation</note> for mankind is the love of desires, such as women, children, heaped-up treasures of gold and silver, branded horses, cattle, and tilted land. These are the goods of this life. But God—with Him is the best return.

Many other passages censure the greedy to warn them not to neglect the other world, so that no excuse remains for mankind after the coming of God’s messenger.

In four things, the good shall be rewarded with good after the separation from the body:

b) the greed of Adam <note>A reference to the desire for the forbidden fruit in the Garden</note>;

These three are, as it were, the mothers of disobedience, the root of all evil, and from them—like similar sisters—branching limbs extend.

**Sisters of pride** and similar to it are self-admiration, turning away from the truth, failing to confess the truth, disobedience toward the commandments and prohibitions that one should obey, transgression, injustice and oppression, contempt for what is right, unfairness in action, meanness in speech, folly, quarrelsomeness, vehemence, deception, disdain, vanity, and the desire to belittle others, as well as other blameworthy qualities and bad character traits.

**Related to greed** are false desire, intense addiction, striving for what is unjust, the toil of the body and the exertion of the mind to collect, hoard, and increase. The delight in wealth and the fear of poverty, stinginess, avarice, narrow-mindedness in trade and conduct, mistrust, forbidden greed for gain, suspicion, lies and deceit, strife, enmity, secrecy and falsehood, perjury, fraud in legal disputes, and so forth.

**Related to envy** are hatred, secret jealousy, enmity, persecution, anger, transgression, hardheartedness, pitilessness, severity, slander, and infamy.

**Greatness of soul** <note>original: *Seelengrösse*; refers to the concept of magnanimity or high-mindedness</note> stems from **high aspiration**. This high aspiration was implanted into our **fundamental nature** so that one might strive for **leadership**, and this is sought for the sake of guidance. For in the changing course of their affairs, people require a **leader** <note>original: *Vorsteher*; meaning a governor, overseer, or guide</note> to lead them according to established conditions. If the leader lacks high aspiration and is without greatness of soul, he is unfit for leadership; conversely, for **subjects**, **allies**, **servants**, and **slaves**, greatness of soul is unsuitable.

In general, **greatness of soul** is not something praiseworthy at all times or in every circumstance; however, when it is applied as it should be, to the extent it should be, and for the reason it should be, it is called **purity**, **chastity**, **high-mindedness**, **abstinence**, **nobility**, **beauty**, and **adornment**, and the one who possesses it is praised.

On the other hand, **pride** <note>original: *Hochmuth*</note>—which is the refusal to accept the truth and disobedience toward the commands of the leader—is blameworthy and belongs to the category of evils and rebelliousness.

One must know with certainty how to guide **servants**, **wife**, and **child**; they must never become arrogant or refuse obedience. Likewise, the **leader** must forbid and command, yet in doing so, he must be **just**, **truthful**, **kind**, and **noble**.

From this it becomes clear what benefit and wisdom lie in the fact that **greatness of soul** is imprinted upon nature, and when the person possessed of it is to be blamed and when they are to be praised.

**Greed** <note>original: *Gier*</note> is the striving for what is desired. It is implanted in our **fundamental nature** because a human being requires materials to maintain his **body** and his **person** for a while, and to preserve his **form** through his **offspring** for a period. Therefore, the inclination to grasp these materials, to preserve them, and to store them up for a time of need lies in our nature and basic disposition, since these things are not found at all times and in all places. Now, if a human desires these materials as he requires them, and saves them until the time of need, and then puts into use only as much as is necessary, he is praiseworthy and just; but if he desires more...

For God's blessings toward His creatures are many; God alone knows their number. Now, it is not possible for Him to unite them all in a single person. In His justice, He distributed them among individuals as He willed, yet, as His wisdom demanded, He favored one over the other. None of His creatures is entirely devoid of those blessings, yet none possesses them all; rather, one has more, the other less.

If one person sees that another has a gift which he himself lacks, he should see whether he has not been granted a gift that the other lacks; he should then weigh this against the other's, thank God, and ask Him to let him keep it.

If, on the other hand, someone notices a gift in his brother that he does not possess himself and asks God to take it away from that person, then this is **envy**. The envious person punishes himself and hurts his own heart, as he is an enemy of **God’s grace**.

If one considers the things of the world with the help of **reason** and recognizes that desire for **worldly pleasure** and dominion, as well as the wish to remain in it eternally, then one sees that the cause of most human evils is the great desire for the world and the greed for it; however, the head of all good, and the root of all excellenc— <note>The word "excellencies" or "virtues" (Vortrefflichkeiten) is cut off at the end of the page.</note>

### **The Believers.**

All creatures are servants of God and are subject to His command, whether willingly or unwillingly; yet some stand further from Him, while others are closer, and between both states lie various levels and degrees.

a) The **God-dedicated** <note>original: *Gotteignen*; those who belong uniquely to God, often referring to the "Ahl Allah" in Islamic mysticism</note> are those whom the Lord chose and distinguished above all others. These are the rational and the understanding, to whom He addressed His word and speech, command and prohibition, threat and promise, praise and blame. In His wisdom, God then elevated the believers above all rational beings; they allow themselves to be guided by His command and prohibition according to the decrees of the **Fundamental Law** <note>original: *Grundgesetz*; likely referring to the Shari'a or the Quran</note> and the laws of reason. They refrain from what is forbidden to them, both in secret and in public.

b) Then, from the mass of believers, God raised a group and distinguished them above all the rest. These are the steadfast, the serving, the righteous, and the ascetic, who guard themselves (against Hell); they remember God day and night, do good, and zealously follow the statutes of the Fundamental Law step by step.

cf. Quran 39:9 <note>numbered in the source as 39:12</note>: Is he then, who serves God during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, who is mindful of the other world and hopes for the grace of God [the same as one who does not]? Say: are those who know and those who do not know equal? Only those of insight remember God—cf. 32:16.

c) Out of these, God raised a group by degrees and distinguished them from the others. These are the **ascetics** of this world, who know its true value, desire the next world, testify to its truth, and remain steadfast in the knowledge of it. They are those **near to God**, the **understanding**, and the **seeing**; God mentions them—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the categories of believers and their spiritual hierarchy.
