[Surah] 15, [Verse] 42, where He says to the Devil: "Over my servants you have no power," and the Devil replies: "except for those who follow you from among the erring."

**a) The believers possess many virtues through their good character traits and noble actions. These cannot all be united in a single person. But according to knowledge and faith, there is no quality or character trait nobler than asceticism <note>original: *Enthaltsamkeit*; refers to the practice of spiritual detachment or renunciation of worldly pleasures</note> in this world and longing for the next.**

Asceticism in this world consists of renouncing the superfluous pleasures <note>original: *Niessnutz*; literally "usufruct," referring to the enjoyment of life's benefits</note> of this life, such that one does not pursue its desires and contents oneself with the little that is necessary. Many other beautiful character traits, excellences, and good deeds follow from this quality. The opposite of asceticism is the desire for this world—the obsession with grasping it and its pleasures. The consequences of this quality are many bad characteristics, depraved traits, and wicked actions.

**b) The ascetic enjoy little and have no cravings, and herein lie many praiseworthy qualities, many advantages and virtues; as it is said of the Prophet <note>referring to the Prophet Muhammad</note>: "Stay hungry, then the inhabitants of heaven will rejoice in you."** By enjoying little, a person maintains a healthy body; he retains a good memory, remains insightful and clear-minded; he then sleeps little, possesses sound views and a light soul, is sharp-sighted and endowed with healthy senses; he then turns toward the sciences, and is quick of movement and of a healthy nature.

If asceticism is paired with contentment, it becomes a seed-field for thought, a source of wisdom, a torch for the heart, a barrier against the malice of souls, and a safeguard against the terrible Reckoning <note>original: *Abrechnung*; referring to the divine judgment in the afterlife</note>.

Regarding the disadvantages of over-satiety, Aisha <note>the wife of the Prophet Muhammad and a significant source of Islamic tradition and law</note> says: **"The chief calamity that afflicts the people is over-satiety and the abundance of food; for once the people have so filled their bodies, the bodies become fat, the hearts grow hard, and the desires gain power."** From this arises heartache and physical illness—

The spirits of the unbelievers, the wicked, and the hypocrites, however, do not ascend there, but remain beneath the heavens in the deep abyss of the interspace <term>Barzakh; the spiritual realm between death and the final resurrection</term> until the day they are called to account.

Thus are the wicked repaid; they have a resting place in Hell, and coverings lie over them; the high place (of heaven) is not suitable for them, just as the seats of kings and nobles are not fitting for filthy people.

Whoever wishes to reach that place must purify themselves of bad character traits and corrupt opinions; for more on this, compare Treatise XL, and on the improvement of character, see the Treatise on the Fellowship of the Pure Brethren <note>original: *Lautern Brüder*; referring to the *Ikhwan al-Safa*, a 10th-century secret society of Muslim philosophers known for their encyclopedic letters</note>, XLIV.

c) Those who trust in God can be recognized by many signs and attributes, through which they distinguish themselves from others and especially from the enemies of God; for the insightful person distinguishes similar-looking things by their specific peculiarities.

<meta>This page continues the description of the virtuous soul's journey. Having established on the previous page that the pure soul resides in Paradise or the celestial spheres after death, the text now describes the specific character traits and spiritual insights of these "servants of the Merciful" during their earthly life.</meta>

— 156 —

Many Quranic passages speak of them. God speaks to Iblis <note>The primary name for the devil in Islam, representing the fallen angel or jinn who refused to bow to Adam.</note>: "Over my servants you have no power."

Quran 25:63 <note>The source cites 25:64, but the description of the servants of the Most Merciful begins in verse 63 of standard editions.</note>: "The servants of the Most Merciful walk upon the earth humbly, and when the ignorant address them, they say, 'Peace,'" and so forth until the end of the chapter; further: "They call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His face." <note>Seeking the "Face of God" implies acting solely for God's pleasure and presence rather than for material reward.</note>

They guard themselves against every transgression of what is neither permitted in religious law <term>Sharia; the divinely ordained path and legal framework of Islam</term> nor allowed in the statutes, and especially against everything that is unpleasant to hear or unseemly to behold.

They protect their tongues from lies and concealment, from shameful speech, folly, and cursing, and from the slander of any Caliph <term>Khalifa; the title for the historical successors of the Prophet Muhammad as leaders of the community</term>, whether he be friend or foe, opponent or supporter.

Among their qualities is steadfast loyalty. The foundation of all good is that their breast is free from hatred, falseness, envy, and enmity, as well as from pride, greed, deceit, hypocrisy, and the like.

Their character traits include compassion and mercy, a tenderheartedness for every being endowed with a spirit, as well as gentleness, sincerity, love, kindness, and friendship for all companions.

The primary hallmark of those who trust in God, which distinguishes them from others, is the knowledge of the true nature of the angels and their inspiration. Likewise, they know the actual essence of the Satans, the armies of the devil, and their whispers.

->cf. Quran 7:201 <note>The source cites 7:200.</note>: "Indeed, those who guard themselves remember God when a company from the devil touches them; they then become clear-sighted."<-

They also grasp the doctrine of the visitation, the resurrection, the raising of the dead, and the gathering, as well as the scales, the path, and the transition in a refined and mysterious manner. For most experts in prophetic religious law are unclear regarding the devil and his true nature, particularly concerning his address to the Lord of the Worlds (7:14 <note>The source cites 7:13.</note>): "Give me respite until the day they are raised." *Likewise, most scholars doubt, and most are*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual purity by contrasting the "servants of the Merciful" with the nature of their adversary, Satan. While the previous page focused on the virtues of the pious—such as loyalty, compassion, and the avoidance of slander—this page moves into an allegorical interpretation of the Fall of Adam and the "Greater Jihad," or the internal struggle against one's own lower impulses.</meta>

— 157 —

Philosophers who deny the literal truth of the story of Adam, his enmity toward and address to the Lord of the world, and the Lord’s reply to him—subjects that occupy some fifty passages in the Quran; compare Quran 7:16: "I will surely sit in wait for them on Your straight path; I will come at them from their front and their back, from their right and their left." <note>original: "ich will sie überfallen von vorn und hinten, von der Rechten und Linken". This is the speech of the cast-out Devil, referring here to the beginning of the 7th Sura (chapter) of the Quran.</note> Satan also says (Quran 7:12): "I am better than Adam. You created me from fire and him from dust." And in Quran 20:120: "Shall I lead you to the Tree of Eternity and to a kingdom that does not decay?"

Such passages, many of which are also found in the Torah <term>Tora; the first five books of the Hebrew Bible</term> and the Gospel <term>Evangelium; the Christian New Testament</term>, are discussed in Treatise XLVII, *On the Resurrection*. Here, let us provide something regarding the struggle of those who trust in God against Satan—a struggle waged by day and night, in secret and in public. 

"One of those who trust in God spoke with his peers about the Satans, their enmity, and his understanding of them: 'As I grew up and the decrees of the Fundamental Law regarding what is commanded and what is forbidden became clear to me, I lived accordingly. Then I meditated upon the word of God: "Truly, Satan is a manifest enemy to man," and other similar passages. I also considered the saying of the Prophets: "We have returned from the lesser struggle to the greater struggle," <note>This refers to the "Greater Jihad" (Jihad al-Akbar), which is the internal spiritual struggle against one's own ego and desires, as opposed to external conflict.</note> that is, the struggle against one’s own soul. Or the saying: "Satan circulates through the children of Adam as blood flows through the veins," as well as many other passages from the Quran and Tradition <term>Hadith; the recorded sayings and actions of the Prophet Muhammad</term>. I found—and no one contradicted me—that such passages were addressed to all humanity, and thus to me as well.'"

Likewise, the true meaning of the Satans—the hosts of Iblis <note>The primary name for the devil in Islam</note>—their conflict and enmity against man, and their whispering, is a deep mystery. These forces are, in fact, implanted in our fundamental nature and expressed through character. Specifically, these are the bad character traits and blameworthy qualities that begin even in childhood, followed by corrupt views, evil deeds, and wicked...

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and psychological exploration of the human soul. While the previous page introduced the external enmity of Satan as described in the Quran and other scriptures, this page internalizes that struggle, interpreting the concepts of "Angel" and "Satan" as states of the soul and explaining the "Greater Jihad" as the internal mastery of one’s habits.</meta>

— 158 —

Habits that disturb equilibrium through either excess or deficiency relate to the desiring soul and the wrathful soul. <term>Desiring and wrathful soul; in medieval psychology, these represent the lower impulses of appetite and anger, which must be balanced by reason.</term>

Commandment and prohibition, promise and threat, and praise and blame, however, relate to the rational, discerning soul. Through its good qualities, true knowledge, and right action, this soul is an angel in relation to the desiring and wrathful soul.

Unto these latter souls belong dense folly, blameworthy traits, and wicked ways; in comparison to the rational soul, such a nature is a Satan.

The rational soul possesses good qualities and lives them out according to its zealously acquired good character traits, which are then planted into its fundamental nature through good habits. These are thus the root of all good, whereas all evil and corruption stem from the bad character traits that have been planted in a person’s fundamental nature since childhood.

Accordingly, the true meaning of those Quranic verses—"You return from the lesser struggle to the greater struggle" <note>original: "ihr kehrt vom kleineren Kampf zum grösseren zurück." This refers to the famous tradition of the Prophet Muhammad regarding *Jihad*, distinguishing between external conflict and internal spiritual discipline.</note> and "Truly, Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy" (Quran 35:6)—is to be explained as follows: fight against him. The Prophet says: The enemy is divided into two categories and enmity into two types; likewise, the struggle is divided into two parts. One is open—that is, the enmity of the infidels who fight against the Religious Law <term>Religionsgesetz; often referring to Sharia or the divinely ordained code of conduct</term>—and one is hidden—that is, the enmity of the Satans, who are the opponents of one’s fundamental disposition and the adversaries of nature.

Thus, it is clear that the struggle and enmity against Satan is rooted in the very essence of the human being, whereas the struggle against infidels is merely accidental. <term>Accidental; in a philosophical sense, this means a non-essential or temporary circumstance, rather than an inherent part of human nature.</term>

The struggle against infidels is undertaken for a worldly cause; if they triumph, the result is worldly misery, and the joy and vitality of this life pass away.

The struggle and victory of Satan, however, causes the misery—

<meta>This page continues a philosophical and theological discussion regarding the transition of the soul into the afterlife and the internal struggle between different faculties of the human spirit.</meta>

...in that world, and thereby the desire, loveliness, and joy of the same pass away.

Because of this difference between the two, the Prophet says: "We return from the lesser struggle <note>original: *Jihad*; referring to physical battle</note> to the greater struggle <note>referring to the internal spiritual struggle against the ego</note>," and for this reason, it is stated so often in the Quran: one should beware of the deceit and temptation of the Devil.

If someone wishes to turn a person away from such a teaching, let him not accept that command; for if he were led to it, he would become like the tempter in will and action, and the evil deed and habit within him would become his fundamental nature. The rational soul <term>Vernunftseele: the *nafs al-natiqa*, or the intellectual part of the soul that distinguishes humans from animals</term>, which is indeed a sublime substance, would be transformed into a devilish one; he then remains in the other world a devil condemned to punishment.

cf. Surah 4, verse 56 <note>The German text cites 4, 59</note>: "As often as their skin is roasted through, We shall exchange it for another skin, so that they may taste the punishment."

Surah 78, verse 23: "Abiding therein for ages, they taste in Hell neither coolness nor drink."

If, on the other hand, man accepts the commandment of his Lord, resists the appetitive soul <term>Begehrseele: the part of the soul driven by physical desires and lusts, known in Arabic as *nafs al-shahwaniyya*</term> and fights down the irascible soul <term>Zornseele: the part of the soul associated with anger and the drive for power, or *nafs al-ghadabiyya*</term>, and if he contends with these opponents for the sake of his rational soul, then he overcomes those two. He gains power over them, and all dispositions and character traits become servants to the rational soul; through good deeds, noble character, and true knowledge, the soul becomes an angel, while those other two become vanquished servants.

Thus it is determined in the original religious law <term>Religionsgesetz: likely referring to the *Sharia* or divine law</term> and the conclusions of reason; cf. Surah 6, verse 153 <note>The German text cites 6, 154</note>: "Behold, this is My path, a straight one, so follow it."

If one considers the conditions and the changes of human affairs, one finds that the structure of his body consists of blended mixtures and opposing forces, and that the various desires are grounded within him. These resemble the fires hidden within flint <term>Schwefelstein: literally "sulfur-stone," used here as a metaphor for the potential for fire hidden within minerals</term>, or a lamp whose fuel is the craving for the loveliness and comforts of this world; its fire is never extinguished, and its flame is never stilled. Furthermore, these opposites are like waves crashing against one another, or—

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual "Greater Jihad," explaining how the rational soul must overcome the appetitive and irascible souls to achieve an angelic state rather than a devilish one. It uses Quranic citations to warn of the punishments for those who succumb to worldly desires.</summary>

<keywords>Greater Jihad, rational soul, appetitive soul, irascible soul, spiritual struggle, Quranic ethics, afterlife, asceticism, Islamic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual struggle against the lower self, focusing on how various vices and passions transform the state of the soul into something destructive or animalistic.</meta>

...comparable to violent storms or to the hosts of armies unleashed for an invasion.

When one hungers, gluttony is like a flaming fire, and the **appetitive soul** <term>Begehrseele: the part of the soul driven by base desires and physical needs, known in Arabic as *nafs al-shahwaniyya*</term> is then like dogs falling upon their prey. Greed is, as it were, a conflagration that would destroy the entire world. The soul is like a storehouse filled with all manner of goods; when anger flares up in the **animal soul** <term>Thierseele: the lower, instinctive part of the soul shared with animals, often called *nafs al-hayawaniyya*</term>, it is as if a spark were thrown into it, causing everything to burst into flames.

When pride flares up, the soul resembles a tyrant claiming sovereignty; in pride and the thirst for glory, it acts as if it were the supreme **Caliph** <note>the chief civil and religious ruler, regarded as the successor of the Prophet Muhammad</note>; in the lust for power, it is as if all people were its **slaves**. In the search for honor, it seems as if honor is owed to it, and it behaves as if it were usurping for itself the obedience from many servants that actually belongs to God alone. — When the soul is slow to do what it owes to others, it is as if it were doing something beyond what is commanded, or as if it were carrying a heavy burden. — In games and pleasure, it is as if it were possessed. If one praises and exalts men, it is as if they were the most rational and magnificent of people.

In envy, the soul resembles an enemy who wishes to devastate the world. The same applies to all bad character traits, blameworthy dispositions, and shameful actions; they are all a fire that is never extinguished; a struggle that never ends; a battle that never rests; a sickness that never heals. Therefore, let one arm oneself with a firm resolution and put on the belt of steadfast purpose. Upon the head, one should place the crown of **abstinence** <note>self-restraint or asceticism</note> and lean upon the **fear of God** <note>piety or *taqwa*</note>; one must bind the powers of the soul through prohibition, open one's sight through the guidance of **science** <note>original: *Wissenschaft*; in this context, it refers to religious or spiritual knowledge</note>, take faith in God as a guide, and walk the straight path to the Creator according to the law. Thus, the believer cries out with the cry of a drowning man; he confesses his weakness and prostrates himself before God, for there is no strength and no power except in God <note>original: *La hawla wa la quwwata illa billah*; a common Arabic declaration of total dependence on God</note>; he is devoted to Him like a small boy to his father; then God hears the call and strengthens him, so that **he remains free from the temptation of the world.**

<summary>This page describes the destructive nature of human vices—such as gluttony, pride, and envy—comparing them to fires or wild animals, and advocates for spiritual discipline and total reliance on God to overcome them.</summary>
<keywords>appetitive soul, animal soul, gluttony, pride, Caliph, spiritual discipline, fear of God, asceticism, temptation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's struggle, moving from the description of internal vices to a direct dialogue between a devotee and God regarding the purpose of temptation and the nature of divine commands.</meta>

A devotee who trusts in God <term>Gottvertrauter: one who practices *tawakkul*, or total reliance on God</term> called out to God during a time of temptation: “Lord, You created me, yet You gave me no counsel or command [to protect me]; You made Satan my master, for You placed desire within me and set the adorned world <note>original: *aufgeputzte Welt*; likely referring to the Quranic concept of the world's deceptive beauty or *zayyana*</note> before me. Then, however, You threatened: 'Do not follow this love, do not let yourself be seduced by Satan, and never forget that other world; otherwise, you will be in manifest loss' <note>original: *offenbaren Verlust*; a reference to the Quranic phrase *khusran mubin*</note>. Thus, I found myself caught between opposing things, pulled to and fro by conflicting forces and warring states of being; I did not know what to do. Then I cried out to God: 'Take me by the hand and lead me onto the path of my salvation!' But He inspired me: 'My servant, I did not command you anything for the sake of helping Me thereby, nor did I forbid anything because your doing it could harm Me. Rather, I commanded such things so that you might recognize that you have a Lord who created you, formed you, nourished you, and gave you your beginning. You were meant to recognize that in all commands and prohibitions, you require My help and My assistance, and that you might recognize My providence, which is indispensable to you in this world and the next. You must never turn away from Me, but must always remember Me. You have need of Me always; I see you while you do not see Me. You must leave everything behind you and turn toward Me; then you will draw near to Me and become My confidant, a dweller of Paradise. Then you will believe nothing false about Me and will assume only the truth. So, remember My former grace toward you. Did I not create you well when you were nothing perceivable? I gave you fine hearing, a sharp gaze, good senses, a wise heart, a penetrating intellect, eloquent speech, sound reason, a perfect physical frame, a beautiful form, and strong limbs. I bestowed speech upon you and taught you what is harmful and what is beneficial, and the way of worldly affairs. I unveiled your sight and opened your eye so that you might look upon My kingdom, recognize the course of day and night, perceive the rotating spheres and the wandering stars <note>planets</note>, and calculate the seasons, months, years, and days. I subjected the land and the sea, the minerals, plants, and animals to you, and gave you dominion over them.'”

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the characteristics of the righteous and the spiritual elite who follow the prophets. It moves from general instructions for the soul to specific examples of devotion, centering on the figure of Abraham as a model of absolute trust in God.</meta>

Such passages prove that the sincere servants of God well understand the nature of the Return <note>original: *Rückkehr*; referring to the soul's return to its divine origin after death</note> and the true meaning of the other world. They also know the secrets of prophecy, just as one grasps such things through philosophical practice. Out of longing for the other world, they abstain from this one; this longing is constantly stirred by parables, beautiful descriptions in admonitions, and sayings of wisdom. In their gatherings, they remember God and serve Him alone in all their deeds; they desire only Him and hope in Him alone, pray only to Him and fear Him alone. They are the truly God-fearing, who see the Lord correctly with right faith and a clear gaze, hearing only from Him and expecting only Him. They hold to the Creator instead of the creature, to the Lord instead of the ruled, to the Shaper instead of the shaped, to the Cause instead of the caused. Place and time are the same to them, and worldly changes do not affect them; they leave doubts behind and grasp what is certain, and they are willing to trade this world for the next. — How often God mentions them in the Quran! Furthermore, this saying is handed down from the Prophet: "This community shall never lack forty pious ones who stand in the place of Abraham, the Intimate Friend of God." <note>This refers to the *Abdal*, a group of hidden saints in Islamic mysticism who are said to sustain the world through their piety.</note>

A question was asked: "Tell us about Abraham's standing with his Lord." The Prophet replied: Abraham was a <term>Hanif</term> <note>A term for a "true believer" or "monotheist" who turned away from idolatry before the revelation of the Quran</note> (one turning away from falsehood), a <term>Muslim</term> <note>meaning "one who submits to God"</note> of sound heart. — For when his people thought to throw him into the fire, the angels wept out of compassion for him. Then God revealed to Gabriel: "Attend to him and help him if he asks for help." So Gabriel came to Abraham while he was in the catapult <note>original: *Wurfmaschine*; referring to the engine used by the tyrant Nimrod to fling Abraham into a massive bonfire</note> to be thrown into the fire, and he said: "Abraham, do you have any wish?" But because Abraham’s heart was so firmly attached to his Lord, and he trusted Him and believed so surely in God's promise that He would deliver him—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spiritual elite, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of hidden saints known as the "substitutes" and their role as the true successors to the prophets.</meta>

...[that he] should become, so that he needed no other, Abraham answered: "I have no need of you." <note>Abraham is traditionally said to have rejected the help of the angel Gabriel while being thrown into the fire by Nimrod, trusting only in God.</note> Thereupon God spoke: "O fire, be coolness and peace for Abraham." <note>A reference to Quran 21:69.</note>

It is said of those forty that four are substitutes <term>badîl</term> <note>The plural is *abdal*; these are a specific rank of saints in Sufi cosmology who are said to replace one another to ensure the world is never without spiritual guidance.</note>; they are called this because they represent one creation after another and perform one purification after another.

These forty men are drawn from four hundred ascetic <term>zâhid</term> <note>From the Arabic *zuhd*, meaning world-renunciation or asceticism.</note>, pious, and faithful servants, and these four hundred from four thousand pure believers.

Whenever one of the Four is elevated, one of the forty attains his place and rank; as soon as one of the forty is elevated, one of the four hundred takes his place, and for that one, one of the four thousand enters. His place is then taken by one of the pure believers.

They are very few in number, yet very great in value before God. They are connected to the world with their bodies, yet their spirits hang upon the Highest Vision — (words addressed to Kamil ibn Ziyad). <note>Kamil ibn Ziyad was a companion of Ali ibn Abi Talib; this famous description of the spiritual elite is a well-known tradition attributed to Ali.</note> Moses said in his secret conversation with God: "O Lord, I find in the Torah the description of men who, through the power of their discernment, knowledge, and piety, are almost prophets. Who are they? Make them my people." Then God revealed to him: "O Moses, this is the people of Ahmed <note>another name for Muhammad</note>." This refers to Quran 35:32: <note>The OCR text cites 35:29, but the quote corresponds to verse 32 in modern numbering.</note> "Then We gave the Book as an inheritance to those We chose of Our servants; and among them is he who wrongs himself (does not accept it), and among them is he who takes a middle course; and among them is he who is foremost in good deeds by God’s leave."

Those mentioned are the heirs of the prophets and their representatives on earth. They inherited from them knowledge, faith, the consciousness of a servant, the acceptance of divine strengthening, revelation, and the asceticism required to abandon this world and long for the next. They resemble the angels in their actions, character, and conduct, for they renounce bodily desires and turn away from pleasures. They chose lack instead of abundance, toil instead of rest, and placed these burdens upon their souls to please God and to imitate the prophets and messengers of God. They are

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of the spiritual elite. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing men found in the Torah who, through their piety, were "almost prophets." This page picks up by defining the soul's potential to reach an angelic state.</meta>

...without doubt angels in potential <note>The German "dem Vermögen nach" refers to the Aristotelian concept of *potentia*, or the capacity to become something.</note>, but when they separate from the body, they are angels in actuality <note>The German "in der That" refers to *actus*, or the realization of that potential.</note>. The union of the soul with the body has the goal that the soul becomes insightful and an angel in actuality, after it was previously one only in potential.

If this were not so, God’s commandment would not exist—that the soul must become like the angels in action, character, and conduct—nor would it be promised that the soul should meet and speak with the angels; compare <note>Quran</note> 41:30: "The angels descend upon them and say: 'Fear not, nor be grieved, but rather rejoice in the Paradise which you were promised; we are your allies in this life and the next.'"

God calls such pious ones the "possessors of heart, restraint, and vision"; they are those who stand near to God and are His friends. Regarding them, He says to the Devil <note>Quran</note> 15:42: "Truly, over my servants you have no power."

In the testament <term>wasiyya</term> <note>A formal counsel or parting exhortation common in Islamic literature.</note> of the Prophet to Abu Hurayrah <note>A prominent companion of the Prophet Muhammad, known for his vast memory and narration of traditions.</note>, the Prophet says:

> O Abu Hurayrah, observe the conduct of those people who, when others falter, do not fear, and when others seek safety from the fire <note>The fires of Hell.</note>, do not tremble.

Abu Hurayrah: "Who are these, O Messenger of God? Describe them to me so that I may recognize them."

Prophet: "They are people from my community <term>ummah</term>; at the end of time, they will awaken at the Resurrection like the prophets. When the [other] creatures look upon them, they will judge them to be prophets based on their state, until I make them known by their signs. I will call out to my community, and the people will then know that those men are not prophets. They pass by like lightning and the wind; their light covers the assembled crowd."

Abu Hurayrah: "O Messenger of God, let me act as they do; perhaps I may join them."

Prophet: "O Abu Hurayrah, those people travel a steep path; they endure hunger after God has satiated them, nakedness after God has clothed them, and thirst after God has quenched them." <note>This describes voluntary asceticism—choosing a life of simplicity and deprivation for spiritual growth, despite having the means for comfort.</note>

...at they [might follow them].

They do such things in hope of what is with God. They abstain even from what is permitted out of fear of the Reckoning <term>hisāb</term> <note>The final judgment where souls are held accountable for their deeds.</note>; although they are in the world with their bodies, nothing of it clings to their hearts. The prophets and angels admire their obedience to the Lord and wish that God would unite them with them.

Thereupon the Prophet wept in longing to see them and said: "If God intends to punish a people of the earth and looks upon those [pious ones], He turns the punishment away. Well then, Abu Hurayrah, walk upon their path; whoever opposes it falls into the power of the Reckoning."

---

Another tradition <term>hadith</term>. The Prophet said: "Greetings to my brothers."

He was asked: "Do we not belong among your brothers?"

"No, you are my companions <term>sahaba</term>, but those are my brothers."

"Who then are they?"

"A people who, at the end of time, believe in me although they do not see me, who hold me to be truthful and follow me. Greetings to them."

Furthermore: The Prophet said to Usama ibn Zayd <note>A beloved young companion and the son of the Prophet’s adopted son, Zayd ibn Harithah.</note>: "Well then, onto the path of Paradise; woe to you if you are drawn away from it."

"How can one traverse it most quickly?"

"Through the longing of love (for God) and restraint from worldly desires; O Usama, devote yourself to fasting. Nothing is dearer to God than the breath from a fasting mouth. Death must find us with a hungry stomach and a thirsty liver <note>In the physiological understanding of the time, the liver was often associated with thirst and vital endurance.</note>; then you shall ascend to the highest place in Paradise and dwell with the prophets. The angels will then rejoice when you arrive, and the powerful ones <note>Likely referring to the archangels or high-ranking celestial beings.</note> will pray for you; God delights in such as these."

Thereupon the Prophet wept; his lamentation became so strong that the people feared to speak to him, for they believed a new revelation was coming to him from heaven. And he said: "Woe to this community <term>ummah</term>; one finds no one among them who [fears?] God..." <note>The German text "ge-" suggests the beginning of "gehorcht" (obeys) or "fürchtet" (fears), but the sentence remains unfinished at the page break.</note>

There is no god but God, and Muhammad is the Messenger of God."

Content yourself with only so much of the world as a small piece of land to stand upon, a piece of cloth to cover your nakedness, and enough shade that you may rest within it.

Let no reproach find you in the sight of God.

Suppress your anger and do good to those who have done you evil.

Beware of the love of the world, for that is the chief sin; it destroys its companion, but its companion never destroys it.

Be faithful toward others as toward yourself; do not reproach them for what is also found in yourself.

### **Concerning the beautiful striving in faith.**

The following is reported in the tradition:
God said to Moses, the son of Amram <note>original: "Imran", the father of Moses in Islamic tradition</note>, as the latter called upon Him twelve thousand times and addressed just as many sayings to Him; at the end of every saying, He said: "O Moses, draw near to Me and recognize My omnipotence, for I am God, O Moses. — Do you know why, among all My creatures, I speak with you alone and entrusted you with My message among the Children of Israel?"
Moses: "Grant me knowledge of myself."
"I know," said God, "the secrets of My servants, and I find no heart that is purer for My love than yours."
Moses: "Why did You create me, O Lord, since I was nothing?"
"I wanted to do good for you," replied the Lord.
Moses: "Lord, be merciful to me."
"I will," said God, "let you inhabit My Paradise and bring you into My proximity, leading you with My angels to the place of My grace, so that you may be there eternally: well, joyful, and serene."
Moses: "Lord, what shall I do?"
"Your tongue," said God, "must always be fresh in remembrance of Me <note>This reflects a common Islamic expression, "a moist tongue in the remembrance of God," meaning constant prayer</note>, your heart in My fear, and your body in My service; do not consider yourself safe from error against Me, even if you set your foot into Paradise."
Moses: "Why do You test me?"
"Because I," said the Lord, "have prepared you for Myself, that I may speak through your tongue to the Children of Israel, let them hear My speech, teach them My law, the Torah, and the practice of religion, and lead them on the path to the hereafter—you and whoever follows you, whoever it may be. Speak to the Children of Israel and let them hear My speech; speak thus to them:
*Since I created heaven and earth, I also created for both...*"

He did not keep My covenant but was contrary to Me; so I sent him out of Paradise, but since he repented, I promised him that I would bring him back there. I wrote it in My soul: none of his offspring shall enter Paradise unless he has accepted My legacy and fulfilled My covenant. But the unjust do not reach My covenant, and the arrogant do not enter My Paradise, for I established it for those who seek neither high status on earth nor seek corruption; may the good end be theirs, those who guard themselves <note>This likely translates the concept of "Taqwa" or God-consciousness</note>!" ---

Know then, that the craving for this world and the striving for the next never meet. He who desires this world renounces that one, and he who strives for that world renounces this one. The Messiah spoke in one of his exhortations to Israel: This world and the next are like East and West; the more you approach the West, the further you distance yourself from the East.

In a book of the Israelites <note>A likely reference to a midrashic or apocryphal collection of divine sayings</note> this passage is found: We made you eager for the other world, yet you had no desire for it; we taught you abstinence from this world, yet you did not embrace it; we made you fearful of the Fire, yet you did not fear; we stirred longing for Paradise, yet you did not long for it. We had compassion for you, yet you did not weep over the most shameful death, for God is a sword that never sleeps, a fire of Gehenna <note>term: Gehenna (from Hebrew Ge-Hinnom): originally a valley in Jerusalem, it became a name for the place of spiritual punishment or purification in the afterlife</note>.

God spoke to Adam: My bounty descends to you,

yet an evil deed rises up to Me; We bestow goodness upon you, but you prove yourself hostile through stubbornness against Me. Daily a noble angel carries your wickedness to Me; do you not consider that you are beneath My eyes? Be mindful of Me when you are alone and forbidden desires come to you; ask Me to lift the stubbornness against Me from your heart and your limbs, so that obedience toward Me may succeed for you and I may hear your prayer. I have given you commandment and prohibition so that you might seek help from Me and hold fast to My **rope of salvation** <note>original: "Rettungseil"; likely a reference to the "Rope of God" mentioned in the Quran (3:103), symbolizing divine covenant and protection</note>, not so that you could do without Me or turn away from Me. I, indeed, can do without you, but you are in need of Me. I have created you in this world and subjected it to you so that you might prepare yourself to meet Me and equip yourself (for the journey) from within it, not so that you would turn away from Me and surrender yourself to the earth.

The next world is better than this one; choose nothing except what I have chosen for you. Do not avoid meeting Me, for whoever does not desire to meet Me, his meeting I also abhor; yet whoever loves it, I also meet him gladly.

"There follows a detailed exhortation to take the given precepts to heart and to choose the path of those who trust in God. Let those be the <term>Imams</term> <note>from Arabic: "Imam", meaning leaders or examples to be followed</note> (leaders) of right guidance, the torches in the darkness. The most beautiful gift of man is said to be reason, and its best quality is knowledge. Every thing has its own special quality, but that of reason is the power of discernment and the knowledge of the true essence, of a righteous life, and of the right choice. Accordingly, one should act. — The next world is better than this one, just as its inhabitants are more excellent and their rank more sublime; thus, let your choice be complete. — One should reflect upon these matters in order to awaken from the sleep of heedlessness <note>original: "Sorglosigkeit", often used to translate the spiritual concept of 'ghafala', or being unaware of divine reality</note>."

# Index of Arabic Expressions

---

->Syn. = synonym. Opp. = opposite. Def. = definition. Div. = classification/division.<-

**Treatise** <note>original: "Abhandlung"; Arabic: رسالة *risāla*, pl. رسائل *rasā'il*</note>

**Section** <note>original: "Abschnitt"; in the context of Euclid's Elements: مقالة *maqāla*</note>

**Inflection** or **Variation** <note>original: "Abwandlung"; Arabic: تصريف *taṣrīf*, often referring to grammatical morphology</note>

**Branching** <note>original: "Abzweigung"; Arabic: فرع *farʿ*, pl. فروع *furūʿ*</note> Opp. **Root** <note>Arabic: اصل *aṣl*, pl. اصول *uṣūl*</note> ——

**Accident** <note>original: "Accidens"; Greek: συμβεβηκός *symbebēkos*; Arabic: عرض *ʿaraḍ*</note> <term>Accident</term> <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature (e.g., the color of a rose).</note> Def. **A completing form** <note>Arabic: صورة متممة *ṣūra mutammima*</note> Opp. **Substance** <note>Arabic: جوهر *jawhar*</note> — — = **constitutive form** <note>Arabic: صورة مقوّمة *ṣūra muqawwima*</note> Div. **corporeal** <note>Arabic: جسمانى *jismānī*</note> **spiritual** <note>Arabic: روحانى *rūḥānī*</note> cf. essential, special, accidental description, p. 24. **accidental** <note>Arabic: عرضى *ʿaraḍī*</note> cf. Form.

**Apprehension** or **Premonition** <note>original: "Ahnung"; Arabic: وهم *wahm*</note> cf. **Inspiration** <note>Arabic: الهام *ilhām*, وحى *waḥy*</note>

**Existing alone for itself** <note>original: "allein für sich bestehend"; Arabic: مفرد *mufrad*</note>

**Universal Soul** <note>original: "Allseele"; Arabic: النفس الكليّة *al-nafs al-kulliyya*</note> Syn. **World-soul** <note>Arabic: نفس العالم *nafs al-ʿālam*</note> cf. Soul.

**Universal Reason** <note>original: "Allvernunft"; Arabic: العقل الكلى *al-ʿaql al-kullī*</note> cf. Nature. 

**Alphabet** <note>Arabic: هجاء *hijā’*</note> cf. Letter.

**Analogy** <note>original: "Analogie"; Arabic: قياس *qiyās*</note> <term>Syllogism/Analogy</term> <note>In Islamic logic, "Qiyas" refers to both logical syllogisms and reasoning by analogy.</note> cf. Norm. Def. **The syllogism is the composition of premises** <note>Arabic: القياس هو تاليف المقدّمات</note>. **Application of the analogy:** **The syllogism is the extraction of the results of the premises** <note>Arabic: القياس هو استخراج نتائج المقدّمات</note>. **Artfully well-constructed analogy:** <note>Arabic: قياسة مصنوعة مركّبة</note>. **Crooked, too large, or too small analogy:** <note>Arabic: قياس معوج زائد ناقص</note>. **Logically correct analogy:** <note>Arabic: قياسة منطقية</note>. **Conjectural analogy:** <note>Arabic: قياس للحزر</note>. **Knowledge and instruction from analogy:** <note>Arabic: علم وتعلّم قياسى</note>. **Basic rules of analogy:** <note>Arabic: قوانين القياس</note> —
**In the demonstrative syllogism, one must first establish whether things are and what they are** —
**In a proof, a thing should not be the cause of itself** — **The effect should not come before the cause** — **Necessary accidents should be used in the proof** —
**The cause must be essential to the thing** — **The premise must be universal** —
**The existence of the predicate in the subject must be a primary existence (72)**

**Analytica, I, II** <note>Aristotle's works on logic, the "Prior" and "Posterior Analytics"</note> <note>Arabic: انولوطيقا الاولى الثانية *Anūlūṭīqā*</note> see Science.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical index of philosophical and logical terminology, moving from concepts of the soul and creation into the physical structure of beings, the nature of meaning, and the formal rules of logic and instruction. It heavily references Aristotelian categories and Neoplatonic pedagogy.</meta>

**Structure of the Body** <term>temple/structure</term> <note>original: هيكل (*haykal*)</note> See **Body** <term>body</term> <note>original: جسم (*jism*), جسد (*jasad*)</note> artfully well-joined
->The structure for artful wisdom<-

**Meaning** <term>meaning, meanings</term> — Meanings are entirely images and sketches in the thoughts of individual souls, which they have acquired from matter by way of the senses. <note>This reflects the epistemological view that the mind abstracts "meanings" or "forms" from physical objects perceived through the senses.</note>

**Need** <term>need</term>

**Attributing** <term>the qualifier</term> **attributed** <term>the qualified</term> cf. **Property** <note>original: نعت (*na't*)</note>

**Desire** <term>desire/appetite</term> cf. **Impulses**, **implanted** <term>desires rooted in the innate nature</term> <note>original: شهوات مركوزة فى الجبلّة (*shahawat markuza fi al-jibilla*)</note>

**Newly begun** <term>originated/innovated</term> <note>original: مبدع (*mubdi'*)</note> See **newly arising** <term>contingent/created in time</term>; **Being** <term>existent</term>

**Limitation** <term>delimitation</term> cf. **Definition**.

**Affirmation** <term>affirmation</term> <note>original: ايجاب (*ījāb*)</note> *kataphasis* <note>Greek: κατάφασيس</note>. Opp. **Negation** <term>negation/denial</term> <note>original: سلب (*salb*)</note> *apophasis* <note>Greek: ἀπόφασις</note>. Definition: Affirmation is the confirmation of an attribute for an object of description. ❦ Negation and affirmation are two contradictory judgments in both word and meaning together; they cannot coincide in truth or falsehood regarding a single attribute of a single object at a single time, from a single perspective, in a single relationship. If even one of these conditions is missing, it is possible for them to coincide in both truth and falsehood. <note>This is a classic summary of the Law of Non-Contradiction from Aristotelian logic.</note>

**Known** <term>the known</term> Opp. **unknown** <term>the unknown</term>

**Instruct** <term>instruction/teaching</term> see **be instructed** <term>learning</term> Definition: Instruction is the bringing forth of what is in potentiality—meaning possibility—into actuality—meaning existence; learning is the transition from potentiality to actuality. ❦ Instruction is an awakening provided by the soul that is knowing in actuality to the soul that is knowing in potentiality. Learning is the soul's picturing of the image of the known thing. ❦ Learning is nothing other than the path from potentiality to actuality, and instruction is nothing other than pointing out the path. Methods of instruction: 1. **Demonstration**; 2. **The method of definitions**; 3. **Division**; 4. **Analysis**.

**Name** cf. **Name**. **Eloquence** <term>eloquence</term>

**Well-prepared** see <term>to be prepared/ready</term>

**Nature** <note>literally: "condition"</note> properly **Reality** <term>reality/truth</term> <note>original: حقيقة (*haqiqa*)</note> = *ousia* <note>Greek: οὐσία, meaning "essence" or "substance"</note> **Property**.

**Description**, also **Property** <term>attribute, description</term> a. **different, essential** <term>differentiations; differentia</term> *diaphora* <note>Greek: διαφορά, the specific difference that distinguishes a species within a genus</note>; b. **special** <term>property/peculiarity</term> *idion* <note>Greek: ἴδιον</note>; c. **accidental** <term>accident</term> *symbebekos* <note>Greek: συμβεβηκός, a non-essential quality</note>. Definition: The differentia is that which, if it is removed, the object described by it is invalidated. ❦ The property consists of attributes which, if they cease, the existence of the object does not cease.

**Letters** <term>letters</term> <note>original: الحروف (*al-huruf*)</note>, which are individual sounds. [Definition D]; **Letters of the alphabet** <note>original: الحروف المعجمة (*al-huruf al-mu'ajama*)</note>, letter, letters.

**Written letter** <gloss>original German: *Schriftbuchstabe*</gloss> — **Spoken letter** <gloss>original German: *Wortbuchstabe*</gloss> — **Conceptual letter** <gloss>original German: *Denkbuchstabe*</gloss> --

***

**Character** <term>character/ethics</term> <note>original: خلق / اخلاق (*khuluq / akhlaq*)</note>. **Psychology** <gloss>original German: *Psychologie*</gloss> = the study of character.
The characters; the diversity of characters. <gloss>compare: disposition, nature. Diversity of character</gloss>
— From the perspective of the **humors** <term>the four bodily fluids</term> of their bodies and the temperament of those humors. ۝ (page 104)
— From the perspective of the soil of their lands and the differences in their climates. ۝ From the perspective of their upbringing according to the religious traditions of their fathers and the schools of thought of their teachers and masters. ۝ From the perspective of the dictates of the **astrological rulings** <note>original: احكام النجوم (*ahkam al-nujum*)</note> at the roots of their births. — **Earthly character** <gloss>original German: *Erdchar.*</gloss> — **Airy character** <gloss>original German: *Luftch.*</gloss> — **Watery character** <gloss>original German: *Wasserch.*</gloss> — **Fiery character** <gloss>original German: *Feuerch.*</gloss>
For each of these characters, there are similar "sisters" and there are opposites—those that differ from them. All of them have distinct and opposing actions. (page 108)

**Characters rooted in the natural disposition** <gloss>original German: Character founded in the basic disposition</gloss> <note>original: الجبلّة (*al-jibilla*)</note>
**Characters acquired through current habits** <gloss>original German: Character acquired through habit</gloss>

[Definition D]: The **character rooted in the natural disposition** <term>innate nature</term> is a certain readiness in an organ of the body, by which it becomes easy for the soul to manifest a specific action, a work, a craft, the learning of a science, or a branch of literature, or management, or politics, without the need for thought or deliberation.

[Definition E]: All characters are of two types —
Either they are imprinted in the natural disposition of souls and rooted within them, or they are acquired and made habitual through the flow of custom and frequent practice. Among characters, some are roots and principles, and some <insert>are branches based upon them (116). — </insert> The characters of the **children of this world** <term>worldly people</term> <note>original: ابناء الدنيا (*abna' al-dunya*)</note> and their natures are those which <insert>nature has rooted in their disposition without any acquisition, choice, thought,</insert> deliberation, effort, or burden on their part. 

However, the characters of the **children of the hereafter** <term>spiritually-minded people</term> <note>original: ابناء الآخرة (*abna' al-akhira*)</note> are those they have acquired through their effort and striving, either by the dictates of reason, thought, and deliberation, or by following the commands and discipline of the **Law** <term>Divine Law or religious code</term> <note>original: الناموس (*al-namus*)</note>. This becomes a habit for them through long persistence and frequent use; for this, they are rewarded and compensated. (p. 135, **praiseworthy** <gloss>original German: *lobensw.*</gloss>)

The **praiseworthy** <note>original: المحمود (*al-mahmud*)</note> among acquired characters are those according to the dictates of reason and its judgments, and those according to the rulings and commands of the Law. Likewise, the judgment for **blameworthy** <gloss>original German: *tadelnsw.*</gloss> characters is the opposite. (page 147) — Praiseworthy acquired characters are attributed to the **angels**, while those that are blameworthy are attributed to the **Satan**. <gloss>Character traits</gloss>

The difference between the **Essential Definition** <term>hadd</term> and the **Descriptive Definition** <term>rasm</term> is that the Essential Definition is derived from the things of which the defined object is composed, while the Descriptive Definition is derived from the attributes specific to the thing being described. Another difference is that the Essential Definition informs you of the essence <term>substance</term> of the thing defined and distinguishes it from all else, while the Descriptive Definition distinguishes the described thing from others based on [its properties]. [7] — Through Essential Definitions, the realities of species are known: to which genus each belongs and by how many differentia <term>specific differences</term> it is distinguished from others. [61] — As for the method of defining, its purpose is the knowledge of the reality of the species. [63]

<summary>This page defines the moral origins of vice—attributing them to the sins of Satan, Adam, and Cain—before transitioning into a dense philosophical glossary. It explores the logical distinctions between essence (what a thing is) and identity (that a thing is), and the technical differences between essential and descriptive definitions.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, vice, Satan, Adam and Eve, Cain, logic, essence vs. existence, quiddity, haecceity, definition vs. description, Aristotelian philosophy, Active Intellect, prime matter and form</keywords>

<columns>2</columns>

**Particulars** (see **Individuum**) <note>original German: *Einzelding*; Arabic: الأمور الجزوية (*al-umur al-juz'iyya*)</note>. **Composite Particulars** <note>original German: *zusammen-gesetzt*; Arabic: الاشياء المركبة (*al-ashya' al-murakkaba*)</note>: **physical, natural, artificial.**

**The Simple things** <note>original Arabic: الاشياء البسيطة (*al-ashya' al-basita*)</note> or **Primary things** <note>original German: *Urdinge*</note>: **psychic** <note>spiritual</note> and **corporeal.**

**The secondary things mirror their primaries.** <note>original Arabic: الثوانى من الامور تحكى اوائلها (*al-thawani min al-umur tuhki awa'ilaha*)</note>

**Art of Disputation** <note>original German: *Disputirkunst*; Arabic: الجدل (*al-jadal*, dialectics)</note>. **Threat** <note>original German: *Drohung*; Arabic: وعيد (*wa'id*)</note> — **Opposite: Promise** <note>original German: *Verheissung*; Arabic: وعد (*wa'd*)</note>.

**Balanced** <note>original Arabic: معتدل (*mu'tadil*)</note>; **Proportion** <note>original German: *Gleichmaass*; Arabic: اعتدال (*i'tidal*)</note>; **Symmetry** <note>original German: *Ebenmaass*; Arabic: استواء (*istiwa'*)</note>.

**Property** (see **Description**) <note>original German: *Eigenschaft*; Arabic: الصفة (*al-sifa*)</note>. **Specific Property** <note>original Arabic: صفة مختصة (*sifa mukhtassa*)</note>. **Additional Property** <note>accidental attribute; Arabic: صفة زائدة (*sifa za'ida*)</note>. **Opposing Properties** <note>Arabic: صفات متضادة (*sifat mutadadda*)</note>.
**Definition: Opposing properties are accidents residing in a substance, but not as an integral part of it.** <note>original Arabic: اعراض حالة فى الجوهر لا كالجزو منها (*a'rad halat fi al-jawhar la ka-l-juz' minha*)</note>

**True Nature of the existing thing** <note>original German: *eigentliche Beschaffenheit*; Arabic: حقيقة الشىء الموجود (*haqiqat al-shay' al-mawjud*)</note>.

**The One** <note>original German: *Einer*; Arabic: عدد مفرد (*'adad mufrad*, a single number)</note>. (see **Conceive** <note>original German: *einbilden*; Arabic: تصوّر (*tasawwur*)</note>).

**Composite** <note>original German: *zusammengesetzt*; Arabic: مركّب (*murakkab*)</note> — **Opposite: Simple** <note>original German: *einfach*; Arabic: بسيط (*basit*)</note>.

**Isagoge** <note>original Arabic: ایساغوجى (*isaghuja*)</note> — **Introduction**. <note>original Greek: εἰσαγωγή (*eisagōgē*)</note> **This is the introduction to the craft of philosophical logic.** <note>original Arabic: هو مدخل الى صناعة المنطق الفلسفىّ (*huwa madkhal ila sina'at al-mantiq al-falsafi*)</note>

**Division** <note>original German: *Eintheilung*; Arabic: قسمة (*qisma*)</note>; **correct rational divisions.** <note>original Arabic: اقسام عقلية صحيحة (*aqsam 'aqliyya sahiha*)</note>

**Influence** <note>original German: *Einwirkung*; Arabic: تأثيرة (*ta'thira*)</note>. **Particulars** (see **Individuum**).

**Element** <note>original Arabic: اركان ركن (*rukn/arkan*, pillars)</note> — **Fire, Air, Water, Earth**. **Elements of Judgment** <note>Arabic: عناصر للحكم (*'anasir lil-hukm*)</note>: **possible, necessary, impossible.**

**Emanation** = **Outpouring** <note>original German: *Erguss*; Arabic: فيض (*fayd*)</note>. **To emanate** <note>original Arabic: افاضة (*ifada*)</note>.

**Angel** <note>original German: *Engel*; Arabic: ملك (*malak*)</note>. **Definition: Angels are forms abstracted from matter that employ bodies.** <note>original Arabic: الملائكة الصور المجرّدة عن الهيولى المستعملة للاجسام (*al-mala'ika al-suwar al-mujarrada 'an al-hayula al-musta'mala lil-ajsam*)</note>
**The Angels brought near** <note>archangels; Arabic: الملائكة المقربون (*al-mala'ika al-muqarrabun*)</note> **are those that govern them and from whom actions proceed.**

**Opposite** <note>original German: *entgegengesetzt*; Arabic: متضادّ (*mutadadd*)</note> — **Opposite: Corresponding** <note>original Arabic: مطابق (*mutabiq*)</note>.

**Abstinence** <note>original German: *Enthaltsamkeit*; Arabic: زهد (*zuhd*)</note> **from the world** — **Opposite: Desire/Greed**. **The opposite of asceticism is the desire for the world and the eagerness in seeking it.** <note>original Arabic: ضد الزهد هو الرغبة فى الدنيا والحرص فى طلبها (*didd al-zuhd huwa al-raghba fi al-dunya wa-l-hirs fi talabiha*)</note>

**Decisions of the Basic Law** <note>original German: *Entscheid d. Grundgesetzes*; Arabic: احكام الناموس (*ahkam al-namus*)</note>
a. **Clear rulings of the Law** <note>exoteric; Arabic: ظاهر احكام الناموس (*zahir ahkam al-namus*)</note>
b. **Secret rulings of the Law** <note>esoteric; Arabic: اسرار احكام الناموس (*asrar ahkam al-namus*)</note>

**Generation and Corruption** <note>original German: *Entstehen u. Vergehen*; Arabic: الكون والفساد (*al-kawn wa-l-fasad*)</note> <term>Coming-to-be and passing-away</term> <note>original Greek: γένεσις (*genesis*); φθόρα (*phthora*)</note>.

**To grasp or perceive** <note>original German: *erfassen*; Arabic: ادراك (*idrak*)</note>.

**Knowledge** <note>original German: *Erkenntniss*; Arabic: معرفة (*ma'rifa*)</note> <note>original Greek: ἐπίγνωσις (*epignōsis*)</note> (see **Science/Wisdom**). **Knowledge of the Truth.**

# Knowledge of the realities of things consists of four types, namely Division **Essence of Things** <note>original German: *Wes. d. Dinge*; Arabic: معرفة حقائق الاشياء اربع انواع وهي التقسيم (*ma'rifat haqa'iq al-ashya' arba' anwa' wa-hiya al-taqsim*)</note>
## Analysis, Definition, and Demonstration <note>original Arabic: والتحليل والتحديد والبرهان (*wa-l-tahlil wa-l-tahdid wa-l-burhan*)</note>

**Acquired** <note>original German: *erworben*; Arabic: مكتسب (*muktasab*)</note>; **Innate** <note>original German: *G. eingepflanzt* (planted within); Arabic: مركوز (*markuz*)</note>.
**Existence** <note>original German: *Existenz*</note> = **Being** <note>original German: *Vorhandensein*; Arabic: وجود (*wujud*)</note>; **to exist** <note>original Greek: *ὑπάρχειν* (huparchein)</note>.

---

**To fan** <note>original German: *fächeln*; Arabic: ترويح (*tarwih*)</note>. **Color** <note>original German: *Farbe*; Arabic: صبغ (*sibgh*), لون (*lawn*)</note>. **Pigmentation** <note>original German: *Färbung*; Arabic: اصباغ (*asbagh*)</note>.
**False** <note>original German: *falsch*</note>; **Error** <note>original German: *Fehler*; Arabic: خطأ (*khata'*)</note>. **Synonym: Slip** <note>original Arabic: زلّة (*zalla*)</note>. **Error/Delusion** <note>original German: *Irrthum*</note>. **Enmity** <note>original German: *Feindschaft*; Arabic: عداوة (*adawa*)</note>.
**To manufacture** <note>original German: *fertigen*; Arabic: صنع (*sana'a*)</note>; **Manufactured** <note>original German: *gefertigt*; Arabic: مصنوع (*masnu'*)</note>.
**To establish** <note>original German: *feststellen*; Arabic: اثبات (*ithbat*)</note>. **Figure** <note>original German: *Figur*; Arabic: شكل (*shakl*)</note>; **shape** <note>original Greek: *σχῆμα* (schema)</note>.
**Surface** <note>original German: *Fläche*; Arabic: سطح (*sath*)</note>. **Definition:** Surfaces are of three types. **Explanation:** A surface is that which has length and width.
-> flat, convex, concave <- <note>original Arabic: بسيط (*basit*), مقبّب (*muqabbab*), مقعّر (*muqa'ar*)</note>

**Fluid** <note>original German: *flüssig*; Arabic: سيّال (*sayyal*)</note>.

**Form** <note>original German: *Form*; Arabic: صورة (*sura*)</note>; **form/species** <note>original Greek: *εἶδος* (eidos)</note>. **Definition:** Form is that through which a thing is what it is—Forms are what a person perceives in their thoughts as information after observing them in **Primal Matter** <note>original Arabic: الهيولى (*al-hayyula*, from the Greek *hyle*)</note>.
**Constitutive forms are** **producing and perfecting** <note>original German: *E. herstellend, vollendend*</note>—they are existing entities perceived through the senses. **Completive forms are** their attributes, descriptions, and states.
**Completive forms** = **Accidents** <note>original Arabic: اعراض (*a'rad*, non-essential properties)</note>—they follow upon the object and the object is described by them.
**33. Matter, Form**: **Material form** <note>original Arabic: صورة موضوعة (*sura mawdu'a*)</note>. **Archetype** <note>original German: *Urform*</note>. **Constitutive forms = Substance** <note>original Arabic: جوهر (*jawhar*)</note> of posterior things—for things prior in existence = **Matter** <note>Hyle</note>. **Form free from Matter, see Angel.** **Form of the Structure of Creatures**: the forms of the frames of animals.

**Question** <note>original German: *Frage*; Arabic: سؤال (*su'al*), مسألة (*mas'ala*)</note>. **The 9 Questions:** Whether it is <note>existence</note>; what it is <note>essence</note>; how much it is <note>quantity</note>; what kind it is <note>quality</note>;
**5.** Which thing it is; where it is <note>place</note>; when it is <note>time</note>; why it is <note>reason</note>; who it is.

**The Pious** <note>original German: *d. Fromme*; Arabic: صالح (*salih*)</note>. **See Substitute** <note>original Arabic: بديل (*badil*, a rank in Sufi hierarchy)</note>. **Ascetic** <note>original German: *Enthaltsam*; Arabic: زاهد (*zahid*)</note>. **Believer** <note>original German: *Gläubig*; Arabic: مؤمن (*mu'min*)</note>, sincere. **Characteristics of the Pious:** Knowledge, faith, worship, and the acceptance of divine support.
**165.** Longing for the hereafter, asceticism, and inspiration.

**Genus** <note>original German: *Gattung*; Latin: *genus*; Greek: *γένος*</note>. **Species** <note>original German: *Art*; Latin: *species*; Greek: *εἶδος*</note>. **Individual** <note>original German: *Individ.*</note>.
**Definition: The Individual** <note>original Arabic: شخص (*shakhs*)</note>; **indivisible/atom** <note>original Greek: *ἄτομος* (atomos), *τίς* (tis, "a certain one")</note>. An individual is every term used to refer to an existing thing that is distinct from other existing things and is perceived by one of the senses. **The Species** is all...

<meta>catchword: The Species is all</meta>
<vocab>Wesen der Dinge (Essence of things), Existenz (Existence), Fläche (Surface), Form (Form), Urform (Archetype), Materie (Matter), Hyle (Primal Matter), Gattung (Genus), Art (Species), Individuum (Individual), Fragen (Questions), Glauben (Faith)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines core philosophical concepts including existence, surface types, and the distinction between constitutive and completive forms. It also lists the nine Aristotelian modes of inquiry (the "Questions") and defines the logical hierarchy of Genus, Species, and Individual.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian logic, Islamic philosophy, ontology, Hyle, substance and accident, categories of inquiry, Sufi terminology, genus and species</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical philosophical glossary from page 181. Having concluded the letter 'F' (Figure), it moves into the letter 'G,' focusing heavily on 'Gattung' (Genus), 'Geist' (Spirit), and 'Glaube' (Faith). The author continues to provide cross-linguistic definitions, bridging Aristotelian logic with Islamic theology and Neoplatonic concepts.</meta>

A term by which a multiplicity shared by one form is indicated. ۞ **Genus** is any term by which a multiplicity of different forms is indicated, all shared by another higher form. ۞
<note>The first definition describes a "Species" (*naw'*), while the second describes a "Genus" (*jins*). In logic, a genus is a broader category containing various species.</note>
Compare also descriptions of the different, specific, and accidental.
**Genus of genera** = **Category** <note>original Arabic: *jins al-ajnas*</note>
**Commandment**; **Usage**; see **Fundamental Law**.
**Hour of birth** <note>original Arabic: *mawalid*, *maulid*</note>. **Thought** <note>original Arabic: *fikr*, *afkar*</note>.
See **Opposite** <note>original Arabic: *mutadadd*</note>; in the context of stars <note>astronomy/astrology</note>: **Opposition** <note>original Arabic: *mutaqabil*</note>.
**Vessel** <note>original Arabic: *wi’a’*</note>.
**Opposition, Opposite** <note>original Arabic: *didd*, *addad*</note>. The **Great Opposites** <note>original Arabic: *al-addad al-kubra*</note>; the **Small Opposites** <note>original Arabic: *al-sughra*</note>.
**Obedience** <note>original Arabic: *ta’a*</note>. <note>original Arabic quote: "It has been said that obedience is the greatest name of God, by which the heavens and the earth were established."</note> <note>reference to 149</note>
**Spirit** <note>original Arabic: *ruh*</note>. **Soul** <note>original Arabic: *nafs*</note>. **Body** <note>original Arabic: *jasad*, *jism*</note>. **Spiritual Beings** <note>original Arabic: *al-ruhaniyya*, *al-ruhaniyyat*</note> = **Spiritual Forms** <note>original Arabic: *al-suwar al-mujarrada ‘an al-hayula* (forms abstracted from matter)</note> that act upon the bodies they direct, and from which their actions proceed. <note>Note: These are identified as **Angels**.</note>
Where to arrive at/attain: **Attainment** <note>original Arabic: *bulugh*</note>.
**Learned/Scholar** <note>original Arabic: *‘alim*, *‘ulama*</note>. The bad character traits of scholars: pride, vanity, and boasting <note>original Arabic: *akhlaq raddiya al-‘ulama: al-kibr, al-‘ujb, wa-l-iftikhar*</note>.
**Commonality**; see **Participation** <note>original Arabic: *ishtirak*</note>. **Tool** <note>original Arabic: *adat*</note>; see **Instrument**.
**Smell** <note>original Arabic: *shamm*, *ra’iha*</note>. **Taste** <note>original Arabic: *dhawq*</note>. **Sight/Vision** <note>original Arabic: *basar*</note>. See **Sense**.
**Creature** <note>The author defines *al-hayawan*, literally 'animal' or 'living thing'</note>: a sensing, self-moving body.
**Law**: a. **Fundamental Law** <note>original Arabic: *namus*; original Greek: *nomos*</note>. b. **Religious Law** <note>original Arabic: *shari’a*</note>.
**Set/Subject** = **Raw Material/Subject Matter** <note>original Arabic: *mawdu’*</note>. **Produced/Manufactured** <note>original Arabic: *masnu’*</note>. **Set/Predicated** and **Subject** <note>original Arabic: *mahmul* and *mawdu’*</note>.
**Craft/Trade** <note>original Arabic: *sina’a*</note> = **Art**.
**Habit/Custom** <note>original Arabic: *‘ada*</note>. **Current/Prevailing Custom** <note>original Arabic: *‘ada jariya*</note>.
**Proportion, Symmetry, Equilibrium** <note>original Arabic: *i’tidal*, *istiwa’*, *sawiyya*</note>.
**Faith/Belief** <note>original Arabic: *i’tiqad*</note> — Belief follows character, and character follows belief. **Religious Faith** <note>original Arabic: *al-iman*</note>. The **Believer** <note>original Arabic: *mu’min*</note>. See **Servant**.
**Happiness/Fortune** <note>original Arabic: *al-sa’ada*</note>. a. **Worldly Happiness** <note>original Arabic: *al-dunyawiyya*</note>: the longest possible persistence of every existing soul.

<summary>This page provides a multilingual philosophical glossary for terms starting with the letter 'G,' covering logic (Genus, Category), metaphysics (Spirit, Soul, Form), and ethics (Character, Faith, Happiness). The principles of reason only arise in the souls of rational beings through the stabilization of sensed things, one after another, examining them part by part, and contemplating them individual by individual. When they find many individuals encompassed by a single attribute, it arises in their souls by this consideration that everything belonging to the genus of those individuals or the genus of that part shares this ruling—even if they have not witnessed every individual of that genus or every person of that species. 
The seven known things in the **Principles of Reason** <note>reference to the 7 axioms of Euclid</note>.

**To have** <note>an Aristotelian category; Greek: *hexein*</note>. Possession <note>original Arabic: *al-malaka*</note> is the combination of one substance with another substance; it is divided into two types: either internal or external.
**To act** <note>an Aristotelian category; Greek: *poiein*</note>. Acting <note>original Arabic: *yaf‘alu*</note> is of two types: that where the trace of the actor remains in the thing produced, and that where no trace remains for the actor.

D.** <note>The 'D.' likely refers to a Definition or a specific source</note> **The individual is simply a reference to every whole gathered from various things, composed of several distinct parts, and distinguished from other existing things.** ۞ **61. — Individuals are of two types: a collection of similar parts, and a collection of different parts for substances with varying accidents.** <note>Accidents (German: *Accidenz*; Arabic: *'arad*) are properties that can change without changing the essence of the thing, such as color or weight</note> ۞ **61. — Since the senses only perceive composite individuals—made of simple substances in distinct locations and particular accidents in specific places—one only knows from this that they are distinct existing entities. As for their quantities, qualities, and the reasons for their being (whyness), these cannot be known with precision except through the use of constructed, composite syllogisms.** ۞ **64.**

— All existing things other than the Creator are either **Substance** or **Accident**. ۞
— **Substance** is that which subsists by itself and is capable of receiving contraries. ۞
— **Accident** is that which exists in a thing, not as a part of it, and perishes without that thing perishing. ۞
— Substance includes that which is simple, such as **Prime Matter** <note>original Arabic: *al-hayula*; Greek: *hyle*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical philosophical glossary from the previous page, transitioning into the letter "M" in German. ...and necessity. Some of it is compound, such as the body. ۞ Every **substance** <note>Arabic: *jawhar*</note> is either an **active cause** <note>Arabic: *‘illa fa‘ila*</note> or a **passive effect** <note>Arabic: *ma‘lul munfa‘il*</note>. ۞ Every active cause is more noble than the passive effect. ۞ There is no station between negation and affirmation, nor is there a rank between non-existence and existence. ۞ The **accident** <note>Arabic: *‘arad*; a property that is not essential to a substance</note> has no action of its own. ۞

Balance, excess, and deficiency <note>original German: *Gleichm.* [Gleichmass], *Ueberm.* [Uebermass], *Unterm.* [Untermass]</note>.

**Matter** <note>original German: *Materie*; Greek: *hyle*; Arabic: *al-hayula*</note>
Matter is a simple substance capable of receiving form, having no quality in it at all. ۞ Matter = the things that precede existence. ۞ The **readiness** <note>original German: *Bereitsein*; Arabic: *tahayyu’*</note> of matter to accept form and shapes. The **binding** <note>original German: *Bindung*</note>. ۞ The restriction of forms in matter and their stability within it. Geometric triangles are sensible things perceived by sight, even if their meanings are heard and grasped by the mind.

— Blood, phlegm, and the two biles <note>yellow bile and black bile</note>. — The nine temperaments. — The mixtures of the four elements <note>fire, air, water, and earth</note>.

**Man** <note>original German: *Mensch*; Arabic: *insan*</note>
Man is a collective whole composed of two distinct substances: a corporeal body and a spiritual soul. ۞ Man is a rational, mortal animal. ۞ The **Absolute Universal Man** <note>original German: *d. allg. abs. M.* [der allgemeine absolute Mensch]; the archetypal human</note> is naturally disposed to accept all human character traits, all human sciences, and the wise crafts. He exists at all times and in every age, and with every individual human person. All people are individuals belonging to this Absolute Man. ۞ [Page] 111. All people are children of the Hereafter and its people, just as they are children of this world and its people. However, they are divided therein into two groups, just as they are in this world: the fortunate and the wretched. ۞

*The development of Man:*
— A sperm-drop from humble water <note>Arabic: *nutfa min ma’ mahin*; a Qur'anic reference to semen</note> in a secure lodging <note>the womb</note>. — A thick clot in a firm place. — A fully formed embryo. — A moving, sensitive infant. — A clever, understanding boy. — A vigorous, strong, and active youth. — A mature man, experienced, learned, and knowing. — A wise, philosophical, and godly elder <note>Arabic: *shaykh hakim filaysof rabbani*</note>. — A celestial, spiritual angel, eternal in existence.

**Feature** <note>original German: *Merkmal*; Arabic: *rasm*</note>. See: **Definition**.
**Mineral** <note>original German: *Mineral*; Arabic: *ma‘dan*</note>. — Mineral bodies. See: **Science**.

**Letter D. — Particle** <note>Arabic: *harf*; German: *Partikel*</note> — Signs indicating meanings that serve as tools for the speaker to connect nouns with verbs.

**Philosophy. D.** <note>German: *Philosophie*</note> To become like God according to human capacity.

**Plant. E.** <note>German: *Pflanze*</note> Plants are of three types: those that arise from planting, those from seeding, and those that arise of themselves <note>i.e., spontaneous generation or wild growth</note>.

**Porphyry the Tyrian** <note>original: *Porphyrius d. Tyrer*; a 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note>

**Practical Craft** <note>Arabic: *al-san'a al-'amaliyya*; German: *Praxis* — cf. Art (*Kunst*)</note> is the artisan’s extraction of the form that is in his mind and placing it into **prime matter** <term>Hyle: the fundamental, formless "stuff" of the universe</term>.

**The Produced. D.** <note>German: *Producirt*</note> is the composite whole made of both matter and form together.
Produced things are of four kinds: human, natural, psychic <note>pertaining to the soul</note>, and divine. ۞ **E.**
Human productions are like what craftsmen make in the way of shapes, engravings, and dyes within natural bodies. ۞ Natural productions are the forms of the structures of living things, the various shapes of plants, and the colors of mineral substances. ۞ Psychic productions <note>pertaining to the Universal Soul</note> are like the ordering of the four elements <note>Arabic: *al-arkan al-arba'a*</note>, the composition of the celestial spheres, and the systematic structure of the world's form as a whole. ۞ Divine productions are the forms and matters of the "innovated" things <note>Arabic: *al-mubda'at*; things created *ex nihilo*</note> brought from non-existence into existence all at once, without time or place. ۞

**Logic, Speech** <note>Arabic: *mantiq*, *nutq*; German: *vernünftige Rede*; Greek: *logos*</note> — **Intellectual** <note>German: *Denkr.*</note>, **Verbal** <note>German: *Wortr.*</note>, and also **Linguistic**.
**Intellectual logic** is the origin, and **verbal logic** is its branch. **Useful speech** <note>German: *sinngebend*</note>. **Speech in general**.

When words are arranged, they become discourses <note>or "statements"</note>. .29 — **Word** <note>German: *Redetheil*; part of speech</note>.
**Spiritual Kingdom**. <note>German: *Reich, geistl.*</note>
**Purity** <note>German: *Reinheit*</note>. The ordering of the four elements: **Sequence**. <note>German: *Reihung*</note>
**The Related / Relation**. <note>German: *Relation*</note> Two types: the reciprocal and the non-reciprocal. The reciprocal is where both attributes are equal in name <note>e.g., "brother of a brother"</note>. ۞ The non-reciprocal is where the two related things differ in name <note>e.g., "father of a son"</note>. ۞ The characteristic of this category is that the two related things revolve around one another and are not mutually exclusive; they exist together in the state of relation. ۞ 40.

**Religion, Faith**. <note>German: *Religion*</note> **Divine Law**. <note>German: *Religionsgesetz*; Arabic: *shari'a*</note>

**Juice / Essence**. <note>German: *Saft*; likely referring to humors or botanical extracts</note>
